THE SAVINGS BANK.

Annual Meeting Discloses Satisfactory State Of Affairs. NEW CHAIRMAN ELECTED.

The annual general meeting of the Warring- ton, Runcorn and District Savings Bank was held at the offices of the Bank, Butterma rket- street, Warrington, on Tuesday morning. .In the absence of Mr. C. Jackson Holmes, who recently 'met with an accident, Mr. Josiah Longland was voted to the chair. The Actuary (Mr. W. A. Proud) read letters of apology for non-attendance from Mr. C. Jackson Holmes, Mr. J. Fulton-Smith, Mr. Inger C. Parr, Mr. J. E. Richardson, Mr. Lewis R. Rowson and the Rev. the Hon. C. F. Cross.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0007.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ANNUAL REPORT.

• The annual report of the Bank Management Committee stated that in view of the abnormal times they considered the progress made during the past year eminently satisfactory. When the War Loan .was issued in June last, facilities were offered by the bank to enable depositors to pur- chase the loan, and it is gratifying to know that no less a sum than £35,000 was taken up by them. • During the year premises have been pur- chased for the business at Runcorn. The neces- sary alterations are nearing completion, and it is hoped to have the new Bank opened early in the New Year. The following extracts from the accounts and year's work show the progress the institution has made :—During the year there has been an increase of 220 in the number of accounts. making a grand total of 19,639. The amount received from depositors is • £123,762 ss. id. representing 27,280 transactions; £153,305 11s. 10d. has been paid to depositors in 12,104 trans- actions. The total number of transactions is 39,384, with a turnover of £277,067 16s. 11d. Funds of the Bank :—Assets: Ordinary depart- ment, £372,441 2s. 11d.; war and other Govern- ment stocks, £54,689 Bs. 3d.; investment depart- ment, £139,469 7s. 10d.; total, £566,599 19s. Liabilities, £557,420 10s. Id.; leaving a cash sur- plus of £9,179 Bs. 11d. • The surplus assts over liabilities, inclusive of the value of the Bank premises, &c.,-,amount to £11.177 85.,11d.,„ an in- crease of £566 10s. Bd. for the year.

Summary cif deposits and payments :—De- posits: Ordinary department, £104,915 16s. 2d.; investment department, £18,846 Bs. , 11d.; pur- chases of stock, £35,168 6s. Bd.; interest added, -ordinary department, £8,960 7s. Id.; investment department, £3,990 16s. Id.;' total, £171,881 14s. 11d. Payments: Ordinary department, £136,235 13s. 9d.; inve3tment 'department, £17,069 18s. Id.; sales and transfers of stock, £2,795; total, £156,100 us. 10d; increase for the year, £15,781 3s. id. • Branches.—The business transacted at the branches again shows that good progress has been made at Bank Quay, where £925 us. 10d. has been received for head office accounts. There are 594 open accounts with £7,674 due to depositors; whilst at Earlestown, 377 depositors have the large sum of £13,366 to their credit. The special investment department has been successful, showing an increase of £5,767 for the year. The total amount now due to depositors is £136,798 11s. 4d. Day -school banks now number 38, and have standing to their credit £2,567 3s. 7d. The suc- cess of these banks is due to the ability: and efficiency with which they are conducted by teachers in the schools, and the committee take this opportunity to renew their thanks to those ladies and gentlemen: The members of the staff who responded to their country's call, ' viz. :—Messrs. Hallwood, Gillies and Darbyshire—are now on active ser- vice.

The committee have to record with regret the loss the institution has sustained by the deaths of Mr. Wm. Long, chairman of the institution for the last 19 years, a trustee; of the Rev. J. Carson and Mr. Wm. Handley, managers. Audit and inspection.—The bank's books and accounts have been regularly and continuously audited during the year by Mr. A. Bennett, who has paid nunierous visits to the Bank for the purpose of comparing depositors' pass books with their respective ledger accounts. Mr. H. B. Davies and Mr. C. G. Hall, of the Trustee Savings Bank Inspection Committee have visited the Bank during the year and verified all returns made to the Inspection Cora raittee and National Debt Commissioners. The report was taken as read and adopted.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0008.txt | included=true | included =====
THE AUDITOR'S REPORT.

The Actuary read the report of the Auditor (Mr. Arthur Bennett), which stated :—" I have attended at the bank month by month dur- ing the year, and have checked the additions

_ of the recepits and payments, proved the accuracy of the ledger postings, examined the vouchers, compared all communications from the National Debt Commissioners which affect the' accounts with the entries in the general ledger, and verified the Valances in the hands

of the treasurer and the actuary. I have fre- quently attended for the purpose of comparing depositors' pass books with the respective ledger accounts, and have tested 2,300 during the year, and have found the same to agree. The ecuri- ties for the sums advanced to various local authorities by the special investment depart- ment have also been produced for my inspection, and appear to be in order. Every facility has been afforded me by the staff, and I am glad to add that the books are well and carefully kept."

===== 0002601_19151225_art0009.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PAST YEAR.

Giving his review of the past year's working, The Chairman said that one could not help regretting the very unfortunate circumstances that caused him to occupy that position. He was sure that all present would agree with him when he extended his sympathies to the Vice- Chairman, Mr. Jackson Holmes. (Hear, hear.) The bank was to be congratulated" upon the position it had been able to maintain during the past year. About £277,000 had been received -which showed what a large amount of business was done. The total number of transactions was 39,384, and these and the other figures in the report showed the great amount of work the staff had to do. As the meeting had been in- formed, the staff had been depleted by the absence of three of the members, who had joined the Forces, so that a larger burden of work had naturally fallen upon the shoulders of those who had been left behind. He was sure he was expressing the views of his fellow- members of the committee when he said that they were indebted to the staff for lie manwir in which their work had been carried cut dur- ingthe year, (Hear, hear.)

Mr. Proud had had tO obtain temporary

assistance, and the work had, to all intents and purposes, gone on satisfactorily. (Hear, hear.) The amount due to depositors showed an in- crease of £15,781, and there had been invested a sum of £35,000 in the War Loan, so that it was evident the people of Warrington, Runcorn and district had managed to save. Whether it was due to the lessons and lectures on the need of thrift that had been given during the year he did not know, but it was a fact that money had been saved, and the bank did its best to encour- age thrift. (Applause.) As all present knew, the Warrington Savings Bank had amalgamated with•the Runcorn Bank, and the amalgamation had been a very great success. The business there had increased over £5,000 this year in reference to deposits. (Hear, hear.) The bank had been altered and reno- vated, and they had a most convenient and up- to-date building for carrying on their business. It would soon be necessary to increase the exist- ling facilities for business at Runcorn. There was no doubt that increased facilities did lead to an increase in business. That had been shown, not only in Runcorn, but in the other branches. The school banks had done well. They were a most valuable adjunct to this bank, inasmuch as they inculcated lessons of thrift upon the children of the town, and in that con- nection a debt of gratitude was owing to the teachers at the various schools for their work. (Hear, hear.) The question has been raised as to bringing this bank into line as far as pos- sible with the work done for the Treasury. There had been a good number of suggestions in the papers and elsewhere as to how the deposits in the Post Office Savings Banks should be in- creased. A committee representing the Trustee Savings Banks was sitting in London and was endeavouring to promote a scheme in the direc- tion that he had indicated. The committee wa.:3, in constant touch with the Treasury, and it was hoped that some useful 'scheme would be evolved.

"Since the last annual meeting," continued Mr. Longland, "we have lost the Chairman 0 the bank (Mr. Wm. Long). He had been chair- man for a great number of years, but had done a tremendous amount of work for the bank. Since his death the Committee of Management have sent a letter of condolence and sympathy to the-family. I am sure that we shall be very fortunate if we secure another chairman who will serve the bank as well asildr. Long did." (Hear, hear.) Mr...longland then moved That ;;e state- ment of accounts prepared by the aciotry, and examined by the auditor, be approved, and that copies be transmitted to the National Debt Commissioners and the Committee of Inspec- tion, and that a duplicate statement be publicly affixed in the bank."

Seconding, Councillor H. Roberts said that probably the fact that a great deal of money was being distributed in the district just now in connection with matters of a destructive rather than of a constructive character had something to do with the fact that the deposits had in- creased during the year, in spite of the terrible war which was now going on. While the source from which the money came was to be re- gretted on some grounds, the fact that thrift was increasing was a very satisfactory feature of the position, and when they were told that to win the war—as they would -win it, as they ought and wanted to win it—men, munitions and money were necessary, it was up to them to dg their part to encourage to the fullest possible extent the thriftiness of the people in order that when the time came for - them to ,do their share they would be . able to do it. (Hear, hear.) The moti9n was carried.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHILDREN'S SAVINGS.

Mr. J. W. Hayward moved: "That the report of the Committee of Management be approved, and that this meeting acknowledges the valuable services ,rendered to the institution by the gentlemen acting on the committee during the past year, and tenders its best thanks to them." It was, he said, a gratification to them to know that they were doing something to bene- fit the citizens of Warrington, Runcorn and the district. It was also pleasant to find that Run- corn was having better and handsomer premises in which to carry on the good work which was being done there. A pleasing feature of , the report was the statement concerning the im- provement of facilities for saving amongst chil- dren.

Mr. Clegg seconded the resolution, which was carried.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NEW CHAIRMAN AND VICE- CHAIRMAN.

Mr. J. 11. Smethurst moved that Mr. C. Jack- son Holmes should be the "chairman for the ensuing year, and that Mr. Josiah Longland should be the vice-chairman. He said that he regretted that Mr. Holmes could not be present at the meeting owing to the accident that he had sustained. In him they would find a worthy succes- sor to Mr. Long. His father was chairman of the bank years ago, and now the son was following in his footsteps and would do the bank credit. Mr. Longland was one of those retiring gentle- men that you had to dig under before you knew. They knew him to be a man of sterling value, and they could confidently recommend him to the managers.

Mr. F. A. Frost seconded, and the motion was carried unanimously.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
APPOINTMENTS.

Mr. Wm. Skinner was reappointed treasurer and Mr. Arthur Bennett auditor. The following " managers were elected to join the • trustees on the Com- mittee of Management for the ensuing year, viz.: The Revs. W. Bracecanip and John Yonge, and Messrs. J. H. Smethurst, J. G. Craik, J. E. Richardson and Ed. Wallington. Messrs. J. S. Hill and Vere B. Davies were appointed trustees. The following names were added to the list of managers: The Mayor of Warrington (ex-officio), and Mr. E. Rose and Wilfrid J. Rowland, Mr. F. A. Frost stating that it would be in accord- ance with the democratic character of the bank to have the Mayor of the Borough for the time being as a manager. A vote of thanks was heartily accorded Mr. Longland for presiding.

BIRMINGHAM ECONOMY.

Birmingham Education Committee on Wed- nesday decided as a measure of war economy to close three technical schools and five evening schools. It was ,stated that this step had been taken as the result of a demand by the City Council for economy. Closing at eight centres would effect a saving of £BOO during the present financial year, and in the estimates for the next financial year provision bad been made for saving £3,000 by closing a number of smaller technical schools, institutes and evening classes.

Little Sis,—the English mail—but over here they call it the American mail—has been sent on to me at Oldways Castle. They have invittAl me to keep Christmas with my fiancée. Your letter and the governor's re- mind me a little of a catechism, and very much of your good selves. As usual, the governor gets to his point briefly. "Are the goods value for money?" ha asks. That's all. I have replied in the space below, in our business style. "In- trinsically, yes. Value to me remains to be

proved." You, as usual, little, loved Sis—l bad to be away to learn what a loved little Sis you are—hide up the sharp points with much soft velvet, but I have found them, and I'll try to answer what you wish me to answer. First, what is _Lady Muriel. like? Her age is two-and-twenty. She is tall and very, very beautiful; fair and com- posed, and stately; blue eyes and fair hair, regular features, and rather pale. The word that describes her best is "still." I think she is clever, but she wouldn't be pleased if I told her so. Stupidity is the idol of the British aristocrat. She is certainly that. You have probably found her photograph in some English papers, but in case you haven't I send you one herein. It doesn't do her justice. Secondly, do I - love her very much?

Answer, Yes. Thirdly, does she love me very much? I fear that the answer is "no." I take it that

she loves my money, and does not regard me as a serious drawback.

Fourthly, how did it come about? I don't quite know how to explain that. I admired her immensely from the first, but I did not intend to propose. Vide answer to question three, as. to which I was not under any delusion. Also I thought that I dared not. • She seemed such a lovely icicle. After our third dance at a ball the ice, seemed to melt a little. She was flushed, ivid the flush made her loveliness too lovely for my resolution. We were walking through the conservatory, and I heard myself, as if it were someone else, saying, "Lady Muriel, will you marry me?" My 'heart sank into my boots as soon as I had said it. She stood still and looked straight • ahead of her for some time. Then she said "Yes." Then there was an awkward pause. I said, "I suppose I may kiss you?" She said, "There is no necessity, unless you par- ticularly want to." I said; "But I do!" She laughed faintly; and I did. She was not very responsive, then or since. On the other hand, she did not shrink from me— then or since. I think she has no objection to me, but she does not encourage demon- stration, and I do not bore her with it. She is easily bored. Fifthly, how do I get on among the Eng- lish aristocracy? My dear Sis, I don't get on. To me they are denizens of another world, a world that is partly very noble, and partly very petty. I call them "the inhabitants of Mars." They seem to have been brought up in another planet. They are very polite to me, and I am very polite to them; but we haven't an ' idea or an interest in common. The exception to prove my statement is not that fairest Martian Lady Muriel, but her crabbed old Aunt Esther, the grimmest alai most martial of these strange people. She is sixty and nearly six feet; a gaunt, impressite old woman, with a tongue like a razor; but I like her, and she likes me. We talk a deal, far more than my fair fiancée and I talk. I have propounded the Martian theory to her, and expressed my dtmbt of Martians being flesh an 4 blood. She says, "They'd bleed if you scratched them, you silly young donkey ! " She aflded to that yesterday. "Have you. scratched scratched Muriel?" she asked, putting up her eyeglasses and glaring it me through them. That set me thinking, and your letter has stimulated thought. I intend to pursue the subject with Aunt Esther this evening. (It's Christmas ,Eve, by the way.) Afterwards I'll add another in- stalment to this letter. _ _ _

I feel lonely among the cold Martians. I wish you were here, little flesh and blood Sis. You're such a good girl to me.

I went downstairs and found A,uut Esther sitting aloof from the rest, playing Patience. She told me to go away, but I didn't. When she bad finished the hand and was about to start another, I removed the aces and informed her that I wanted to talk.

"You are the only person in this house who would dare to behave like that to me!" she observed. "I -wonder why?" "Because you like me," I stated. She nodded several ,sharp nods. "Yes," she agreed. "What do you want to talk about, stupid boy?" "Martians," I said. "Martians? What is the matter • with them?"

"They're so inhuman," I complained. "I've told you before," she observed, "that they're human enough, if you scratch them." "I don't quite see my way to scratching them," I told her. "Not even Muriel?" She looked at me as if she was riddling my jacket. "Certainly not Muriel," I "stated. "Then," she said, "you've no business to marry her. Why are you doing it?" "Because she's scratched me, I suppose," I replied. "Badly? Come. Own up like a man!" "Infernally badly," I acknowledged. (In- fernally isn't really a swear, dear Sis). "You've been engaged for over a fort- night," she reminded me. "Do you tell me that you haven't made sure of her feeling toward you?" "I haven't," I stated. "I suppose you're not an entire amateur at courting? ,Don't you dare, prevaricate with me!"

She looked so fierce that I had to laugh. "Next time I catch you under the mis- tletoe," I declared, "I'll show you!" Aunt Esther laughed for the first time in our acquaintance. It drew all eyes on us for a moment. "Do you hug Muriel?" she demanded sharply. I believe I gasped. "No," I owned. "You see—help me out, Aunt Esther. Muriel is an inhabitant of Mars to me." "Poof ! She is the girl who has promised to marry you. And you're afraid of her!" "Do you think that hugging would urn. prove matters?" I inquired. Aunt Esther pursed her lips. ,"I think it would clear them up," she said. She looked very hard at me. "If you propose to marry a—an inhabitant of Mars —you might as well ascertain her feelings toward you. Of course, I don't know your motive in proposing to her, but . . . Boy, is it love with you?" "Yes, Aunt Esther," I said. "I —I sup- pose Muriel wouldn't marry me if she didn't—if she disliked me." "If her position /is only that," Aunt Esther said determinedly, "she has no right to marry you. She does not honour me with her confidence. I can only guess at her reasons. If she wishes to repair the family fortunes—they are a set of incapable, improvident fools !—I don't blame her so much as I blame you. I call you a boy. You are a boy to rue. But you are seven and twenty becoming a person of note in the financial world, I understand; spoken of as a worthy successor to your clever father. You've knocked about and seen life—Oh! I know! You're tolerably good- looking You're a grown-up man to Murfel—you've a right to ruin your own life, if you choose, but you have no right to rain Muriel's, A man can endure a pretty wife without much love between them. To a woman it's—damnation !" She 'set her lips with almost a snap. "I know," she said with almost a groan. "I know." "You mean that she doesn't care for me?" I asked. I felt a bit white. "I mean that you ought to find out. You ought to have found out long ago; at the very first. I can't tell you. The inhabi-

that I was older, and more responsible; and she told me that I had only to ask you the question to learn the truth. "I am indebted to her. I am unable to congratulate, you upon your choice of time and place for putting the question. It strikes me as a somewhat embarrassing one." She fanned herself vigorously. "It should have been put three; weeks ago," she told me rather sharply, "since you attached importance to it. I was justified in sup- posing that you did not. You have no right to blame me." _ .

"Lady Muriel," I said, "if I tried to blame you, or think any fault of you, I could not. I understand your answer, and you need not pain yourself by putting it in words. If you think that I should not make you unhappy, I should still wish—" She held up her hand quickly. "Pardon me," she said; "I do not wish to depreciate your understanding, but you have asked me a question, and I prefer to answer it in my own terms. I will not do so now. There are too many eyes upon us, and I am too angry. Well, I am not exactly angry with you, -because I believe all that you have said, and I esteem you as an honourable, if somewhat—Sinnewhat foolish gentleman. I will answer you upon a more favourable opportunity—l, too, should like to consider how to express myself nicely." She smiled faintly. "Father is coming to 'fetch me ,to help him with the presents. We attach great. importance to our Christmas remembrances of one another in Mars. Yes, I'll come, father. Mr. Rayner has been teaching me astronomy. It appears that some of the planets are inhabited by curious people, whorn lack all the terrestrial virtues— except truth. They don't even tell that, unless they are asked." "Sensible people!" Lord Oldways said. "Truth is always deuced unpleasant!" "Remember that," she told me, "and put their reticence to their credit." _ _ _

She bowed daintily to me, looking back over her shoulder. Oh, little Ski! She's so beautiful. Wasn't there a story I used to read to you, when 'I could read and you couldn't, about a man who caught a fairy and couldn't keep her? Of course, I don't want to keep her, since she doesn't care for me—and of course I do— Well, I've played a Man's part, and put it out of my power now. It was a despicable bargain to buy a woman who didn't care for me, and I knew all along that she didn't. The portrait I am sending is my best excuse. Don't blame her, Sis. She is honest. If I had made love a condi- tion she would not have agreed to marry me, and she will not now. I know the sub- stance of her answer, though I do not know how daintily she will clothe it in words. She only acted as people do in English society. They are different from you and me—the in- habitants of Mars.

I am writing this before I go to sleep; if I sleep to-night. I doubt it. I went downstairs some time before twelve. Muriel was helping in the final arrangement of the presents. I did not see her till I trailed into the library with the procession, side by side with Aunt Esther. We had not talked, but she had seen me speaking to Muriel. She touched my arm. An inhabitant of this cruel planet can be human! You would like her.

_ Muriel was standing beside her father and mother. Every time I see her afresh I am amazed at her beauty, but I was never so amazed as then. She looked her loveliest. The thought that I was losing her cut me like a knife. _ _

The presents were arranged in batches, with a card in Muriel's very clear writing showing the name of the recipient of each batch. The separate presents were labelled by the donors. They were very numerous. I liked this pleasant Martian custom. It seemed the most human thing that I had found among them. I liked also the way that they bowed to each other as they took up the gifts. I had presents from Lord and Lady Old- ways, and all their little children—l had given them presents, too, of course—and a cigar-case "From Aunt Esther to Harry Rayner, in appreciation of his courage in being friends with her." I had given her something, needless to say, and we bowed and shook hands. The last present I took up was labelled "Mr. H. D. Rayner, from an Inhabitant of Mars." Muriel had evidently written the label since our conversation. It was a tiny package, obviously jewellery. It flashed across my mind that I should find her en- gagement ring it in. I looked quickly at her hand, but she held it under the table. She noticed my look and turned away. I opened the package behind Aunt Esther's broad back, and found a little red charm on a small golden chain. It was a heart! The room seemed to go round and round, and the chatter of the people near me sounded a long way off. For the heart represented Muriel's own, I knew. I had often seen it hanging at her neck. She was opening her presents at a little side table, and carefully avoided looking at me. Presently she walked to the door, and I followed. There was a footman in the pas- sage. I would have liked to annihilate him. "Come to the conservatory, sweetheart," I bege,fred. She gave a faint cry. You have never called me that before!" she said. _ _ _

"But . you are," I told her. "Yes," she said. "I am." We passed the sfately footman. There was a • wrap lying on a chair. I put it round her shoulders. "Mars is a cold place, lovely lady," I said, and she smiled and smiled. "But ?he man in the story was very care- ful of the inhabitant," she whispered, and 'still she smiled. I slipped my hand through her arm and led her into the conservatory among the palms— " You will make me drop all my Christmas presents," she objected; "and—oh, Harry! If you squeeze me so you will break your Christmas—sweetheart !

CHRISTMAS THOUGHTS.

Why Not Make Christmas A

Permanent Example ? - • The festival which carries with it the great- est happiness and well-doing is at hand. Christmas is the fruition of ,Christianity's hopes. On this day more than any other do we get closer to the Divine Command to love one another—to do to others as we wish to be done by. The unbeliever as well as the believer can rejoice that one day is set_ apart for ehibiting that brotherly love which ought to prevail among men the other 364. If it is good for the wealthy to relax the purse strings at Christmastide, why not extend the practice to the other days? Those in need are always with us, and the claim they have upon those blessed with abundance is as urgent in summer and spring and autumn as it is in winter. The cheery greeting which goes with the relaxed purse can confer just as much sweet- ness upon those whom we mingle with in every- day life on the other days of the calendar ,as on Christmas. The smile, the pleasant word, cost nothing, yet how many there are who act' as if their manifestation was something of an effort to be indulged only at intervals and then to an exclusive few. Cultivate the habit of giving cheer to those about you all the year and see if you are not well repaid at its close by the happiness you have conferred upon others. The spirit which prompts the well-to-do to assist the poor at this season, to visit the sick -and to perform other works ?f Mercy, is Christ- like. In no way can we better testify to the truths of our religion. In no Other way can we make so plain our love for' our fellows. In no other way can we. promulgate so well the superiority of' our civilisation over the kinds which sit in the darkness and have no heart for those who fare ill in the struggle for existence. -

Let flaese reflections have a part in your meditations as the glories of Christmas are unfolded next Saturday. You have given liberally to the poor so that no one shall go hungry or shiver from the cold on that day. Your horizon of helpfulness; however, can be enlarged. if all who make Christmas a day of giving and of cheer would extend these attri- butes to all the days in the calendar there would he little suffering Where now the misery is immeasurable.

—Kindness and helpfulness are the watch- words for Christmas! What would be more noble than to extend their sway over the entire year?

"I don't know that she has much heart; but such as it is—l am afraid, if she had been free to marry as she pleased, it wouldn't have been you. I don't mean that there was anything serious; or even that there was a formed attachment .on her side, but—l've exceeded what I ought to say, Harry, and I shall say no more. As you have asserted, I like you. You remind me of someone else—lt was a long time ago, and—There's no fool like an old fool. A broken heart is supremely ridiculous after thirty. Don't break yours and hers. Have it out with her. Don't flatter yourself she'll grow to 'love you, if she doesn't now. There are soft little fools who can be 'hugged' into loving. Muriel isn't that sort. "No," I agreed. "I gather you think she doesn't care for me; and won't?" , I looked at Aunt Esther, but she was silent. I continued after an interval. "I'll ask her outright," I promised. "It wasn't that I dared not ask. I was— another sort of coward. I wouldn't put my question in a way that made it harder for her to say 'yes. I wanted her, on any terms, and I thought—Well, I didn't realise what it meant to her. Thank you! If I am banished from Mars, may I come to see you in town sometimes?" "Do," she said. I suppose no one who knew her would believe me; but the way she said it was charmingly pleasant and cordial, and she actually smiled. I remember being in a real blue funk three times—when I first went to school, when I ,first played for the college team, and when I first had to clinch a big deal with- out consulting the governor. I'd rather go through all three again than say what I have to say to Muriel to-night. It isn't fear, really. It's—l suppose you'll think I'm a fool—but the best word I can find is reverence.

I've done it—loved little Sis. I am sit- ting in my room for half an hour trying to steady myself before I go down to the Santa Claus affair at midnight. They have a custom here—they are all customs in Mars —of giving the grown-ups their Christmas presents at midnight on Christmas Eve, just before Santa Claus visits the children's stockings. It is a pleasant custom, I think. You will be thinking that it is immaterial to the real subject, and that I am writing about it mainly becalm I shrink from coming to that. You are quite correct. I don't like writing this • but I will. When we . went to the drawing-room after dinner I talked to Aunt Esther for a time. Muriel had • gone to help her father lay . out the - presents in the library. Presently, she appeared and walked up to us. "Are you entertaining Aunt Esther?"_ she asked me, "or is Aunt Esther entertaining you? Or neither?" "Chiefly neither," Aunt Esther said tartly. `He has no scandal and no small talk. I have been racking my brains to find subjects suited to his intellect." If any," I commented. "I did not intend a missing • word com- petition, but you win the prize! Take him away, Muriel. I am at the last gasp of in- tellect." "Then I realise the vast expenditure oo ray behalf," I observed. "You take the prize again," Aunt Esther said; "but for goodness' sake go !" We strolled over to a deep recess in a big bay. window. "And what was the subject suited to your intellect?" Lady Muriel asked in her unmoved way. She always speaks as if nothing 'natters. "The inhabitants of Mars !" I stated. "Oh! It's a distant planet, I believe." "That," I said, "is the point. Where will you sit? Shall I turn a chair round?" "I'll face the window, I think., We might see stars; and they might be planets. There is only the electric lamp at the end of the drive, so far as I can discover; but it may represent Mars. Shall we take the lodge- keeper as the inhabitant?" "I think," I said, "I will take you." ,"I don't know whether that is flattering or the reverse. Perhaps you can tell me about the Martians?" "Yes; I can tell you something. There was a man once who went to Mars; s' very earthly man from the very warm earth, Lady Muriel." She raised her eyebrows. "The hand is Esau's," she observed. "Is the voice Aunt Esther's?" "No," I denied. "It is a voice that you have not heard distinctly before, Lady Muriel; my own He had nothing to say againstO the new world that he found him- self in. It was, in many ways, a very noble world, and he. thought that perhaps the fault was in him, and that he failed to understand its very noble people. To him they seemed so cold. The man fell in love with a beautiful inhabitant • of Mars—in ' love in his warm earthly way.. I want you , to understand that he was very much in love with her—as I am with you." She fanned herself slowly. Otherwise she was very, very still. "The story seems to have a somewhat personal application," she observed quite calmly. "Frankly," I said, "it is personal. It seemed easier to put what I. want to say to yoa in that way. I will put it more per- sonally if you wish." "On the contrary," she denied, "I am admiring your discretion." "Then I will tell you about the inhabit- ant of Mars. She was really very beauti- ful. He asked her to marry him, and she consented. He would have been very happy if— He was not sure whether her cold way—the way of the exalted world in which she was born and bred—meant that her care for him was love in the manner of her world; or whether it meant that she did not love him at all. He wanted her so lunch that he feared to ask the obvious question. He did- not ask for nearly three weeks. You see, he wanted her on any terms. Till it occurred to him that, if she did not love him in any way at all—not even in the colder way of her world—it was a very terrible thing for her. For that reason, and for that 'reason only—this is a very honest story, Lady Muriel—he decided to speak 'frankly to her. He 'wanted to find some way of speaking that did not compel her to tell him anything that she did not wish to tell; that left it to her to answer his question plainly, or by a sign; or, if .she pleased, not to answer it at all. He wanted her to understand quite clearly that he spoke from no diminution of his desire to- ward her. I hope I convince you of that?" "I am stupid at parables," Lady Muriel remarked. She had a. colour, which was not usual with her, otherwise her face was un- moved. "In plain English, then," I said, "I loved you, and I love you, and I always shall. is my statement clear?" "Yes," she said. "It is clear. And the question?" • "The question is, do you love me?" I told her. "And unless I do," she continued, do not wish to marry me?" "I wish to Marry you," I told her; "but I do not wish .you to marry, me at the cost of —" I paused. "Of my self-respect," she- supplied, icily. "I presume that Aunt Esther has supplied you with an estimate of my', character, and of the motives which may be presumed to influence me?" "Aunt Esther is well able to defend her- self," I said. A CHRISTMAS WOOING

Christmas! The very word sounded foreign to the ears of Birsie, and fell strangely from its lips. In those far-away days when London and Edinburgh were still a long week's journey apart. Christmas to donee Scottish folk savoured pagan observance. If the young laird's new wife had fixed her supper party for Hogmanay or Ne'erday, Birsie could have understood it, and would have trooped willingly to the festival but Christmas! But, poor thing, she was English, and knew no better.

And up at the big white house the little bride was amazed and affronted at the doubtful reception of her invitations, and piqued by her husband's laughing, "Didn't I tell you so?" "Not keep Christmas!" she ejaculated in dismay. "They are no better than heathen, then, for all their kirks and Sabbaths. But I'll make them keep it." And Lady Mary, put upon her mettle, had triumphed, and for the first time since Reformation days Birsie was going "to keep Christmas," and in every house that Christ- mas Eve some preparation was going for- ward for the great occasion. Even in Rab Plenderleith's house there was no exception, and little Lady Mary had every right to plume herself over her at least partial con- quest of the old weaver. It is needless to try to explain to which particular "straitest sect of the Pharisees"

e belonged. A stern, hard man, a natural ascetic, to whom all pleasure was doubtful if not sinful, he himself lived up to the very letter of his rigorous code. - It was a tribute to Lady Mary's powers that she had wrung from him an unwilling consent that his orphan niece should join the company at the Big House. Now, in the attic under the rafters, and by the glimmering light of a cruisie, Mile, like every other girl in Birsie, was looking over her "braws." Poor child, it did not take her long. She had only her "best" frock, sacred hitherto to Sabbath wear, and even that had been handed down to her, after years of service, by her Aunt Bethia. • Ain() was a slim, _slip of a girl, like a plant which has shot up in the shade. She was in truth: "A maid whom there were none to praise."

It was allowed, indeed, that she had bonnie hair. It had the sheen and the changing hue of ripe, rustling sun-gilt corn, but its beauty was disguised as much as might be, since it was drawn so tightly back, and so severely flattened to her small head. Under Rab's iron rule every irnocent vanity, every girlish desire, had been so sternly repressed that Ailie had humbly accepted the clumsy gown. Of late, however, a gnawing dissatisfac- tion had awakened within her. The Big House, closed through . the laird's long minority, had been re-opened on his mar- riage, and peopled not only by the gentry but by a troop of serving men, and smart, strapping maidens. Among these "bonnie Bell Armour" had at once made herself a conspicuous figure—a plump, red-checked, black-haired lass, whose laughing eye S held a lure which to many of the Birsie lads Seemed irresistible. And alas, and alas! among those who seemed most ready to bow to that garish rising sun was Sandy Armstrong— Sandy, who on one memorable day had stood up to take Ailie's ",palmies " for her and had dared the dominie to "lick the lassie." _ _

All Birsie, except flab himself, had ex- pected that they would marry some day. Sandy ' Armstrong, donee, thriving lad though he might be, was not "sound," nab considered, on some minutite of doctrine or kirk discipline—and that ;was enough. • Still, love, which laughs at locksmiths, is not likely to be fettered by the bonds of abstract theology. In the narrow bounds of village life, the lad and lass were often thrown together, and in spite of flab matters might already have come to a crisis between them, but for the tongue-tied bashfulness which had fallen upon Sandy. But now, instead of haunting the loan- ings round the weaver's cottage, Sandy was hapging about the back regions of the Big House for a smile or a word from his flaunt- ing goddess. And if this were so in every- day life, what could be expected to-morrow night? The new maids, like their betters, had brought the new modes from Edin- burgh. In what braws might Bell appear, while she—she had only Aunt I3ethia's old grey gown., • A gush of salt, burning tears made the dim light of the cruisie flicker before Ailie's eyes, ,and blurred still more the little pale face at which she was gazing in her flawed handbreadth of looking-glass. Suddenly Ailie gasped, a flush, which might have vied with Bell's, dyed her face. She had nothing but the old grey gown, there was nothing she could do to better it, but why should not her hair curl, too? Ailie's breath stopped at the audacity of the thought. She had never done any- thing save at her aunt's or uncle's bidding, and she had not been brought up under flab's rule without her conscience instantly taking fright, and clamouring that it was wrong to think so much of outward things —the true adorning was of the heart. Perhaps it was, but what will a girl, waking to a knowledge of her own heart, not do to beautify herself in the eyes of .the man she loves? She shook out her hair, and it fell in a glory round liar slim shoulders. But the thought of Bell's clustering black ring- lets blinded her to its beauty—besides, every- one was wearing. curls now. She glanced fearfully round, and then with panting breath and trembling, awkward fingers began her unaccustomed—her unheardrof attempt. Down in the kitchen flab was sitting with his big Bible still upon his knee, but he was listening anxiously to the faint sounds over- head. "What's the lassie doin'? " he demanded with suddenness which made his sister, a little faded wisp of a woman, jump. "Doin' ? What should she be doin'? She went awa' to her .bed, as soon as the exercises (family prayers) were over." "Ay, and I'm dootin' if she heard a word o' them. Bethia, I'm in a sore strait," he went on solemnly. "The charge o' my puir brither's dochter's soul has been laid upon -"me, and I'm dootin' if I havena be- trayed the trust. Against my conscience and my judgment I let myself be whillie- wha'ed by a smooth tongue to gie my ion- sent to this carnal pleasurin', but—' "Leah, .Rab ! " sried' Bethia aghast, " ye'd ne'er keep her at hame the morn's nicht. The haill toou's goin' but oureel's "—even in Bethia's well-trained mind there may have been a stir of regret—" an' ner heart's fair set on it." flab's pale eye lightened. "Out of thine own mouth will I judge thee, woman. If her heart's set on sic carnality isna it my bounden duty to wean her from it? :what's the morn's nicht after a'? No more than 'the crackling o' thorns under a pot,' but for her it may well lowe into the fire that never shall be quenched. I've seen the change in her ever since I gie'd my word, an' she was fair itchin' till I'd put .by the books to-night. That bodes . something. Awa' up an' see what's she doin.' Dinna let her ken." Poor Bethia crept away towards the ladder-like stair. In a few moments she came back, her face white. "Oot wi' it, woman—the truth ! " de- manded Dab fiercely. "Oh flab, she's but a wean! Dinna be hard on her ! " quavered Bethia. •"I kenna how she's been so led astray. It was ne'er me that learned her. Wed, if a mann say it—" the words wrenched from her by sneer panic. - one's—sue a tnrang earwig her hair ! " She sat rocking herself to and fro. She hardly knew whether •she was awake or asleep, when she looked up at last and saw her brol-' 3r standing over her. She uttered a faint cry of fright as the light fell on a pair of shining shears in his hand. "Hold your peace," he said, putting down the cruise. "I've been wrestling sore for

What for is she no at the Big Reese'?" Sandy demanded abruptly when the door of the weaver's cottage was opened a cautious crack. "She's—she's no wed," stammered Bethia. "No weel ! The Portrait painter.

A woman with a fatal squint came to a fashionable portrait painter. He looked at her and she looked at him, and both were embarra.Qed.

He spoke first. • "Won't you permit me," he said, "to take your portrait in profile? There is a certain shyness about one of your eyes which is as dim- cult in art as it is fascinating in nature."

SUCCESS IN THEIPOULTRY r•;. YARD.

The Chance Of The Life For

Small Keepers.

By E. T. BROWN.

Never before in the history of the poultry in- dustry has there been so great a demand for eggs, and never have prices been so high. The poultry keeper has the opportunity of his life, if he will but rise to it. We are receiving Jess than half our usual supply of eggs from abroad, while many of those which do arrive are only of third-rate quality. For the home develop- ment of the industry we must look to the farmers of this country, to the smallholders, to the cottagers, and to the suburban poultry- keepers. Each in his own special direction can do a great deal towards the increase of home production. It is to a multiplicity of staall poultry keepers we must look for the greatest development, not to the establishment of a few huge poultry farms. To achieve the full measure of success new and up-to-date methods must be adopted. The best varieties must be chosen; feeding, hou,sing and general management must be on right lines. The modern hen is a very different thing to her sister of twenty or thirty years .ago. She lays far more.eggs,.and consequently a greater strain is put upon her system. She thus requires very careful management, she must be provided with suitable quarters, and she must be fed in a proper manner. , • Economy in the Poultry Yard.

To-day is a day of economy. Extravagance in any shape or form must be avoided. The poultry yard is no- exception to the rule. Per- haps in feeding fowls is waste most apparent. The waste which occurs in this direction is very great, and I do not think it is an exaggeration to say that nineteen out of twenty poultry- keepers are at one time or another guilty of wasting food. The common practice of throw- ing food down upon the ground is responsible for a good deal. When the ground is hard and dry there is no objection to scattering grain, but in this ease it is necessary to see that too much is not distributed, for it cannot be collected again. But the plan of throwing soft food on the ground is very wasteful, because a large part of it is trampled on by the fowls, and renderei quite unfit for consumption. The use of the feeding trough—a good. one can be made for about twopence—will effect a great saving in the food bill, the bane of many a poultry-keeper's otherwise happy existence. So far as appliances, are concerned, there is plenty of room to practise economy: It often happens that a nail here or a screw there may prolong the life of a house by Years. A large waste . occurs on nearly every poultry farm through the neglect of appliances. They should all be regularly overhauled, and repaired whenever necessary. .They should also be tarred Or painted at least twice a year, for this preserves the woodwork greatly. The Curse of Tainted SO.

Nothing. brings about disaster in the poultry .yard sooner than foul ground. On fowls of all sizes and ages and kinds it has a very injurious effect, but particularly upon chickens during the first few months of their lives. The same plot. if possible, should not be used for rearing two consecutive years. If space is very limit, it is .better in the long run only to -rear half the number of birds,- so that fresh ground can be used each season. It is customary to confine chickens, at any rate during the first few weeks of their lives, in small runs, and when this is the case. it is very necessary indeed to remove the run every day, or at least, every, alternate day. • Foul ground is an almost certain fore- runner of, disease, and many of the complaints from which chickens 'suffer are directly brought about through inattention to this matter. Not only does tainted sbil bring about disease, but it retards growth and stunts development. It also, means that the soil over which chickens are running is devoid, or almost devoid, of animal and vegetabletgrowth, two extremely im- portant items in the diet of 'young chickens. If the ground in a poultry run becomes foul the birds. should be remoyed,-and a light dressing. of gas lime applied. It should also be watered with a one per cent, solution of sulphuric acid. For a few days afterwards it is unwise to allow the fowls access thereto. Some Causes of Disease.

- ----- Among the principal causes of ill-health among poultry are. negligence in housing, inat- tention to sanitation, and over-stocking of land. There is no end to the evils of bad housing. There are many diseases that owe their beginning to the manner in which fowls are housed, diseases which, perhaps, start in a very mild form, ,but which are often the forerunner of much more serious complaints. Freedom from draughts is an absolute necessity if .the inmates are to. be, kept from colds. and the evils . that follow in their wake. Badly-fitting _joints, or boards not closely joined together, .or a downward current .of air is each-- quite. Sufficient to cause disease, , and yet :each can he .so simply remedied with. very little trouble ;arid practically .nO expense.' The 'material of which the house is constructed .more often than otherwise is too thin, the wind almost blowing through. .In such houses as these the birds are always roosting in . a cold atmos- phere, which is far from conducive to their well- being. •Ventilation is of first importance, since this is doubtless a great factor towards the main- tenance of health. The aim should be to secure adequate ventilation, at the same time avoiding a draught. It is equally important that clean- liness be strictly observed, for -this. will :assist very considerably in keeping the air sweet. All the internal fittings, such as perches and nest- boxes, should be simply constructed, and made so that they can easily.,a.nd quickly be removed.' This will ensure the attendant reachingUll the corners • and crevices when cleaning. The perches should rest in slots cut in the frame- work of the • house, and they are thus easily taken out. • The Late Pullets.

There are ,4 good many complaints • this season concerning .the lateness of some of the pullets commencing to lay. Such birds should be generously fed upon nutritious foods, meals and grains of a fattening nature being avoided. 1 have always found it is a good plan to supply three meals daily for a, few weeks previous to the commencement of laying, as this Seems to give the birds such an excellent 'start, and they appear better able to withstand the heavy strain of laying. It should .not be forgotten in this connection that in laying there is a' consider- able strain upon the system of the hen. If any of the pullets are backward, and have • every appearance of not beginning to lay for some time yet, a little maize added to both morning and evening meals has an exceedingly beneficial result. It must not be • overdone, of course; about, one-fifth part of bulk of maize to four. fifths of wheat or 4.rley or oats is the best proportion.

If all the bees were to hang round the hive, the honey supply 'would indeed be scarce.

Many a man's good luck really consists of having been born with a little common sense.

A milkman in a town not far from New York was brought before -the Court to answer a charge of adulteration of milk. ".You are charged," said the judge.," with a most serious offence. Have you anything to say in answer to the charge?" "Well, your worship, the night before it was raining very hard, and the only cause I can givc is, the: tow 'lnnst have got wet through.

DRINK RESTRJC:IIONs.

Sir,—Your correspondent, "Mr. Simpson, of Liverpool," is sadly lacking in a sepse of proper-

. tion. He has frequently alleged that the action of the Control Board had resulted in the punish- ment of the innocent—the vast majority of :munition workers—for the sins of the guilty minority. In proof of his allegations he recently quoted Mr. Ben Tilled, who asserted that the • .guilty minority did not exceed 5 per cent. of the total number of men snemployed. Anyone reading Mr. Simpson's letter would igghtly, assume that he had adopted :Mr. "Tillett"s figures, and I have no doubt whatever that .such was his intention until I showed what an enormous aggregate a 5 per Cent. of shirkers amounted to and the tremendous loss of potential output which . such "slacking" Mr. Simpson has fallen into the pit' which he ./diniself,has "digged," out of which he 'cannot easily emerge. On the 5 per cent. basis—which Was good enough for him in his ignorance to quole against me and which aggregates to 25,000 men, be furnished me with a weapon with which I have completely destroyed any • value which otherwise might have attached to his asser- - f ions. Mr. Simpson's arguments and his facts (?) are ,inutually destructive and are on a par with his statement about the "remarkable outburst of public opinion" which has recently occurred, and which has "compelled the Contrel 'Board to seriously modify the restrictions in the London area." • This remarkable "outburst of public opinion" • kis resulted in the "fiasco" at Cleveland—one of the most unpatriotic and contemptible efforts which has ever disgraced the public life of our country—but .there is one grain of comfort to be found in the fact that the true opinion of the electors on this question has been ' ascer- tained, and while it is a bitter disappointment to the Trade apologists of Mr. Simpson's type, it is a great encouragement to all trite patriots and will do much to strengthen the hands of the Control Board in their efforts for our be- loved Country in this grave time of peril. CITIZEN.

Sir,—The controversy between "Citizen" and myself has been unduly prolonged., and I think we .might well declare a "Christmas truce." Although my opponent's letters have occupied twice the, space taken up by mine, I am wil- ling to allow him the privilege of haring the last word, provided, of course, that he does not open up any fresh aspect of the subject, or otherwise take an unfair advantage of my offer to sheath the sword—pro tem. I must confess that I cannot share "Citizen's" admiration, for the :moral elevation ,of the professional "temperance" lecturer. Mr. Stiggins is id! mittedly not a very pleasing character., and .his modern prototype is almost equally unlovely. A man who .gets his living by vilifying his fellow-citizens and endeavouring to destroy-. their rights and liberties, with the ultimate object of ruining a lawful and necessary indus- try, is certainly not in a position to point the finger of scorn at his opponents. When we remember that teetotalist funds- are, to some extent, sustained by trade rivals. of the brewer and distiller; the matter assumes a 6till more dubious character.

As regards "Citizen's" new supporter, Mr. Robert Williams, everyone who knows anything about Trade Unionism—and "Citizen," I think, rather. prides himself on his deep and exclusive understanding of the British proletariat—knows that the leaders, and espe- cially such leaders as Mr. Williams, are no- wise representative of the rank and file. What- ever "labour leaders," the overwhelming majority of the rank and file bitterly resent these undemocratic restrictions. "Citizen" says: "The evidence of the good results which have followed the Operations of the Control Board are obvious and overwhelm- ing." I most emphatically deny that the evi- dence is either obvious or overwhelming. The Board in its own official report only claims a miserable half-success, and the "evidence" for this is a mass of cooked statistics and un- supported assertions. If the Board has any really convincing evidence, why does not it put it forward? 'perhaps " Citizen " is better in- formed of the Board's ,beneficent achievements than is the Board itself. Mr. Patrick O'Brien intervenes to tell us that Mr. Tillett is, or has been, a•teetotaler. and—to judge from the extracts given—a pretty hot one at that! I was aware of this before, but the fact that the famous leader of the -Docks Strike held these- anti-liquor views only adds weight to his present protest against too drastic restriction. -

J. E. SIMPSON Liverpool, DeeenOler 11111.

First And Third Places In Lancashire And Cheshire.

At a meeting of the Warrington Education Committee on Monday evening, a tunmary of the results of the examinations in hei ence, commercial and other subjects for the ,session 1914-15 was presented, and it showed that a good number Of successes had been gained by local students.

The number of students examined under the Board of Education Science Examination was nine. Five passed and four failed. In the Union of Lancashire and Cheshire Institutes (Science Examinations) 58 students were examined. Twenty-seven passed and 31 failed.

in the City and Guilds of London Institute, 41 students were exaMined in various subjects. A SPRIG OF HOLLY

The little foom was very bare, but in all his hardy, strenuous life, Pere Michel had never known the meaning of the word Luxury. If he might have a table at which to write—a table whose corner must be cleared with a sigh -whenever his white- haired servant, worthy Catherine, elected to bring him his scanty meals—an armchair in which to sit by the wood fire in the even- ings and enjoy the one cigar he daily allowed himself, arid a shelf of treasured books, his chief solace and refreshment, Pere Michel needed nothing more. Unless, per- haps, he would have found it difficult to do without the great pots of deep-coloured carnation flowers that stood upon his little window-sill, turning their fragrant blossoms to the sunlight which flooded the peaceful valley from morning till night. Beyond the window soft grey mountain peaks cut across the blue of the sky, mountains whose feet were set in olive gardens and orange groves, whilst their higher slopes were clothed -with oak and birch, and all those hardier growths which seem to belong rather to the north than to the south. Pere Michel• loved the mountains. Something in his strong and simple nature was akin to those grey rocks and clean-cut peaks that raised such erect heads in the sunlight; and his blue eyes, though they were faded now and dim with age, held something of the clearness of the blue, over-arching sky. Year in, year out, he lived in that peaceful valley, shut off by its rampart of grey rocks from the outside world, only hearing echoes of that world now and again when some traveller toiled up from the town far below, to look about the little mountain village. with curious, critical eyes.

Seated by his open window, the old Curd could hear the whistle of the. train in the plains beneath, could even see the white smoke trailing awdy across the mulberry trees and the vines and the maize fields, and a smile would flicker out over his fine old face, as memory brought back to him thoughts of the long-ago days when he, too, had lived in the great world of trains, and men, and the busy hum of life. Well, well! He was old now; be had grown old in the service of those people in the valley amongst the mountains, and he had learnt to love the little village set against its rocks, and looking out across the sunny plains to the far-off snow peaks that bounded the horizon. Although the, month was December, mad the days wanted only a fortnight till Christ- mas, the sheltered little village was very. warm, very bright. The Cure's carnations were not the only ones that swung frag- rant heads in the little breeze that danced along the mountain sides. Outside many of the villagers' windows carnations trailed their grey-green leaves, and crimson or pale pink blossoms along the wooden balconies, and down towards the sunny square. And in the little square itself, before the white church, pink rosebuds nodded, in the sun- shine, and over Mere Angelique's house the bees were humming amongst crimson roses, opening their royal hearts to the light. Pere Michel, his frugal midday meal ended.. sat in his chair beside the window, patiently waiting until Madame Catherine should have cleared away the plates, allow- ing him to return to his writing and his books. His brief siesta was over; lie cast occasional longing glances towards the table, and the writing materials which Ms servant had removed to a neighbouring chair. Her entrance with his meal had cut ruthlessly into his train of titought, but the gentle old man never dreamt of remonstrating with Madame Catherine who, as far as his bodily needs were concerned, ruled him with a rod of iron. Perhaps it was as well that she did wield that iron rod, otherwise it is more than probable that he would have forgotten .the necessity for eating, any meals at all; `and it is quite within the region of possi- bility that he also would have omitted to go to bed had not Catherine taken away his lamp at a fixed hour every evening, and in- formed him sternly that his bedroom was ready and his candle alight. Stern as was her discipline, the good old servant adored her master, as, indeed, did every man, woman, and child who came under Pere Michel's kindly influence. He never passed along the village street without a score of laughing, chattering urchins at his heels clinging to his hands, grasping his shabby old cassock. He could not stir a foot from his house but some village wife or mother would come to pour into his sympathetic ears a tale of joy or wee; and .morning, noon and night the door of his little- dwell- ing stood open wide that. all . Who would might enter and seek his never-failing help and counsel.

The door was set open now, framing that wide landscarie of sunny plain, and grey mountains, and far-away snow-peaks, which made so restful and complete a whole, that a woman who slowly climbed the steps to the open door stood on the top step for many seconds before knocking at the door, looking out silently at the beautiful world below her.

"Such peace here. Such turmoil outside a'.l this peace," she whispered, and a weary sigh broke from her as she turned away from the sunshiny prospect, and softly knocked on the Cure's door. '

Perhaps he echoed her sigh. He had greatly hoped for a quiet afternoon, during which he could give himself with undivided mind to the book he was writing—that book which in his guileless mind he fancied-would be the magnum opus of his life. Deer, gentle old man. He did not know, he never dreamt. that his greatest work—his work that would endure for ever—lay in the human lives he had helped, in the men and women he had taught to be better, purer, to come nearer to God. His innocent dreams of fame as a writer were not destined to he fulfilled; jewels that were set in Pere Michel's crown were fashioned of the souls he had led into the paths of goodness. His sigh, when he heard that knock at bis doer, was scarcely audible, and as he rose and moved across the brick-paved floor to greet his visitor, his ever-ready smile of welcome was on his face. But the smile flashed into a look of surprise when he saw the woman who waited on the threshold, for she was not in the least the sort of- visitor he had expected to see, and for a moment .the sight of her completely took the old man's breath away. TY& , woman was tall and graceful, and very stately; dressed in black, sweep- ing garments which even to the Curd's igno- rant eyes seemed very rich and costly. Their dead blackness of colouring enhanced her delicately fair complexion and the bright glory of her hair, whilst it seemed to Pere Michel that her eyes were like the wall- flowers that grew in his tiny garden in spring-time—brown, soft • (wallflowers, with the light of the dewdrops glistening in their depths. For that first moment the old Cure and his unexpected visitor looked at one another in silence, and then Pere Michael stood aside and said courteously: "Will you enter, madam? You perhaps wished to speak to me—to me, Pere Michel?"

A smile crept over the beautiful face—a smile holding in it such an infinity of sad. pow: that the old man's heart ached for her.

"I did not know your name, my father," she answered, speaking his language with fluency, though will'''. a strongly-marked foreigc accent, "I only saw—the church— your little sunny house---and—the open door. It was the open door that made me come."

"My door is always open," he answered, setting a chair close to his carnation-filled window for his surprising visitor. "My people can come to me when they will. Day and night my door is open -wide for them, and for all who need me, he added, seeing how, as the smile faded from het face, an unutterable sadness fell upon it. and what anguish looked out of the depths of ber brown eyes. "For—all who need you?a, she repeater) wistfully, "but I—am a stranger, in the land, alien in race, alien in faith. I do not worship as you worship, Mon Pere; I have no right to come to youjor help:" . _

“No right' - He looked down at her

By L. G. MOBERLY.

and something in tne glance or nis 'quiet eyes seemed to bring some balm to her troubled soul, "all who are in need or in sadness have a right to come to me for help, my daughter. And—what if our ways of worship or of faith be different—are we not all the children of God—and in God's hands?" "Are we?"—she stirred restlessly and rose from the chair in which he had placed her—"are we in God's hands? The-q His hands hurt us. They are cruel. Why do we say 'Our Father,' when he hurts us so? A father loves his children. He does not hurt them." . "Sometimes—a father hurts his children because he loves them." The old man's voice was very gentle. "A father knows better what Is good for his child, than the child can know for itself; and the great Father of us all knows best what is best for us."

There was certainty in the gentle voice; the blue eyes met the anguished brown ones with a steadfast assurance that brought with it a sense of strength.

"Y-ou say that as if you had n-o doubt of its, truth,' she said wistfully. _

"I have no doubt of it truth," he an- swered. "I have lived on this beautiful earth of ours longer than the fourscore years of which the Psalmist speaks, and I know that our Father always knows the best for all of us--,always does the best. God—is-- love."

"Is He? "—she wrung her hands passion- ately together—"is He? Then why does He' seem as if He liked to hurt me? As if— trouble 'after trouble came to me when I did not deserve them? Why have I so much to bear when all I asked for was a little happiuess—a little love?" . _ _ _

"All you mired for? You asked for love and happiness, my daughter, what did you give?"

- "What did I give? I do not understand." "Have you given love and happin? Have you spread them all 'about you, so that those who came near you basked in their sunlight? Have you greatly loved, my daughter?" _

"i" loved and was deceived "—her face all at once hardened, a great bitterness looked out of her eyes—"and since the day when I found I was being deceived I. have wan- dered over the world, trying to find peace and love and happiness again, and finding none of them." She laughed, a bitter little laugh, and Pere Michel's hand touched her shoulder. His voice was almost stern.

"You have not been carrying peace and love and happiness to others. You have only been eeking them for yourself. No wonder that your search has been in vain; it will always be in vain until you forget yourself and live for others—only for others.'!--

"I have no one to live for," she an- swered drearily. "The man I loved—tke man whom I dreamt loved me—" "What of him?" Pere Michel broke in quickly, when she paused. "Where is the min you love? Had you no duty; to fulfil towards him?" "I —towards him?" The woman drew herself up to her full regal height, a flush of colour ran over her face, a gleam of anger shot into her eyes. "He forgot his duty to me. I—have done with him for ever."

For a moment silence fell over the little bare room, and the old Cure looked thoughtfully at his visitor's beautiful changing face and at the gown of bright hair, into which the sunligfit wove threads of shinifig gold. "My daughter," he said very quietly. after that moment's pause, "will you tell me your story?" Under his steadfast eyes her own gaze wavered; her eyes with their flash of indig- nation no longer met his eyes; they turned instead to the great landscape, flooded with the light of the afternoon sun. "My story," she faltered. "It—it—is the story of many unhappy women. My hus- band----" "Your husband?" The old man's tones all at once became very grave. "You are wan- dering about the world in search of happi- ness whilst you have a husband, by whose side your place should surely be?" "Why should, it be there?" Defianeo gleamed in her eyes again. "He made my life intolerable; I thought he loved me, but he grew Aired of me, as all men tire of the women they pretend to Jove. He has his amusements,'. his 'pleasures he made ,my life a misery, and I have come away to find peace." "You tried to win him back? You were all that a wife should be to him?" the old man asked, forgetting that he spoke to a stranger—a great lady from a foreign land —and speaking as though to one of his own village folk who had forgotten her wifely duty; "you have done your utmost to heal the breach between you?"

"Done my utmost? No!" She shrugged her shoulders slightly. "He did not care to make me happy. Why should 1 concern myself to make his life smooth when he gave no thought to me? Surely my rights had to be considered. Why-" "Why should you put self aside and think of the man you promised to take for better :--for worse?" The gentle voice was suddenly. 'very stern. "Why 'should you remember your duties, and not only your rights? Why? Because the world is not a play- ground, but a place of struggle; because the good God set you here .to live for others. not for self; because," his voice softened again, "it is more blessed to give than te, receive."

Silence fell over the room again, and in the silence the old man caught the sound of a sharply indrawn breath. But the tall woman had turned her' face from him, and once more looked out towards the moun- tains.

"Tell me," he said, and there was a strange, fatherly tenderness in his voice, "tell me of the days when first you and he learnt to love one another."

"What is the good of telling you?" she answered recklessly, turning back towards him with a laugh that hurt the kindly old man; "he loved me once—he does not love me any more. Let the dead past bury its dead. Why dig it up afresh to spoil the present. In time I shall find what I want. In time I shall learn to be light of heart again. I was a fool to come here and whimper to you. I ought to have known it was a foolish quest !" Once more her laugh rang out; she made 'a little movement towards the door ; her eyes, very hard and bitter, looked defiantly into the patient old eyes that watched her, then wandered round the bare room. _ _ _

"I " she began again, and having uttered that one word, she stopped, utter- ance and movement alik arrested, whilst her eyes fixed themselves upon something over the mantelshelf. Old Catherine had stuck it there, its stalk resting behind a superlatively hideous print, its glossy leaves an& scarlet berries clearly defined against the background of limewashed wall—just a sprig of holly. brought . down from the mountain heights by a village urchin, who had shyly presented it to Pere Michel. Only a sprig. of holly. But to .the woman who saw it m this moment it seemed the symbol of so much—so very, very much. England, home. the lanes where Jack had wooed and won her, the dear grey land that was so far away from this land of sunshine; and yet— and yet— " Tell me "—the gentle old voice broke in upon a strange medly of Ihoughts—"about the days when you and he first loved each other.", Ah! Pere Michel had learnt to observe much, and to know much of the human heart, even though he lived in this sheltered valley away from the great world. "It was winter time," she said, a sudden wistfulness in her eyes, and a tremulous smile upon her lips; "an English winter— not like this." She put out her hands to- wards the glowing landscape. "There was frost on the ground, and the bare boughs of the trec.s 'made a .brown lace-work szainst t`la sky—a pale, soft sky, of misty _ AA. .............. Alm •

THE TWINS.

The benevolent old gentleman stopped at the ,sight. of the two similar-looking infants in the baby carriage, .and said in a pleasant voice to the girt in attendance:,

"AI ! Twins?" ' "Yes, sir," replied the girl; "both boys."

"So?" said the old gentleman. "How do you tell them apart? Which is which?" "This one," said the nurse, pointing, "is this, and that one is that."

"Dear me," said the old gentleman, "how very interesting. But." he added, indicating tile second one, "might not this one be this also?"

"It might," said the girl, after a short pause; "then, of course, that would be that." "Well, then," said the old gentleman, "how do you manage to separate them?" "We seldom do, but when we want to we put one in one room and the other in another."

"Do you, indeed. Which do you put in one room F"

"Sometimes this one and sometimes that."

"How do you know which one you're putting in which room?" "We look and see which is in the other room, and then we know the other is in the which room."

"Very good," said the old gentleman, warm- ing up to the problem, "but if one of them was in the house and the other was away somewhere, would you be able to tell which was in the house?"

"Oh, yes, sir," said the girl earnestly; "all we would have to do would be to look at him and then we would know that the one we saw was the one in the house, and then, of course, the one away somewhere would be the other. There are only two of them, you see, which makes it very easy."

The benevolent Old gentleman then passed on
