THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, DECEMBER 25, 1915,

CHRISTMAS It is scarcely " the old, old wish" that we offer our readers this Christmastide. A Merry Christmas is out of the question, and the ICeiv.Year is dot, likely to be happy, how- ever ardently we may long for the return of "letter days. But we offer our readers the next best wish in the strange conditions in which the world finds itself at a season specially dedicated to the aspiration of man- kind, that there shall be peace and goodwill among all men on earth. It is that we may t contrive to be as happy as circumstances will allow; that we may be content with our lot whatever it may be, if our troubles have not been self-inflicted; ,and that we may cherish the confident hope that before another Christ- mas we may have, with God's help, restored peace to the world, having in the process re- moved for ever that polluter of the world, that menace to peace, and that insult to the Creator of this beautiful Universe, that are concentrated in the fungus exerescence known as German Militarism.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
RECRUITING FIGURES.

In another column we publish an analysis of the result of Lord Derby's appeal to War, ringion men under his scheme. Last week we were able to state, generally, that all the eligible young manhood, married and unmar- ried,. with a small margin of about five per cent., had enrolled themselves in the service of their King and country. This week we give the precise figures, which confirm our last week's generalisations. It was expected that the Government would hae been able this week to give the result of the national canvass as A whole, but so trzmcaslous has

been the work that they have been unable to meet the expectation. As the Prime Minister told the House on Tuesday, Lord Derby's task has been one of stupendous magnitude and complexity, and we must perforce wait a reasonable time for the information. We do know, however, that so numerous were the recruits that Lord Derby brought to the standard, that "they snowed under" his great staff of voluntary workers. We may also assume that this " snowing under," when it is known, will bring cold comfort to our friends, the enemy, who may now expect to have against them a nation voluntarily in arms. We are going straight for the goal, and the German leaders know it, even if they. do manage to deceive their people for the time being.

Of course, this great arthy of potentisil fighters will have to be classified. We shall have to divide them into unmarried eligibles who have been attested, and married eligibles whose offer to enlist is conditional upon the Prime Minister's pledge of single men first." We have already had something to say on this point, and suggested that'compul- sion may be applied to the small body of single men who prefer to be fetched, as they style it. It is suggested that there will_be a supplementary canvass of these defaulters. If compulsion were applied now it would be a very small matter, and could easily be en- forced, the vast majority of men having given their consent to serve. Thatis a-question for the future. :We are 'more immediately con- cerned with the success of the scheme so far as its ‘local results have been revealed. For this success we have to thank a small but enthusiastic body of workers, who, assisted by Messrs. Hayes and Sanders, as joint hon. secretaries, have left no stone unturned to provide a satisfactory end to the only real effort that we have made to vindicate volun- taryism.

COUNCILLOR OSBORNE'S RESIGNATION It will be remembered that when Councillor Osborne's resignation in November sent his colleagues on the Town Council into a state of indignation, we counselled a suspension of judgment on the part of the public until the case for ,the. Savings Bank Management had been made public. This week the annuaJ meeting of the Savings Bank has been held, but the discussion has not carried us much further. On the one hand, the managers contended that they had a right4to make a bargain with an employe not to' tlevote .any part of his time to public duties; on the other. hand it was argued that such an institution as a savings bank, run on democratic lines, has no right to put so drastic a limit upon the freedom and the public spirit of any man. Some of the managers weed with the prin- ciple- involved in the latter irgument and fought for its application three years ago. They now -wept on the other side. A prin- ciple, we thought, remained a principle, in spite of the flow of time or any other con- sideration; but it was deemed otherwise on Tuesday. The most unfOrtunate part of the whole proceeding is that the turn the con- troversy has taken may cause the superficially minded to entertain the idea that Councillor Osborne agreed to terms and then did not honour his part of the bargain. As a matter of fact, he appears to have honoured "the bargain " up to the hilt, resigning his public work unreservedly'. It was his resignation, in fact, that raised the ire of the Council. The discussion could well have been dispensed with, especially in view of the undignified and irrelevant character it often assumed.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OLD FOX.

The Old Fox stood in the winding street; It had smiled at the sun and scoffed at the sleet, And had watched the centuries ebb and flow, And the living come and the dea&men go.

It was built of oak and could stand the storm; It was roofed with thatch to keep folks warm; Its quaint old cellars were filled with beer;' - In its chimney •corner men kept good cheer.

But its comrades vanished one by one Till, at last, deserted, if stood alone; Its doors were closed to the passers-by; Its hearth was empty, its cellars dry.

But some lamented who passed that way. And they cried to the local Vandals, "Stay! The quaint old place to our hearts is dear; We have known and loved it for many a year.

"Our fathers sat by its fires, and theirs; It stood when Cromwell said hi, prayers In another inn On that fatal day When the routed Scotehmen rode this way.

"Its windows watched old "Howard pass, And saw how fair flAtitia was When eggs and butter were sold below, And the horns of the coaches used to blow.

" But its stout old beams still braved the storm, And its wattle and daub still kept men warm; Its leaden lights and windows small Have seen kings rise and empires fall."

• It linked us all to our deathless past, But' doom o'ershadowed its doors at last; Its roof has vanished, its walls are gone, And the great beams followed them one by one.

But they shall not rot in the mist and the rain; Its time-worn timbers shall rise again; 1.-And soon, where the grass and the trees are spread, Like the flowers from the earth, it shall rise from the dead.

ARTHUR BENNETT.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 25, 1915.

this Bank to be discussed at all. The subject was lugged into the discussion and an attack of the most virulent and unjustifiable .char- acter was made. In the course of the attack I came in for my share of the knocks, as usual. While I have to thank the Mayor for having given the contradiction to one state- ment made by an illustrious member of the Council, nevertheless I was said to be up to

the neck in what was called a dishonourable and disgraceful attack. I have not attended any meeting of the Committee of Management, except at the annual meeting, for the last three years. With Mr. Smethurst and others, three "years ago, I came here to fight for an abstract principle—for the right of a man to employ his leisure time in such a manner as he might consider consistent. The,committee were induced to make a concession, which was duly observed and carried through, but cir- cumstances have occurred during the past three years which, to my mind, entire:y alter

the position. The principle -remains ,the same, but a man can contract- himself .out -of his privileges if he chooses to do so; and that is. what has happened. The interest of the Bank is really the only thing we -have to -.consider at all, and we are not influenced by any other motive. We may be wrong in our judgment, and are subject to criticism, • like everybody else. I can speak . without feeling in this matter, because I am one who has 'taken no part or lot whatever in the matter, and I can, therefore, speak with freedom, but I say that the committee, in considering this question solely and exclusively from the point of view of what in their judgment was in the best interests of the Bank, came to the .decision—in view of the fact that the staff had .airt-ady been depleted bt two or .okree members having en- listed-7-that it was 40.bsolutely essential that every .official. should • devote the whole of his time to the interests of :the Institution, -and should not be disturbed,- mentally or physically, by occupation of any other kind except of a recreative nature.

The officials of the Bank were approached on

that understanding, and the whole thing was discussed with Mr. Osborne and others; and the situation was explained to them. Mr. Osborne was given a very handsome advance of salary—(hear, hear)—in consideration—XBo, I believe. At all events, it was such an advance that does not often occur in an Insti- tution of this character. It is not often that a clerk in a Bank gets a, jump of £BO a year for any consideration at all. I know something about bank clerks' salaries. I don't say that Mr. Osborne's is a "soft job," but it is a well- paid one by comparison with similar positions in other Banks. I don't think I shall be wrong if I say it is the best paid appointment of the character I know of. However, the fact remains that after discus- sion with Mr. Osborne, he voluntarily agreed with the arrangement and consented to give ,up all the work he was engaged upon, either in the Council or out, in consideration of this very substantial advance of salary. That took place in May. The committee have fulfilled their part of the contract, and Mr. Osborne has received his proportion of the increased salary, as the salary dates from the time of the arrangement. The time has now come for Mr. Osborne to carry out his part of the con- tract.

Councillor. Roberts proceeded to repudiate a suggestion that he had had anything to do with Councillor Osborne's resignation. "I want to justify myself," he said. "I have a reputation to keep and a reputation that I wish to be re- spected during the few- remaining years that I have to live. . . . I want my vindication, and I have derended myself this morning from these beastly • personal insinuations."

THE RIGHT TO BARGAIN.

The Chairman: I am bound to decide against Mr. Upson' ti resolution, which amounts to a direct negative. Mr. Upson: I am interested only in the principle, and while you have Made this bar- gain I hold that you had no right to do so. Councillor Roberts: That is a question. Mr. Upson: I take it I have a right as a manager to move an amendment? The Chairman: You have a right to move an amendment.

Mr. Smethurst: On a point of orcler—can the matter be raised on the agenda under "any other business," and let the business of the Bank: go on? Cries of "No; let's get it finished at once."

Mr. Upson: I move "That the managers of the Warrington and District Savings Bank, having heard the explanation of the Com- mittee of Management as to the case of an employe of the Bank, who was a member of the Warrington Town Council, express their disapproval of that treatment." Councillor Roberts: It amounts to a question of confidence or no confidence in the manage- ment.

councillor Craik asked whether the Corn.] raittee of Management had a right to make an 1 arrangement with a servant of the Bank. It was a most ridiculous thing. He had only been a member of the committee twelve months, but he could say without fear of contradiction that the arrangement entered into by Mr. Osborne and the other officials was loyally carried out by Elie committee. The committee• had carried out their agreement, but at the last moment• it seemed that Mr. Osborne wanted to have his position improved at the Bank, a hand- some increase given to him, and to retain his position as a member of the Town Council. Ile :the speaker) thought the answer of the Bank which appeared in the newspapers was quite sufficient for the public of Warrington. They had had no signs that the public were not satisfied with the explanation which had been given to them. If they were not satisfied they would have asked for further particulars. It was difficult to see, the move that was going on in the matter that morning. He could say, however, that the committee had loyally carried nut their agreement with Mr. Osborne. The amendment Was seconded by the Bev. H. Ogden, but on being put to the meeting only the proposer and seconder voted for it. A number present did not vote, but the majority of the managers were against the amendment, and it was declared lost. The original resolution, that the report of the Committee of Management be approved, was carried unanimously.

St. Ann's Old Scholars.

The first reunion of St. Ann's old scholars took place last Saturday evening in Monett-Street Schools. About 50 persons sat down to tea, dUr- ing which many stories of youthful /escapades, one dating back nearly fifty years, were related. Mr. Glover, who has been headmaster for about 31 years, filled the duties of chairman very agreeably. After tea asocial went merrily along for •aboui two hours, the following artistes taking part Travis, Messrs. Beswick, Berry, Hardman, P. Rigby aTid W. Rigby. Mr. W. Houghton acted as accompanist. "Auld Lang Syne," which concluded a pleasant even- ing, 'had a fuller and .deeper meaning than on most occasions.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
Transport Waggon On Fire.

• As a steam motor waggon loaded with bales of cotton was passing Hollins Green on 'Satur- day afternoon, a man informed the driver that the cotton was on fire. The driver and his assistant tried to roll the burning bales off, but were only able to remove a few .of the upper ones on to the road. They drove the burning load down the lane near the Old Boathouse; and removed a few more, but were beaten by tne heat and smoke. P.C. Wilson, of Cadishead, mounted his bicycle and telephoned to Warring- ton for assistance, and Superintendent. Turner sent a steamer in charge of Deputy-Superin- tendent H. Cook to the scene. On their arrival they found the load burning furiously. A. supply of water was obtained with some diffi- culty from the Ship Canal, and the engine was got to work just in time to save the waggon and engine, with only slight damage. It was, how- ever, some hours before the cotton was ex- tinguished,, but the firemen, working well. under trying conditionS, succeeaed in saving the bulk from destguetion. - The motor waggon was owned by Mr. -John .Leatherbarrow, Park-road, Salford. Both waggon and cotton were covered by insurance.- It is supposed a cinder from the engine funnel dropped on the tarpaulin cover, burned through, and ignited the cotton.

COUNCIL DEBATE CRITICISED.

The discussion at the annual meeting of the Warrington Town Council concerning the resignation of Mr. Edmund Osborne as a member of the Town Council was referred to at the annual meeting of the Warrington, Runcorn and District Savings Bank, which was held at 'the offiCes of the Bank in Butter- market-street on Tuesday 'morning. In the absence of 'Mr. C. Jackson Holmes, Mr. Josiah Longland was voted to the chair. -The, Trustees present were: Messrs. William Tinsley, Henry -Roberts, J. Rymer -Young, F. A. Frost, G. W.. Joseph, S. C. Beckett, John Pritchard, George Summerfield. and Thomas Wright. The mana- gers present were Messrs. T. P. Bradshaw, William Clegg, J. G. Craik, J. W. Hayward, J. S. Hill, J. Moore Murray, Segar Owen, James Pritchard, James Richardson, William Roberts, J. E. Seeker, S. Percy Silcock, James ilenry Smethurst, John Upson, Edward Wal- lington, the Rev. H. Ogden, the Rev. G. Vale Owen, and the Rev. J. Yonge. Mr. J. W. Hayward moved, and Mr. William Clegg seconded, "that the report of the Com,. mittee of Management be approved, and that this 'meeting acknowledge the valuable services rendered to the Institution by the. gentlemen acting on the committee during the past year, and tender its best thanks to them."

upon salary. This case is far more on a parallel with the Banks than with firms like Messrs. Crosfield's. The question is how far is an employe to try to depart from an ar- rangement to which he is a party. If any of you desire a clerk, you would be justified in naming your terms, and if they are unpalatable to the candidate he can decline them. ,If Mr. Osborne regards himself as an aggrieved—l don't see how he can be, as he has an extremely handsome salary, and it is one of the softest jobs in Warrington—if he is aggrieved, the remedy is in his own hands. If he finds his time • more valuable than we pay him for it, he is at liberty to resign. And I assume that the committee would be able to dispense with his services. I do object to this manifest breach of contract; it is a distinct endeavour to get out of an arrangement. It comes to this: Has this Bank, which has been a suececs for years and years, to go on its own way to success, or should it degenerate to a committee of the Town Council?

===== 0002601_19151225_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. UPSON ASKS A QUESTION.

As -the Chairman was about to put the reso- lution to the meeting, Mr. John 'Ellison said: " There is-a question in: the minds of a great many- here this morning. A very- strong attack has been made iipon this Institution, or, rather, upon those who are responsible for its management, and I should like .to know whether the Committee of Management has any information to give them as to what ar- rangements have ,been. made with an employe of the Bank and publiC services. I don't want to raise the whole 'question, or to enter upon a discussion, but 'I think it is due from the Committee of Management that we should ,have some information as to what is to be done." Mr. William Roberts: I think that after the attack at he Council Meeting the reply Of the Committee of _Management, which ap- peared in the nesyslialiers, was very dignified, and I think it was a reply which was emin- ently satisfactory to those who have the welfare of the Bank at -heart. So far as I am personally concerned, I think that. nothing fur- ther is required from here, but. something is required from elsewhere. We ought/ to have some explanation from the Mayor. of Warring- ton for what / consider a- very ,unwarrantable attack upon the Committee' of Management, and unless something of that sort is to be made ,here this morning, I think, ~that the most dignified course for the managers and com- mittee of this Bank is to say nothing further about the matter. I think that the answer made through the medium of the Press was the only answer that -was required; I think it was a complete answer, to the charges.

Mr. Upson: I don't think we are all so easily satisfied as Mr. Roberts. I think that we, as a body, ought to have a statement from you (the Chairman), or from some authoritative person, as to the position at the moment. The answer of the trustees ,or Committee of Management, through the Press, is no answer to this body. We are not puppets, simply ;to come•bere once a year to approve of everything that has been prepared by the Board and to move resolutions as typewritten -by the officials of this Institu- tion: I am not at all satisfied, as a manager of this Bank, to receive a Press answer to a charge' that has been made against this Insti- tution. I think it is due to us, as managers, to receive a statement as to the position of this employe. AN ARRANGEMENT MADE.

The Chairman: It might be convenient if I say a word; the position is precisely as it was. The employe made an arrangement with the nianagers, and that -,arrangement is still in force. The managers made the arrangement in what they considered the best interests of the Bank, and for the welfare of the Bank. As the . paragraph in the paper stated, the arrange- ment was a perfectly friendly one on both sides.

The Rev. H. Ogden said that there did not -seem to be any: remuneration attached to the work of the Town Council. He thought that an employe of, 'Mr. Osbornes's ability would cat the glow over the Bank by the ability that he displayed in conducting the affairs of the Council. Certainly, as one of the managers, he (the speaker) failed to understand the rea- son why this employe was refused permission to give his leisure time to the work of the Council. He gathered 'that the arrangement was that an employe should not take up any remunerative work in his spare time. He was not aware that an employe would not be allowed- to do any public work in his leisure time. It seemed to him that it would be quite as logical to deny a man the right to use, his leisure time in some form of pleasure, amuse- ment, or recreation, as to deny him the right to work on the Town Council.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
A QUESTION OF LIBERTY.

Mr. T. P. Bradshaw: As. a representative .of the Friendly Societies, who have a considerable amount of money in the-Savings Bank, I per- sonally have no concern as to what the Mayor of Warrington said at the Council meeting, but I eam concerned that.. the 'ratepayers of this :town should; be ..deprived..of the services of one of their best men by a iesOlution of this Bank. Firms in the town not only. allowed their men to attend Council meetings, but offered them facilities. Messrs. JOseph Crosfield and Sons (who had sent more workmen to the Colours than any other firm in the town), when there was a vacancy ,in the. Whitecross Ward, and Mr. Dale was approached, and through the kind services of Mr. Henry Roberts, Metsrs. Crosfield unhesitatingly, gave him ,permission. Councillor H. Roberts: He asked . for per- mission. .

Mr. Bradshaw: But. I understand you went out of Your way to get him that permission. Mr. -Clegg, the representative of the Friendly Societies on the Education Committee, received permission from the same firm. Messrs. Walker have granted Mn. Fulton- Smith similar privileges; and the same have been granted by Rylands's. We now find that one of our own servants cannot attend meet- ings in his own time. He attended the cora- mittee meetings on `Thursday afternoons and the Council meetings at six o'clock in the evening, when the Bank was closed. And now, simply because of an increase in salaries, we are told that it is necessary for Mr. Osborne to retire from the Town Council. Are you going to take away his liberty and his freedom? It is an admitted fact that the late Alderman Davies, your own actuary, was a mayor and alderman, but now that it is a-cashier that is concerned, I suppose that it is "infra dig." If Mr. Osborne had been the actuary instead of cashier, very probably nothing. would have been said. I do object to an Committee of Management tying down any man who is doing what he can in the interests of his own town in his own time. As a representative of the Friendly Societies, I protest.

MR. RYMER YOUNG DEFINES THE ISSUE.

Mr. J. Rymer Young: Mr. Bradshaw did not enumerate the various Banks of the town, and the Banks are better parallels than the firms that he has mentioned. 'Parr's Bank, and the other large Banks, not only do they stipulate as to whether their employes should take part in Town Council work or, in politics, but they went further and told 'them when they could marry. That arrangement depended, of course,

===== 0002601_19151225_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
NOT AGGRIEVED.

The Rev. H. Ogden: I don't know if Mr. Osborne is an aggrieved person—LA Voice: "He iS.")—but I happened to mention the matter to him. He refused to discuss it, and he re- quires credit for that. I think it is most deplorable at this public Board that it should be said that we have an employe who has a soft job. (Hear, hear.) I regret deeply that• at a meeting of such highly-honoured gentlemen such kind of phrasing should be used, or in any measure endorsed. What- ever the agreement was,. I think it was a finan- cial one—that no financial work should be under- taken after /the uSual business hours. That is what I understood, but what I would like to ask is whether an employe, who is serving the town so well, is acting in a manner inimical to the interests of the Bank or otherwise by attending the meetings of the Town Council? I take it. with all respect to the greater banks, men as Parr's, that the chief contributors in our case are working people--the people of small -neans— and I should take it that if those working folk have confidence in an employe who is serving the town, it would rather increase than go against the welfare of the bank.

The Chairman interrupted, saying that he thought Mr. Ogden had risen to refer to a point mentioned by another speaker. He could not make a second speech.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
APPEAL; BY THE MAYOR.

Mr. Upson: I would like to ask a question. Is it a fact that an appeal has been made for this employe—l raise the question on principle; I have not seen Mr. Osborne to speak to for two months—is it a fact that an appeal has been made that he Should be allowed to serve ut his unexpired term of ten months, and, if so, has it been refused? • Mr. J. H. Smethurst: I should like to have the privilege of replying to Mr. Upson. The appeal has been through me, so far as regards the re-consideration of the position of Mr. Osborne. The appeal was made by his Worship the Mayor, and his letter was considered this niorning at a special meeting of the Board of Management. But in dealing with the 'matter that has been raised, I would like to say that, three years 'ago I was one who, with others, strongly objected to the idea that a man's private time should be taken up with the services of the Bank. There was an objection from certain sources that Mr. Osborne should not be allowed to take a position as a member of the Town Council, if though he used only his spare time. I fought for the principle at the time, and Mr. Osborne Was allowed to continue in his position at the Bank and to become a member of the Town Council. But there is a right way and a wrong way even in starting a matter like this. One would have thought that he would have asked per- mission to become a member of the Town Council. When we came to the matter of the rearrangement of the duties at the Bank, so far as the question of hours was concerned, it was considered most desirable that the whole of the time of the various members of the staff should be devoted to the interests of tile Baak. It was considered how far .this Bank resembled a Joint Stock Bank. There was not a big difference in the work entailed. Mr. Rymer Young has mentioned that no official of a Joint Stock Bank is allowed to take any part in public matters. We understand that Mr. Osborne is a man of marked ability, but, apart from any question of ability, the managers of the Bank must have the right to manage their own affairs. Mr. Smethurst went on to say that the managers had carefully considered the matter and had concluded that the officials at the Banks should be in the same position as the officials of a Joint Stock Bank. Mr. Osborne was brought into the discussion and he agreed to the terms we suggested as being fit and proper. He was asked right out, and surely Mr. Osborne is able to take care ,of himself and to see that nothing unfair to him is done. There is no desire to drive him from his position on the Town Council. It is simply • this: Mr. Osborne has a position in this Bank which he considers valuable enough to hold on the monetary side, hut the men who have given him the position have the right to see— as representing the depositors who put money into the Bank—that they get full value for that money. It is unfortunate for this reason that Mr. Osborne is unable to continue on the Town Council. With regard to the Mayor's appeal, I brought that appeal before the managing body this morning, and if the speech of the Mayor had not been delivered in the Town Council in the manner that it was, that appeal would have received, as it received this morning, the greatest possible consideration from the Committee of Management. But they have Come to the conclusion, as the Chair- man has said, that they must manage the affairs of the Bank themselves.

GOVERMENT AND THRIFT.

Mr. E. Osborne's Suggestions To Encourage Working-Class Savings.

The following letter appeared in the "Manchester Guardian" a few days ago:—

GOVERNMENT AND THRIFT. The Trustee Savings Banks. Sir,—The Government have never giveh the attention to thrift that it deserves, and now that the fact is being emphasised they are likely to go to the other extreme and thrust forward hastily conceived schemes which will tax the general resources of the country un- duly. Regarded from a national point of view, whatever may have been the experience of savings banks in the past, there can be no question as to what is required of them and for them at the present time. On the 26th June I ventured to address a letter to the Chancellor of the Exchesuer ask- ing for an expression of opinion regarding the usefulness of trustee savings banks in the coming thrift campaign, and mentioned that, as there appeared to be some uncertainty among depositors as to what their attitude should be towards the new War Loan, a statement from him would clear the air and at the same time give those of us who are interested in the work a lead in the matter. The enlightening reply was as follows: ' Treasury Chambers, Whitehall, S.W., sth July, 1915. Dear Sir,--I am desired by the Chancellor of the Exchequer to say that His Majesty's Government have no desire to prevent de- positors in the Post Office Savings Bank from transferring their deposits to the War Loan if they wish to do so. It is, however, obvious, since the Government has to find the money to repay the deposits, that such transfers do not increase the available re- sources of the country for carrying on the war. They have, therefore, no desire to encourage or discourage withdrawals.—Yours faithfully, E. Osborne, Esq. H. P. ITsmisrox All through the Government have adopted a policy of "laissez-faire." It will be observed that, as usual, trustee savings banks are not even mentioned. Ordinary depositors in eavings banks are not concerned with buying and selling stocks, and 1 any further scheme for borrowing which is submitted ought not to place any portion of their loan in jeopardy. What concerns them most is that for every stl paid in they will receive £1 back, plus a reasonable rate of in- terest. It should also be recognised that in • many instances they cannot manage to lie out of their money for twenty years and also can- not afford to lose by a forced sale. The present rate of interest allowed to de- positors is 21 per cent., and, the principal, or any portion of it, is payable on demand or a week's notice, according to the amount of the withdrawal. The Treasury is the savings banks' "banker," in strict accordance with the principle laid down in 1861 by Mr. Glad- stone, although so long ago as 1817 the Treasury laud been authorised, to receive savings banks' funds. The Government have, therefore, the I use of the millions deposited with them, for 'which they allow the hank e 21 per cent, in- terest. They cannot be charged with extrava- ,gance in„ that respect. 1 I beg to .submit briefly the following work- able suggestions as applicable to savings , banks : • (1) That, without issuing bonds, it should be possible to adopt a, scheme to encobrage further savings .by giving-• increased interest, fixed according to the period for which money is deposited. (2) That the limit, £5O per annum and £2OO in all, be increased. (3) That the regulation that depositors must have £5O in the ordinary department before they can make deposits in "special investments" be altered and the amount re- duced to £2O. (4) That "war loans" be treated as are "special investments." All the money thus deposited to be invested with the Treasury for that purpose. • (5) That those savings banks which have , not a special investment department be per- mitted to open one for the purposes of sug- gestion (4). (6) That a special "War Loan" bank pass- book be issued. [I have based my suggestions on the fact that savings banks will have to continue their use- fulness long after the war has been brought to a successful issue, and any unnecessary dis- turbing element will react in a manner detri- mental to the interests of thrift.—Yours, &c,, EDMUND OSBORNE. i 22, Bold-street, Warrington, December sth.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
"WISE AND PRACTICAL."

These suggestions have met with wide ap- proval. An who is serving on the cora- -31.P mittee appointed ~ by the Treasuri to consider the scheme for popularising the new loan among the working classes, has written to Mr. Osborne, saying:--

"I was very pleased to hear from you again and have read with interest your letter in the 'Manchester Guardian.' The suggestions con- tained therein appeal to me as being wise and practical, and I will see that they are consid- ered by the committee on which I have been appointed by the Treasury. ' If you have any further suggestions of a similar kind in mind I shall be glad to hear, from you in the patter."

Raddon Court.

An interesting innovation took place on Thursday at this institution, for a competition was held, in which prizes were presented for the best and most tastefully decoratel 'wards, Mrs. Segar Owen and Miss Lucy Broadbent being the judges. Paper flowers and garlands made by the patients added colour to the scene, and a decided feature in one of the wards was a model lake lit by electricity. Another fea- ture was a room decorated in red, white and blue, whilst another had over each bed regi- mental badges cut in paper. The patients, exhibiting wonderful skill, made the judging no easy matter. -

At liaddon Court, a few days ago, there took place a draw for a splendid model yacht, pre- sented by a lady whose son is at present serving in the Navy. The fortunate prize-winner was Miss Whitta- ker, and at the close Mrs. Booth, who had charge of the arrangements, handed a cheque for .C 8 Us. 9d. being the proceeds to Miss Broadbent.

A draw which took place at Raddon Court Hospital on Wednesday for two pieces of work done by Miss Amy Grounds, resulted in Mrs. Porter and Mr. Wood, both of Latchford, win- ning. The proceeds were spent in providing a large Christmas cake for the barracks and other presents.

Overcrowded Schools.

At a recent meeting of the School Attend- ance Sub-committee of the Education Com- mittee a, letter was read from the Board of Education with reference to the overcrowding of certain schools, and instructions were given to the Director as to the reply to be sent thereto. It was resolved that the consideration of the provision of temporary premises to re- lieve the overcrowding should be referred to the Sites and Buildings Committee. This committee has decided to leave the matter in the hands of the Chairman and the Director for investigation and report.

Christmas Services at Golborne-street.

On Sunday, at the Golborne-street Baptist Church, Christmas and carol services were held. At the morning service the hymns were " Christians, Awake," "While Shepherds Watched Their Flocks" and "Hark the Herald Angels Sing," In the afternoon a carol service was held in the schoolroom, when a most interesting address was given to the chil- dren by Mrs. Elliott. Selections from "The Messiah" were rendered by well-known local singers at the evening service. The soprano selections were ably sung by Miss Olive Dale, and Miss Maud Jones was a. very capable con- tralto. Mr. A. Pearce was the bass singer, and Mr. J. H. Markham, who was in kildier's uniform, rendered the tenor solos.

A Belgian Lecturer.

Under the auspices of St. Benedict's T.T.M.S. an illustrated lecture will be given in the Hip- podrome (by kind permission of Mr. Frank Macnaghten), entitled "The Holy Land and Egypt," by the Right Rev. Abbot Janssens, 0.5.8. (a distinguished Belgian), on Sunday. The proceeds will be in aid of St. Benedict's. The lecturer has frequently travelled in the Holy Land and the Near East, and is fully con- versant with the country. The slides are from his own photographs. At the present time, when the Holy Land is in the hands of the Turks, whose forces are mobilised in Jerusalem and in the provinces, "where Jesus Christ constantly teached," the lecture should be interesting and instructive.

A final meeting of the Warrington Paella- amentary Recruiting Committee was held at the .Recruiting Office, Horsemarket-street, on Wcgi- -nesday. His Worship the Mayor (Alderman . Peter Peacock) presided, and there were also _present Messrs. C. E. Parker, J. T. Locker, Edwin Bradshaw, 0. H. Smith, W. Bradbury, -James Ball and the Hon. Secretaries (Messrs. E. A. Sanders and H. Hayes). _ _ _

Many of the men under item 13, 664 single and 1,138 married, who at first refused to listen to the arguments of the canvassers, have since, we are glad to say, reconsidered the question, and have attested under the scheme. Many of these men have business and family reasons, but do not feel .inclined "to run the risk" of trusting their reasons to the Tribunals set up. Want Compulsion.

One feature of the camraign has been the 13nmber (comparatively small) of men who positively decline to come in under any circum- stances, and a special report as to these has been sent up to headquarters at the request of the Parliamentary Recruiting Committee. This gallant band of men and women who undertook this great work for the nation, and whose numbers might reasonably have been expected to have been larger, can at least feel they have done their bit in this tre- mendous effort to preserve for the nation the voluntary system.

LORD DERBY'S THANKS.

A Sacrifice For Which. Success Gives Ample Reward.

The following letter, received from Lord Derby, was read:— _

War Office, Dec. 15, 1915.

Dear Sir,—l have the honour to forward you a resolution which was unanimously passed at a joint committee meeting of the Parliamentary Recruiting Committee and the Labour Recruit- ing Committee.

I would ask you to allow me to supplement the resolution with a few words of personal thanks. It is impossible for me to write, as I could have wished to do, to every canvasser and worker throughout the United Kingdom, but I would ask your committee to allow me. through you, to tender to one and all my most grateful thanks for the assistance they have given to my scheme of recruiting. I know what sacrifice of time and convenience all have made, but I hope that the result of the canvass will prove a suffi- cient reward to them for their patriotic efforts. Yours sincerely, DERBY.

APPEALS.

The committee desire to draw the attention of those in the borough of 'Warrington who have attested, and wish to appeal, to the following.:— The Proclamation calling up Groups 2,3, 4, and 5, points out that if any recruits do not receive directions within 30 days Where and when to report themselves, and if they have changed their address since the date of their attestation, they should report by letter to the Recruiting Officer at the Recruiting Office where they were attested, or to the Office, the address of which is entered on their card, Army Form W 3194. The necessary directions as to joining will then be given. • Recruits affected are to present themselves for service on January O. Each man will have a notice paper, informing him as to the date, time and place at which he is to report himself. Fourteen days' notice will be given. If no noti.!e is received, 'however, the Proclamation is aufil- cient notice.

Claims to be placed in a later group have to be submitted to the Clerk to the Tribunal, Mr. A. T. Hallaway, Deputy Town Clerk, and Claim Forms must be obtained from him and filled in and returned within ten days from the date of the Proclamation, that is, on or before the 30th December.

The claims may be made by a man on grounds personal to himself, or (with the consent of the man) by his employer, on the ground that the man is indispensable to his civil employment.

THE HURAL LOCAL TRIBUNAL.

We understand that the Military Authorities have appointed Mr. W. Furness ' honorary recruiting • officer—to . represent them on the above Tribunal—the Rural District including the following townships:—

Burtonwood, Cuerdley, Sankey, Houghton, MAdieton and Arbury, Penketh, Poulton-with-Fearnhead. RiNton-with-Glazebrook, Southworth-with-Croft, Woolston-wth-lfartinseroft.

KII;KI ARMLETS.

Distritmlion (:onthminti At The Dri

Hall.

The distribution of the khaki armlets to the men who have, attested under Lord Derby's scheme commenced last week-end, and already the armlets are fairly conspicuous about the town. The distribution will be continued next Wednesday and Thursday from 3to 9 p.m. The groups for Wednesday are Nos. 14, 15, 16, 17 and 18, and on Thursday they are Nos. 19, 20, 21, 22 and 23.

Last week-end Groups 2,3, 4 and 5 were warned that they would be called up in January. The reason given for this step was to enable the tribunals to get to work, and it already seems that these bodies will have their hands full, for close on 100 applications for exemption from military service have been made by men in these four groups during the past week.

The result of the canvass justifies the state- ment that no man need say he has not had a reasonable and fair chance of serving his country under the voluntary system. "A MEAN, WICKED LIBEL."

An important speech was delivered by Mr. J. H. Thomas, the Labour M.P. for Derby, at a gathering of the National Union of Railway- men, held at the Co-operative Hall, Warring- ton, on Sunday afternoon. The Mayor Alderman Peter Peacock) pre- sided over a large audience.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
A SORT OF EVIL. GENIUS.

Mr. Thomas said that to-day, he was looked upon as a sort of evil genius, but he thought that as people looked at him they would not , think that he was as harmful as they had been led to believe. (Laughter.) Last Saturday, (December 11th), was the con- cluding day of what was known as Lord. Derby!s scheme. The object of that scheme was to enable the Government to have a regular sufficiency of men and to enable the Govern- ment to know exactly what the people of the country were prepared to do. He was going to submit that whatever the result # of the scheme might be, the first thing it had proved was that the overwhelming mass of the people of this country were prepared to make sacrifices order to bring the war to a successful con- clusion. (Applause.) Secondly, he was going to claim that it was an absolute vindication of all. that they claimed for the voluntary sacrifice of the men and women of this country. (A Voice: " Question ?") . Well, continued Mr. Thomas, I am going to get to close quarters at once. (The same Voice: "Hear, hear.") " I am going to submit that in addition,_ to the claim I have made it is a justification and a tribnte to the manner in which the Labour forces of this country have rallied to the suppcirt of the country. (Hear, hear.) In this connection I have no hesitation in saying that no small credit is due to the man- ner in which Lord Derby has worked. He is a !convinced supporter of what is called national service and I, of course, disagree with him, but , it is due to him, and I should be wanting in my duty if I did not say it, to say that he has I worked wholeheartedly, devotedly, and without ! prejudice in order to make the voluntary system an absolute succeas:,:, (Hear; hear.) Keeping these points in Mind. I want to remind von that in connection with this scheme, the Prime Minister made a speech on November 2nd. In that speech .he clearly! indicated that, although he himself was in favour of the volun- tary system—although he believed that volun- taryism would meet every requirement of the Government—yet he did not hesitate to say that he would be prepared under certain cireurn- stances to introduce some other system. And he implied that under certain circumstances he would make the first class on what were called single men. I think lam justified saying that one .of the Primary conditions that he attached to that pledge, was the absolute and essential necessity of maintaining the unity of the nation. !

===== 0002601_19151225_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PdEMIER AND HIS PLEDGE.

What after all we really mean by single men? I think a fair interpretation of the speech was that the burden of the war, the responsi- bility of fighting, should be borne by those with least home responsibility. I think that is a fair and just interpretation of the meaning of the claim for single men. I am going to be quite frank and say that the pledge having been given, hundreds of , thousands of married men have attested on the understanding that the pledge would be fulfilled. I think I should hr. wanting in my duty and indeed praying with the facts, if I did not boldly arid, frankly admit that position. Having admitted that position I regret to see that on Saturday the public were told that—notwithstanding the pledge having been made—forty Members of Parliament, my- self included, waited upon the Prime Minister in order to peisuade him to break the pl&lge that he had made. I say to you, on behalf ,of myself, 'and I believe on behalf of every mem- ber of that deputation, that such a suggestion is a mean, wicked libel on every one of us. (Ap- plause.) But there is something far more im- portant behind the suggestion. There is some- thing behind the spenes in this matter, for what does it really mean? The suggestion meant that the Prime Minister of the country was so unstable that his word could not he relied upon. I say without fear of contradiction that there is a grave responsibility attached to any section of the Press or public who dares to make such a suggestion. What effect must it have on the foreign and neutral nations? At this stage another interruption occurred at , the back of the hall. and Mr. Thomas warned

the interrupter that if be interfered again he would be asked to leave the meeting.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ATTEMPT TO DISCREDIT THE PRIME MINISTER.

continuing, Mr. Thomas said, I want to sub- mit that this suggestion is one more attempt to discredit the Prime Minister. It is a back door method of implying that he cannot be, trusted. It is one more attempt to cause dis- unity in this country. I say on oehalf of the working classes, on behalf of the men who are giving their life's blood, on behalf of the women who are giving their sons, that I am going to fight against any methods that are going to break up the nation at such a time as this. (Loud applause.) Don't get behind this fent men, that behind this movement are the people who want conscription, not for the purpose of winning this war, but for (the remainder of the sentence was lost in a great outburst of applause.) Not because voluntaryism has failed —because it has not—but because they want to establish in this country'militarism and all that it means. I have striven and fought for unity because I am satisfied that we cannot fight each other-and fight the common enemy at the same time; because Irealise that the issues involvei in this war are So fundamental, so far-reaching, so vital to the .future of liberty and progress that I want to see us bring this war to a suc- cessful conclusion as a united people. If one took notice of all the slander and gossip that one heard, one could make some interesting and lively speeches in Parliament. But the one test that I always applied was " Would it heln to wage the war successfully, and what would be the effect on the brave men fighting in the trenches?" That should be the test for every public man to-day. I have refrained from taking any course that is likely to div4,l' •! People and to encourage the enemy. But if these people think that they are going ,to stampede the position; if they think that they are going to exploit the patriotism of the people in this way, then let me assure them that they are going to stet a rude shock. (Applause.) For T at least will be prepared to take my stand and lead the workers against them. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19151225_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
JUSTICE FOR THE SINGLE MEN.

The pledge having been given to the married men, must certainly be carried out. But if justice and fair play is to be given to the married men, is not justice and fair play to be given to the single men ?e In other words, why create any barrier of class, or of anything else? I say emphatically that before you have the right to call any man a " slacker" or a "coward," you are morally bound to prove your case. (Loud applause.) Therefore let me apply the test. Is the man who has got a widowed mother and who feels a moral obliga- tion to do justice to her, a coward? (Cries of "No.") Is the man who has younger brothers and sisters and who has lost his father and mother a slacker? (Cries, of "No.") Then let us consider this question in its true light. Are the men in " starred " trades, who have said to themselves that they are doing better work by making munitions instead of going to fight to be branded as cowards and slackers? (" No.") I know one railway company that did not allow a solitary man to attest. Are these men to be condemned? (" No.") All these things I put to you to show that when we con- sider these things we have got to consider them free-from passion and free from prejudice. All these things go to prove the claptrap that is used on this subject. I say fearlessly that every call that has yet been made has been responded to. I believe that every call that will be made will be equally responded to.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====

was born January 27th, 1892, at -Florence, I Massachusetts, US., and came to England with his parents in .March, 1901. He was 23 years of age last January, had , just finished his College education, and was making arrangements for a situation, when, the war began. Having had three years' training in the Officers' Training Corps, like so many of his fellow-students he immediately applied for a commission and was gazetted Second Lieutenant to the Essex Regiment in October, 1914, and went into training immediately. ON HONOUR'S SCROLL.

WOUNDED.

RIFLEMAN H. NURSE.

Shrapnel Wound In The Side, But Progressing Favourably.

Mr. and Mrs. Nurse, who reside at 165, Bew- sey-road, received news on Sunday, through the medium of a field postcard, to the effect that one of their three soldier-sons, viz., Rifleman Harry Nurse, of the King's Royal Rifles, had been wounded with shrapnel in his left side. Later, writing from the 11th General Hospital, Rifleman Nurse assured his parents that the wound was only a slight one, and that they were not to worry. He added `,` I am really in abed."

Rifleman Nurse, who is 25 yeais of age, was educated at the Silver-street, School, and was formerly employed by Messrs. Jos. Crosfield and Sons Ltd. He enlisted in February, and has been at the Front since June. He is a noted baritone singer. Both his other brothers are

PR.USEWORTHY RECORD.

Belgians in Belgium.

As we have on several occas-ions stated there is to be an Envelope Collection in Warrington and the surrounding districts at the invitation of the Mayor on Christmas Day on behalf of those who are destitute in Belgium.. The col- lection is entirely an undenominational affair as the composition of the committee in charge of the arrangements will show. This com;. mittee consists of the Rector, chairman; the Rev. H. It is asked that collectors will keep strictly to the streets allotted to them and so prevent the possibility of any overlapping in the mat- ter of calls.

Black inn night of November. The atmosphere stifled.

Starless, and still Save for the petulant sob of a car on the high- way .

Cresting the hill.

Turretted towers and dark domes in solemnity splendid Loom like the sky. Blackness envelops this way where we wantonly wended; _ _

Nothing is nigh.

Flickers; a flash; then broad light, the foul pitchiness piercing— Regions untrod. Hail, rigid ray! 'Tie the eye of a purified England Shining to God. Garrisontown, J.

HIS COLONEL'S PRAISE. Could Not Pay His Memory Any Higher

Tribute.

The Colonel Commanding the late Lieutenant Bunting's regiment writing, to his home, says: Dear Sir,—l have taken your address from

the receipt for a registered Packet I found in Mr. R. R. Bunting's pack. As I could find no other clue to any relation, I will write to you, and I don't even know whether you are a brother .or perhaps his father. I have made it a rule to write personally to relations of offi- cers who are killed in action, and I am sure you will understand how .difficult it is, some- times, to express one's feelings. As for the story of how Lieutenant Bunting died, it is easy to tell. He. died, as so many have, .v.ry bravely leading his platoon. .I cannot pay his ,memory any higher compliment.. Personally, he always seemed to me to do his work very quietly, but very efficiently, and I had a great opinion of him. . .

===== 0002601_19151225_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
Men Liked and Fotlowed Him.

"His men liked him, as was evidenced by the way they followed him; the longer I serve, the more am I impressed by the fact that soldiers will not follow (as his men did) an officer they do not like and respect. His body was brought in and taken to a Nullab called 'Y Nullah,' and then buried yesterday (Saturday, 7th). A cross, similar to those marking the graves of all Essex officers, .marks the grave, and the _ _ _ _

burial service was read by a chaplain. I am only sorry I was unable to attend, but the regiment was still in the trenches. His. watch, notebook, revolver (and a Colt automatic pistol he picked up) and one or two small articles are being sent home, together with his valise. Any letters that come I will also return. Par- cels containing consumable articles I have usually given to the men of the officers' platoon. In conclusion, will you please accept and convey to his nearest relations my sincerest sympathy in your and their loss."

===== 0002601_19151225_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
A TERRIBLE POSITION.

A friend, writing to Mr. Bunting, says that in a general advance Lieutenant Bunting's regiment carried the trenches they were sent to take, but the battalions on either side failed. In consequence the Turks, when morning came, were able to enfilade the regiment from both ends. "It must have been a terrible time,' says the letter. "The O.C. says he never wishes for better officers and spoke very highly of Russel."

===== 0002601_19151225_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GENERAL'S PRAISE.

Sir lan Hamilton congratulated the division upon the splendid attack ": which more than upheld the best traditions of the distinguished regiments of which. it was composed.. . • Through the entanglements they swept. north- ward, clearing our left of the enemy for a full thousand yards. Heavily counter-attacked at night, they killed or captured every Turk who had penetrated their incomplete defences and to-day stand possessed of every yard they had so hardly gained?'

===== 0002601_19151225_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
A SUCCESSFUL STUDENT.

Lieutenant Bunting was one of the most suc- cessful pupils the Secondary School has had; In 1912 he obtained his Intermediate B.Sc., London University, and, in 1913 he graduated B.Sc. with Honours in Physics. He is the third "old boy" of the school to lay down his life for his country—the other two being Victor IL Fletcher, of the 4th Soteth Lancashire Regiment. and Harold Fryer, of the King's Own Scottish Borderers.

CHILD WAR VICTIMS.

Presents For Them From Town Officers And Teachers.

The municipal officers—including the teachers in the Warrington schools—are responsible for a very pleasing little incident that is taking place to-day. Carefully chosen presents are being given to children under 14 years of age whose fathers (or elder brothers, if they were the sole support of the family) have been killed in the war.

• The donors thought that in this little way they could show, in some measure at least, their appreciation of the patriotism and self-sacrific, of those of their fellow-townsmen who have given their lives for their country.

TERRITORIAL CONCERT.

Third-41h And Third-sth South Lanea- shires Entertain Comrades.

Members of the 3/ 4th Battalion, South Lanca- shire Regiment (Warrington Territorials) in conjunction with the 3/sth (Rifle) Battalion (St. Helens Territorials), stationed in North-West Lancashire, gave a highly enjoyable concert for the benefit of their comrades on Monday even- ing, in the C.E.T.S. Recreation Room, Redman's Cafe. The entertainment was well-attended, and the " Tommies " were evidently delighted with the efforts" of the artistes. The command- ing officers of both battalions (Major F. R. Monks, of the 3/4th, and Major L. Lackland of the 3/sth), accompanied by other officers, were present. • The Arst item was the song "Coo, sing to me;" by Rifleman Foster, a promising young vocalist, who was loudly applauded. Rifleman Foster apparently judged the taste of• his audience well, for his other songs, "The magic of your smile " and "Sunshine of your smile •'" met with equal success. Sergeant R. F. Ander- ton and Lance-Corporal A. Bailey gave a..very sympathetic and harmonious rendering of "The moon hath raised her lamp." Sergeant Ander- ton also contributed "Sons of the Motherland" in rousing style, while Lance-Corporal Bailey entered splendidly into the spirit of "The trumpeter." These two artistes, together with Sergeant Thomson and Lance-Corporal E. Spruce, combined admirably in the quartet "Sweet and low." Sergeant Thomson also ren- dered "The veteran's song" with • success. Private Harper gave a cornet solo (polka) with distinct skill, and responded to an encore with "The sunshine of your smile." Second-Lieutenant G. R. Crowther struck a topical note with his burlesque on the vagaries of the "Sergeant-Instructor at Maidstone," and the amusing parody "My little wet home in the trench." He was enthusiastically recalled. Lieutenant Challen proved himself the possessor of a splendid voice, in his song "Up from Zummerzet," and was accorded a warm recep- tion.

VAS/ aa• The hit of the evening in the humorous line was generally voted to be the burlesque by Private Reid. His parody on Napoleon at Waterloo evoked incessant laughter, while his ' ditty "Ha! ha! ha!" and (as an encore) his impersonation of George Formby in "They're all good lads" fairly convulsed the audience. Gleave gave a laughable impersonation of Charlie Chaplin. Captain C. H. Clarke and Rifleman Peace lent efficient service as accompanists. The concert was organised by Captain Clarke Land Lieutenant Challen.

CHRISTMAS FESTIVITIES.

How To-morrow Will Be Spent In Our Institutions.

A RIGHT ROYAL TIME AT ORFORD BARRACKS.

" Halt 1 Who goes there?"

Through the -g-loom of night the challenge rang out with emphasis and I, in reply, answered "Friend." _

" Pass friend," and I was in the courtyard of Orford Barracks. A few soldiers were being put through a preliminary drill, and a little distance away a regimental band played martial airs. Round about one would not have thought that Christmas was nigh, judging from outside appearances, yet once within the large dining hall everything was changed. I had as my escort Regimental-Sergeant-Major A. Quick, ponderous in size, with the full stamp of an Army officer in his bearing and manner. At the entrance to the banqueting chamber stood a baker's cart, from which the soldiers un- loaded the loaves. Hand to hand, half a dozen at a time, and on to the storehouse! "The first part of the Christmas fare arrived?" I said to the Regimental-Sergeant- Major. and we shall want it all, my friend," said he with a smile. " ' Tommy ' can shift some." Looking around the spacious hall, one noticed at once the splendid and artistic decorations there. Clusters of holly and mistle- toe and thousands of festoons, and upon the walls crests of different regiments. Hung from the ceilings were the flags of the Allies, and amidst all the warm glow of electric lights I learned that the decorations had been under the control of my worthy host, together with Quartermaster-Sergeant Smith, 12th South Lancs. Regiment; Sergeant-Instructor Dreuker. Sergeant Woolley, Sergeant 3iannerang, and Privates Jones and Doward. "You seem to be preparing for a good time this festive season," I ventured to remark. "Sure," answered my escort, "the boys will see to that, and We are giving them a right royal time too. for Christmas Day in the Army is one of the jolliest holidays we have. Good fare also, which includes turkeys, roast joints, boiled hams, vegetables, fruits, sweets and beverages. The 'tuck in' commences at 1 p.m. sharp and lasts until 2. Then the lads are relieved until five o'clock. Tea from that hour until six, after which we are to have a concert by men of the battalion and depot for two hours. Immediately afterwards we shall make merry with dancing, at which friends of he troops will be present. Yes! we shall have a good Christmas! The regimental band is to ,play special and appropriate music, and at the close the six hundred men will all join in sing- ing the song 'Keep the home fires burning.' I suppose, after the singing of the National Anthem, ' Tommy ' and his comrades will be ready for their night's repose unless it is that the plum pudding and other fare remind him of the previous few hours." When I left Regimental-Sergeant-Major Quick it was with feelings of delight, for I thought that every man at present stationed within the grim walls had in the near future a task, stern and relentless, from which he would not shirk.

This is how the boys in khaki at Orford are :o spend to-morrow.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
PEACE AND PLENTY AT THE LORD DERBY HOSPITAL.

Rain was falling and over all a mist, cheerless and melancholy. The roads were veritably quagmires, and along the gutters of the thoroughfares which I traversed were puddles of water. Eventually I reached my destination, which was the Lord Derby Military Hospital, Winwick, where at present over a thousand of bruised warriors are resting after their labours from fields afar. The cleanly wards and sur- roundings within were comforting, and helped one soon to forget the miserable and pitiless rain outside.

The nurses in their picturesque and clean uniforms were already busy with the decora- tions in this large hospital, arid I noticed also on arrival that several of the patients, con- valescent, wire assisting. Each and every one were infused with the spirit of Christmas. Onc. saw there gigantic Christmas trees decorated with gifts, and I learned that the organisation was so perfect that every soldier at present an inmate was due to receive a Yuletide gift. As a preliminary to the rejoicings to-morrow, the artistes of the Hippodrome gave an enter- tainment on Thursday, which was thor- oughly enjoyed. The happy festal day

itself is to be a bright- and merry one. Bpecial fare is to be provided, and in the afternoon a house concert is to be given. At night the soldiers there are to make their own amusements; in fact it is,to be a "do as you please " evenings with, of course, just a few necessary precautions. After trench fighting and all the horrors of warfare, the present Christmas at least for tho inmates of this noble institution is to be quite in keeping with the thoroughness of those in charge and of those interested. A merry Christmas to you all, for right well do you deserve it!

===== 0002601_19151225_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
RADOON COURT PATIENTS BRIGHT PROSPECTS.

"-How are all at Raddon Court going to spend Christmas," I asked Miss S. Broa dbent on arriving at this institution, and she with kindly con- sideration vouchsafed to me the particulars now set forth.

"We are all doing our utmost," she said, "to make a success of the ChriStmas festivities here at Raddon Court. In keeping with the times, the inmates are to be provided with fare consisting of turkey and other appetising dishes.

• "We have a gigantic Christmas tree ready for decoration, and on the festal day, in the afternoon, gifts are to be distributed to the soldiers. Of course there is to be an entertainment, which r ven- ture to suggest will meet with unqualified suc- cess. Boxing Buy is also to be spent in an enjoyable manner, for the patients are to visit the Royal Court Theatre to witness the -per- formance of the musical play 'Betty." After thanking my informant I left, feeling perfectly satisfied that those heroes at present in the hospital will be well looked after and cared for by the friends and woacers interested in that particular vicinity. "The same to you, boys, and many of them, and here's wishing the next Christmas will see us over the Rhine."

===== 0002601_19151225_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
FANCY DRESS BALL FOR THELWALL.

On making inquiries respecting Christmastide of the patients of the Thelwall Heys Auxiliary Hospital, I found the same feverish anxiety on the part of the staff and others for their welfare. Verily are he soldiers in this our town and surrounding districts to have a right and royal time. One learned that the catering for the in- mates here was to be of high merit. In the conservatory a Christmas tree stately and erect is already in course of ornamentation. Gifts are flocking in from all sources with unfailing regu- larity, which reflects great praise on the donors who wish the recipients an appropriate season- able greeting. Truly does one wonder what is to become of all the good things at present being showered upon the warriors in ouF hospitals. Christmas fare is being provided on a lavish scale, and amusements of different descriptions during the day. On Tuesday there is to be a fancy dress ball for the soldiers and staff, "So Tommies,' you will have no cause to grumble, will you?"

===== 0002601_19151225_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
A GOOD TIME AT WHITECROSS.

Memories of a previous visit, pleasant and unforgotten, were reflected in my mind as I wended my way to the Whitecross Military Hospital, for there I knew my old friend, Corporal Tombs, was stationed, and from in- quiries solicited I found that the noon-day feast is to be served at twelve o'clock, and right good Christmas fare it is. Turkeys, pork, vegetables, and Christmas puddings, in addition to apples, oranges, nuts, grapes, bananas, &c., truly a feast for the gods. Each soldier is to be pre- sented with a Christmas gift, also a Christmas card, supplied by the Whitecross Hospital Comforts Fund Committee, the latter also sup- plying a Christmas tree. Mrs. W. P. Rylands has very kindly supplied a bran pie, which will contain an additional present for each soldier. Chocolates for the patients and the staff have been sent by Messrs. Cadbury Bros. On Christlnas morning, Mr. A. J. Jolley (cligirman of the Management Committee) will present the

Christmas gifts, whilst an entertainment has also been arranged.

JOY FOR THE CHILDREN.

What of the children this festive season in our institutions? And with this thought in mind I made my way to the Padgate Cottage Homes, where at present there are one hundred and twenty young people of varying ages. "Well, and how are they all keeping?" I asked the matron. "Looking forward to the coming Christmas treat as all children do?" " Yet "; replied the matron; "we are already

Jusy at work preparing for the happy day, so much longed for by all those you see around us. At one o'clock they sit down to their Christmas dinner with, of course, some extra little dainties. "In the evening the little ones are to be present at a playlet performed ,by the friends of Mrs. Hildritch. This lady will, I know, be giving great pleasure to many hearts in the audience on Christmas night. The children are in for 9 special treat this year, for we are to have a decorated Christmas tree, the first for a great number of years. It is to be a big surprise for them.

"Yes, we are all doing our -best for the chil- dren, and I'm sure many hearts are full of glad- ness already with the prospects of what are in store for them." AfteT what I had seen and heard I felt sure that such would be the case.

MAYOR AND MAYORESS TO VISIT WORKHOUSE.

"The same as in other years," stated the authorities of the Workhouse, and with the words I knew that the needs of the inmates would be amplified at this institution. Good, homely fare, consisting of roast beef, vegetables, together with plum pudding, an apple and an orange for each inmate and bever- ages, that is what is in store for them on Saturday. Tobacco and snuff are to be pro- vided for those who court*" My Lady Nicotine," always a looked-for pleasure of Yuletide. There is also to take place the usual concert and judging from the list of artistes, will be quite up to the usual high-class excellence. The Mayor and Mayoress will pay a visit during the day. H. M.

LYAIM RECRUITING.

Mr. Alfred WaMin Thanks The

NA °fliers.

To the Editor of " The Warrington Examiner."

Sir,—Now that the difficult, delicate and arduous work in connection with Lord Derby's recruiting scheme is completed, I venture to make use of your valuable paper to express, on behalf of, the Recruiting Committee of Lymm, our great appreciation of the services rendered by those upon whose shoulders the stress and strain of the last few weeks have chiefly fallen. It would 'be impossible for me to express in words -our admiration of the work done by the Recruiting Officer, Mr. F. E. Evans. The detail has been enormous. He has -worked early and late --(Sometimes until three o'clock in the morning). His tact and urbanitylave been pro- verbial, while his powers of organisation have been of the highest order. I hope that. this and other services, past and present, which he has rendered, will be properly recognised in the near future.

To -Mr. Reginald Barker we have also to express our thanks for his conspicuous services as joint hon. secretary. Owing to Mr. Callender's serious illness (which his numerous friends deeply regret), a double amount of .work has fallen to the lot oi Mr. Barker. The,coinmittee oonsider-theraselves extre'mely fortunate; in liaving been able ,to secure the services of so capable and agreeable a colleague.

Dr. Burrowes, whose honorary services and unstinted attention and devotion to the needs of our lied Cross Hospital, have kept him so fully occupied, has yet found time by working bometimes into the early hours of the morning to medically examine large numbers of reoruits. On some occasions he was assisted by Dr. L. Thorp, to whom we now tender our thanks. When the proper time comes the 'attention of the authorities must be drawn to Dr. Burrowes' eminent and most skilful services. • To the captains of the wards and of the out. township of Warburton •and the canvassers generally, our cordial thank i are due. They have one and all done their work splendidly—as the figures of the canvass clearly indicate.— Yours, &c., •

ALFRED WATKIN. Chairman of the Recruiting Committee, Lynn.

Danebank, Lymm, Cheshire, December 22nd, 1915.

It would be nice if a live man could have as many friend's as every dead man has.

LYMIVI

Gas Econamies.—At a meeting of the Gas Committee held in the Council Chambers on Monday it was decided that no additional gas apparatus, such as gas stoves, fires, &c., shall be fixed in the district during the present crisis. The Council also wish consumers to economise with their gas as far as possible, owing to the shortage of coal.

Private Walter Timperley : .Home on fur- lough.—Private Walter Timperley, Station House, Heatley, has, this week been home on furlough for a few days prior to departing with hts regiment to the Front., He is at present with the ,R.A.M.C., and for some time has been stationed at Salisbury Plain. Private Timperley, who is looking the picture of health, is very anxious to get to the Front Prior to joining H.M. Forces, Private Timper- ley was assistant clerk at the Council Cham- bers, under Mr. Mullard.. He was educated at. the Lymm Grammar School and attended Oughtrington .Church.. His many friends and admirers wish him a save and speedy return.

Charity Football Match: Cigarettes for Local Soldiers.—A football match which promises to evoke great interest in the village is to take place on Christmas Day betiveen Lymm A.F.C. and the Oughtrington Park A.F.C., on the Pepper-street enclosure. The match is timed to commence at 3 p.m. Being two local teams of note and the first match .of the season it is creating considerable interest and promises to be a "Derby" Day. Almost all the players on either side are serving with the colours, and it is, therefore, in a sense, to become a khaki match. Two of the players on the Lymm side are Private Walter Hadden, who. was wounded some time ago, and his brother, Lance-Corporal E. Hadden, who is at present home on furlough. The entire proceeds are for providing cigarettes for the Lymm soldiers.

Sapper J. Clare: To Undirgo An Operation.— Sapper Joseph Clare, -whom we some time ago. announced as wounded,: has. this week Written to. Mrs. L. Broader, of Booth's Hill, from the 2nd Western General Hospital, Leicester-road, Higher Broughton, where he is at present stationed. - He- says:—" I am still in the land of the living and suppose I shall be here for the Christmas holidays. I don't know how I, shall go on, for there is an operation before me. However, I hope it is soon; 'then.* ,will' not be so bad for Christmas. I --vrould dearly have liked to be home. this festive season, but it is no use worrying. At-least-L am glad the I people here are to do something for me, for am sure it is getting worse to open-my mouth. Sister says I am going to have what is called, a "gag" put in my mouth, so it will in con-1 sequence stop me from speaking. It is a good I job it is not summer, or maybe I should get I my fill of flies and other insects. Never mind,l I shall keep toddling along and shall pull I through all right eventually." Sapper J. .Clare, it will be *membered, was wounded I when attached to the Royal Engineers, his injuries consisting of a piece of trench mortar which was blown into his mouth.
