THE GRAMMAR SCHOOL.

"Speech Day" And An Entertainment By The Pupils.

A GREAT SUCCESS.

At the request of the governors and head master_ of the Lymm Grammar School, the relatives, friends- of the sttulents, and others were entertained on Saturday last in the school- room. The invitation, it is pleasißg to record, met with an unqualified response, for never on "speech day" has the school been filled to such an extent. With splendid .patriotism the pupils have this year foregone their prizes, with a view that..the . money shall be spent in the furthering of many efforts to alleviate the sufferings of our soldiers; or help to cheer those fighting to ex- terminate the common foe. Instead .r the scholars therefore receiving prizes for meri- torious work for the past twelve months, in their stead certificates were presented, which speaks highly for the pupils' thoughtfulness and self- sacrifice. • •

Looking round the hall the first thing- one rioticed was the Roll of Honour, comprising the names of the old boys who- have so nobly and spontaneously answered the call of their King and country, and as was befitting the occasion it occupied a Prominent and conspicuous, place in front of the platform. The gathering attracted considerable interest, and a topical*: and fine varied musical and dramatic programme was fully enjoyed by those whose pleasure it was to attend.-

School's Military and Educational Record.

At the commencement of the proceedings Vice- Chancellor Stewart- Smith; K.C., gave a very practical lecturette intermingled with many humorous stories, which were thoroughly appre- ciated by the audience. This was followed by a, distribution of certificates, at which the Rector (the Rev., G. B. Thurston), chairman .of the governors, presided. The Head Master Pre- sented his report, in which he referred to the difficulties caused by the enlistment of his staff. These difficulties had, fortunately, been sur- mounted, thanks chiefly to the splendid co-opera- tion of ladies who-had-come, forward/in a very real and sincere.manner. The number of pupils was about the. same—to be precise it was 143, which in itself, considering the present condi- tion of affairs was extremely r...tisfactory, thanks to the unswerving loyalty of the parents con- cerned. Several pupils' names had been sub-1 mitted to examination by the Alorthern UniVar-. ties' Joint Board Examination four scholars ob- them being brought- into contact with other universities. -Referring to the prominence of Latin in the course, ,Mr. Hawkins said that this subject called for concentrated thought, and suggestions had been made that the art of book- keeping should be taught, as many thought- it absolutely essential to commercial enterprise. However, in view of the great call for need of economy, special attention had been paid to this particular subject. At the Northern Universi- ties Joint Board Examination four scholars ob- tained the school certificate, viz., Annie Winnall, Norman Benson, Y. Armitage, and R. Rath- bone. The senior pupil, Ruth Willan, had passed the m.atriculation examination, which had in consequence enabled her to have again renewed her county intermediate scholarship. On the "Roll of Honour" were names near and dear to those present, in- all fifty names; truly al record to be proud of. One word he must say,' not only on behalf of himself, but of the wholel school, and that was that he had to extend sym- pathy' to Mr. and Mrs. Smith in their present anxious time concerning their son, George Victor, an old boy of the school, respected and admired for his straightforwardness and in- tegrity. He hoped and believed that the present work carried on by all concerned would con- tinue to such an extent that real educational ser- vice would be the outcome to that part of the county of Cheshire in which they resided. (Ap- plause.)

===== 0002601_19151225_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
Bright Prospects.

The Rector mentioned the fact that Mr. Hawkins was called up for military service at the commencement' of hostilities, 'and that the governors had a very anxious time until they had obtaised his "demobilisation." (Laughter.) It was satisfactory to. be-able to say to the par- ents that the school had in no way suffered from the.war,.as.at the present time it was in as good a position, in many ways, as .ever it had been during the history of the school. (Applause.) The governors held the Head Master in the highest esteem, and reposed the utmost confi- d.ence in him, not only in his manner of con- ductorship, but also in his ideals. A bright ,future was in front of the school. He also wished to thank, on behalf- of the governors, Miss Hub- back, who until recently was in their midst. Paying a eulogistic tribute to this lady, he said she had come to them at a most trying period in the history of the school, which earned for her the gratitude of everyone. Mr. Stewart-Smith (the Vice-Chancellor) also spoke to the parents respecting the boys and girls leaving school before the proper time. This was not to the benefit of either the parents or their children, for an extra accumulation of knowledge would be very beneficial in these-com- petitive times. Addressing the scholars, he com- plimented them on their thoughtfulness in fore- going the usual prizes, which showed they were trying toilo".`-their.little bit" in a, humble way.

. ..kr: T. J. 'Ridgway, in proposing a vote of thinks 46 the Yice-Chancellor, endorsed his re- Marks as to keeping the . children longer- at school, saying that it was for the benefit of themselves and those at home. The school had had a Ward fight, but success was about to be achieved. The vote was heartily accorded.

The Programme.

After singing the National Anthem, a number of scholars produced two scenes from Shake- speare's ,"Richard H." in a manner which re- 'fleeted great credit on those who had trained theta. The first scene was "A room in John of 44a,unt's Mansion, Ely House," the special feature being a fine address on love of country by "John of Gaunt." This part was played ex- cellently by Master Mounfield. Scene 2 was in the gardens of the Royal Palace, in which the Queen (Miss Doris Richards) heard the news of the King's • downfall from the earden-ers. The following is the cast:. Richard H. Hallows; John of Gaunt, Moun- field; Duke of York, Wallwork; Earl of North- umberland, Bryder; Head Gardener, Willan; others; Parker and Owen; the Queen, Doris Richards; Ladies, Elsie Odber and Ella Short; Halberdiers, Cooke and Houghton; Attendant, Thomson. ' All took their parts in a style very favourable to the audience, and a commodious stage, replete with beautiful hangings and up- to-date lighting, erected by a number of friends of the school, helped in no small way to make the occasion such a success.

The Doll's Tea Party, a very pretty scena, was played by the following characters: Mistress Doll. Molly Rowlinson ; Mistress Mary, Alice Welford ; Jack Horner, J. Furness; Miss Muffett, Katherine Tweedale; Old Woman in a Shoe, Dorothy Moston ; Little Boy Blue, G. Parmiter; Simple Simon, S. Welford; Pieman, B. Kith; 80-peep, Kathleen Barrett; Piou-Piou, B. Such was its success that a repetition of the entertainment was arranged for Tuesday even- ing, at which another crowded audience assem- bled. A small charge was made, the proceeds being for the Serbian Relief Fund and for the provision of huts for the soldiers at the Front.

PADGATE.

Prize Distribution.—On -Friday, December 17th, the annual prize distribution was held at the Padgate C.E. Schools. The meeting was presided over by the Rev. J. Hirst. There was a good attendance of parents and friends. Dur- ing the afternoon recitations and songs were rendered by the scholars. The Headmaster (Mr. Blackwell) read his report, which stated that the school had had a successful year, and that one boy, Edward Higham, had obtained a scholarship to the Boteler ,Grammar School. Prizes for attendance and good work were pre- sented to the boys by Mr. G—Mather, and to the girls by Miss Jackson, to whom a vote of thanks was passed, on the proposition of Mr. Royston, seconded by the Rev. A. J. Morton.

Old Folk's Treat.—The Christian service sec- tion of the Wesley Guild are to be congratu- lated on their efforts to give the old folks in the' district a Christmas treat. Although the weather conditions were most unfavourable, about sixty people, sixty years of age and over, accepted the invitation which had been given to a tea and concert held in the Wes- leyan Schoolroom. After tea a, miscellaneous programme was given, presided over by the Rev. A. J. Morton. The soloists were Miss B. Brough,.Miss Ward, Miss Ball, Mr. Bate, and Master Douglas Pickering. Recitations were given by Miss A.. Tomlin and Miss E. Morton; a duet by the_ Misses Ward and Dewar; Lanca- shire readings by Mr. S. Dawson; whistling solos by Mr. Cottrell; trio by Messrs. G. Walker, A. J. Morton and W. J. Bate; glees by the choir, itc. The accompanists were Messrs. T. Brough, Ytir. H. Ward and Mrs. Bate.

CAPTAIN WITHINGTON DEAD.

The death is announced of Captain Thomas Withington, of Culcheth Hall, Culcheth, near Warrington, at the age of eighty-four ~years. He joined the old Militia as far back as 1854, and the Lancashire Hussars in 1804., He was a justice of the peace for the county of Lanca- shire, having been appointed for the West Derby Hundred in July, 1857. When his health per- mitted he was a regular attender at the Sessions. held at Warrington, Leigh and Liverpool. On attaining his magisterial jubilee in 1907 his brother magistrates presented him with a handsome clock, a silver bowl, and several beautiful pieces of silver plate. Captain Withington held several public posi- tions in Warrington. He was the oldest gover- nor of the Warrington. Infirmary, .the oldest trustee of the Blue Coat School and the Savings Bank, and the senior income tax collector. He was twice married.. His second wife was the eldest (laughter of the late Mr. Octavius Wil- liam Temple, the Governor of Sierra Leone, and a niece of the late Archbishop of Canterbury.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
A CALL TO

Bankers' Manifesto To The Nation.

The bankers of the country ha-e issued a clearly-worded manifesto to the men and women of this country, the tone of which is full of confidence; its object is to show what is required to achieve victory, and how the requirement can be met. The bold statement is made that the one thing now needful is money; and it is the duty of Great Britain, untrammelled in her commerce, to supply that money for herself and for her Allies. The task is shown to be huge, but it can be performed by strenuous co-opera- tion, by- concentrating on the production of essentials only, by eschewing non-essentials, by economy.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
['RESIDENT IVILSON'S MARRIAGE.

President Wilson was married to Mrs. Galt at her home in Washington on Saturday evening. The President was busy earlier in the day at the White House. from which he motored to the home of his bride. The bride's mother gave her away; and the only guests were member's of the families. The distinguished pair left for a fortnight's honeymoon in the South.

STOCKTON HEATH.

In The Trenches.—The Parochial Committee have decided to send the season's greetings to the Clerk (Mr. F. Ashton), who is serving with the Army in France. Mr. Ashton Las recently been home on a few days' leave, but be has now returned to the Front. He has been in the first-line trenches and within 50 yards of the Germans.

At the Parochial Committee Meeting.—A meeting of the Parochial Committee was held in the Council Schools on Tuesday evening. Mr. George Pilling presided, and also present were: Messrs. J. H. •Clarke, F. Pearson, J. McDermid, W. Mclntyre, A. Bottomley, J. Hallows, E. Bradshaw (clerk to the Parish Council), A. Stanley Wright (surveyor), and W. E. Hough (acting clerk).

Mr. James Whitley's Illness.—At the meeting of the Parochial Committee on Tuesday even- ing, Mr. F. Pearson stated that Mr. James Whitley, of Walton-road, was recovering from his recent illness, and that he had sent his good wishes to the members. Mr. Whitley- had a stroke about a fortnight ago and lost the use of his left arm and his power of speech. We are pleased to my that he has practically recovered and soon will be in normal health.

Christmas Gathering.—The annual Christmas gathering arranged by the .teachers of St. Thomas' Sunday School was held in the Parish Rotans on Saturday afternoon. A lar4e and well-laden Christmas tree was in the room, and this proved to be a great attiaction to the large number of children present. A number of stalls were also in the room, and on these were displayed suitable articles for sale. At the conclusion of the gathering the helpers were entertained to tea. The proceeds .4 ill be devoted to a Missionary Society,

"The Messiah."—The choir at the Wesleyan Church gave selection from Handel's "Messiah" on Sunday. The first part was rendered in the afternoon, and the second in the evening. The soloists were Miss Florence Gavings (soprano), Madame Lily Fairhurst (contralto), Mr. W. Hetherington (tenor), and Mr. R. A. Robinson (bass), the singing of whom delighted every member of the large congregation which was present both afternoon and evening. Great praise is due to Mr. F. E. Penketh, who con- ducted the choir with much skill and ability. and to Mr. A. Trow, who presided at the organ. An address was given in the, afternoon by Mr. John Taylor, and in the evening Mr. A. Lumb spoke to the gatherings', Special collections were taken on behalf of the Trust Funds.

" Specials ", at Supper.—The Stockton Heath Special Constables held! a "hot-pot" .supper,at the .Mulberry Tree Hotel on Monday evening, twenty-four being present. The catering was excellent and the supper was ,thoroughly en- joyed. Mr. W. Bouchier (leader) presided and Mr. F. Rose (leader) acted as secretary fcr the occasion. Inspector Breese was also present. The following toast list was submitted and honoured: "The Bing," "Our Army and Navy," "The Regular Constabulary," "The Special Constabulary," "Our Lads at the Front," and "The Wives of the Specials."— Inspector Breese suitably responded for the regular constabulary, and Mr. Bouchier for the "specials." An interesting programme of musical and other items was rendered, the feature of the evening being the fine recitation and songs of Mr. Lambert, who was heartily thanked for his services. Messrs. Pearson Baguley And GernOn also contributed suitable items. The meeting was a short eine, but heartily enjoyed, and the proceedings terMin- ated with the singing of the National Anthem.

NIGHTSOIL DISPOSAL.

The question of the disposal of the nightsoil is one that has been before the Parochial Committee for some time. Recently, Mr. C. L. Evans, of the Arley Estate, was approached as to the terms upon which he would be pre- pared to allow the committee to continue to use a site on a farm as a tip for nightsoil. Mr. Evans's reply was that he would be glad to know the approximate quantity' of refuse per annum before he could give any answer as to terms. His own impression was that it would be far better for the Council to prcvide a destructor.

Mr. Bottomley : Would it not be well to ask the Warrington Corporation on what terms they are prepared to provide facilities? The Chairman and the Acting Clerk were deputed to approach the Town Clerk on the question.

THE COMPLIMENTS ,OF THE SEASON.

At the close of the meeting of the Parochial Committee on Tuesday evening Mr. A. Bot- tomley proposed that the committee should extend to the Chairman (Mr. G. Pilling) their hearty good wishes for a bright and happy Christmas and a prosperous New Year. Mr. Bottomley added the hope that in the coming year Mr. Pilling would not have the family troubles he had experienced during the past twelve months, and that his wife's health would be greatly improved. (Hear, hear.) Mr. J. H. Clarke, seconding, said they were always pleased to see Mr. Pilling's happy countenance amongst them. They looked for- ward to much better things for the country in the coming year, and, when our cause had pre- vailed; we might indeed have a .jolly Christ- mas.

The vote wa.s cordially passed, and the Chair- man briefly thanked the members and returned the good wishes.

DIFFICULTIES AT GALLIPOLI.

Letters From The Sons Of Mr. James Whitley..

Private P. Whitley, who is serving with the Mediterranean Expeditionary Forte, and was recently. on ,the Gallipoli Peninsula, in a letter to his father, Mr. James Whitley, of the Walton- road Post Office, writes:— " I am pleased to say I am in , very g6od health, and doing very well. .I have so far escaped the shells and bullets, which is some- thing to be thankful for when one thinks of the number of machine-guns and rifles that are aimed at us from the hill-top§ and cliffs, where we are under constant observation and fire. The Turks have earned the name of being good fighters on' the defensive, ,and when our men advance they have to climb places literally .studdied with machine-guns, nd in the 'teeth of a terrific...fire from the heights. When I landed here and saw the heights and difficulties which had, to be faced, it almost. looked 'impos- sible for any troops, to land at all, and no finer deeds can be recorded than those performed by the first landing. party. Their work must have been magnificent. I -have, spoken to one or two men who took part in the *first landing, and. heard from them the deeds that were performed that day. If ever men deserved the name of heroes, these men deserve it."

===== 0002601_19151225_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
Cheshire Compared With Gallipoli.

In a letter to his brother, Mr. Jos. Whitley, Private Whitley writes : " I was very pleased to hear from you. Be- fore I received your letter it had been in Egypt, Greece and Turkey, so you see it had to travel a bit to find me. I was delighted with the post- cards you sent me. I have shown them to a few of my comrades, and they all remarked What a beautiful place Whitley must live in.' I waa always ready to assure them it was. I can hardly choose which is the best, ap they are all good ones, but I think the old Post Office at Grappenhall was praised the most by all who saw the cards. I fear I shall be obliged to spend another Christmas from home. Hard luck, eh? Still, it is not for us to grumble. I am pleased I am doing my bit, and I sincerely hope we shall return, and that Germany will be sniashecl be- fore mueh longer. Lance-Corporal Pratt is here with me. and I will ask him about the Stockton Heath people. He is a Lancashire lad and a good one, too. We have already noticed the change in the weather, and winter will soon be upon us. To-day iis pouring with 'rain.

Just picture to yourseif some of those old by- lanes in Cheshire when they are knee-deep in mud and you will then have a slight idea what it is like on the Gallipoli Peninsula." In Hospital.

Another son of Mr. James Whitley, Private J. Whitley, has been transferred from France, where he.has been for the past 13 Months, to a hospital at ChislehuAt, Kent. In a letter to his father, Private J. Whitley writes I am in hospital and had to undergo another opera- tion. I am glad to say that everything was a success, and I hope to be walking about again by Sunday."

TO PREVENT FLOODS.

Agreement With The Manchester Ship Canal Company.

The Manchester Ship Canal Company and the Parochial Committee have decided upon certain measures by which it is hoped to pre- vent certain Streets flooding in the event of a heavy rainfall. _

At a meetinc, of the committee en Tuesday evening, Mr. W. E. Hough (the acting clerk) read a letter from Mr. H. A. Reed (chief engineer to the Manchester Ship Canal Com- pany) with reference to the proposal to lay ft storm overflow between the manhole in Eger- ton-street, Stockton Heath, and the Manches- ter Ship Canal. He' enclosed draft agreement containing the terms • and conditions upon which he was prepared to recommend the directors to grant the desired easement through the property of the company. At a recent in- terview the deputation from the Parochial Committee stated that it was expected that the proposed storm overflow would only come into operation on very rare occasions, as the necessity for such relief in time of flood had only occurred twice in the year 1914, and not at all during the three previous years. The easement would, therefore, be granted by the company on the distinct understanding that the storm overflow would only be used in case of emergency, and should the overflow be found to come into operation with undue fre- quency, or should a nuisance arise through the discharge of sewage or other injurious matter into the Ship Canal, the company would have no alternative but to terminate the agreement.

The Surveyor (Mr. A. Stanley Wright) said the Ship Canal Company had accepted the scheme proposed by the committee. The agreement was approved. The Chief Engineer to the Ship Canal Com- pany also wrote stating that he had carefully considered the question of the proposed over- flow at the head of the syphon under the Ship Canal near Northwich-road swing bridge, btif he could not recommend the directors to accede to the request.

SIR JOHN FRENCH TO HIS

ARMY.

TOUChillij Faro% ell.

DAY OF VICTORY NOT FAR DISTAN'F.

"The Pride And Glory Of My Life."

The War Office has issued through the Press Bureau the text of the order of the day in which Sir John French takes- leave of his troops in France and Flanders on relinquishing the command. The order is dated Saturday. Sir John says In relinquishing the command of the British Army in France, I wish to express to the officers, non-commissioned officers, and men with whom I have been.so closely associated during the last sixteen months my heartfelt sorrow in parting with them before the campaign in which we have been so long engaged together has been brought to a victorious conclusion.

" I have, however,. the firmest conviction that such a glorious ending to their splendid and heroic: efforts . is not far distant, and I shall watch their Progress towards this final goal with intense interest but in the most confident hope. The success so far attained has been due to the indomitable spirit, dogged tenacity which knows no defeat, and the heroic courage so abundantly displayed by the rank and file of the splendid army which it will ever remain the pride and glory of my life to have commanded during over sixteen months of incessant fighting. Regulars and Territorials, Old Army and New Army have ever shown these magnificent quali- ties in equal degree. From my heart I thank them all.

At this sad moment of parting my heart goes out to those who have received lifelong injury from wounds, and I think wiith sorrow of that great and glorious host of my beloved comrades who have made the greatest sacrifice of all by laying down their lives- for their couDtry. In saying good-bye to the British Army in France, I ask them once again to accept this expression of my deepest gratitude and heart- felt devotion towards them and my earnest good wishes for the glorious future which I feel bi he assured.—(Signed) J. D. P. FRENCH. Field Marshal Commander-in-Chief, British Army in France. December IS, 1915.

SIR JOHN FRENCH A VISCOUNT.

Since the commencement of the war, during over 16 months of severe and incessant strain; Field-Xfarslfal Sir John French has most ably commanded our Armies in France an Flanders, and he has now, at his own instance, relin- quished that command. His Majesty's Government, with full apprecia- tion of and gratitude for the. conspicuous . ser- vices which Sir John French has rendered to the country at the Front, :have, with the King's approval, requested him- to accept the appoint- ment .of Field-Atfaishal 'Commanding-In-Chief the troops stationed in the United Kingdom; and Sir John French has accepted that appoint- inent. ,

His Majesty the King has been pleased to confer upon Sir John French the dignity of a Viscount of the United Kingdom.

NEW YEAR'S EVE.

No Peat This Year At The Manchester

Town Hall.

, The Town Hall Committee of the Manchester Corporation have decided that there shall be no ringing of the Town Hall bells to mark" the passing of the old year and the coming in of the new. If, therefore, the customary gathering takes place on New Year's Eve in Albert Square, it will, for the first time in the history of the Town Hall, be without the chief feature of such occasions. The bells will be pealed as usual at nine o'clock on Christmas morning.

FARM AND GARDEN.

'THE ROYAL.

Membership Gives Total Of Over Ten Thousand.

The report of the Council presented to the annual general meeting of governors and mem- bers of the Society, states:— Since the last annual meeting the losses by death include one Trustee, the Earl of Jersey, G.C.11., and two Vice-Presidents, the Earl of Faversham and Lord Rothschild. The above, and other changes, bring the total number of governors and members now on the register to 10,144, divided as follows :-182 annual governors, 88 life governors, 7,317 annual members, 2,529 life members, 28 honorary members; 10,144, as against a total of 10,570 members on the register at the time of the last annual report. Agricultural Relief of Allies Fund.

Following the precedent of 1870, when a fund was raised at the instance of the Society for the assistance of the peasant farmers of France, the Council, in January, appointed a committee (with power to add to its number) to prepare a scheme for the alleviation of the distress caused by the war to agriculturists in the countries of Britain's Allies. Agricultural and kindred societies appointed representatives to serve on the General Committee, and at a meeting held in February more than seventy societies, with an aggregate membership of over 100,000, were represented. His Grace the Duke of Portland. at the outset, was good enough to head the move- ment as President, and it was decided that th,t title of the new body should be the Agricultural Relief of Allies Committee. Already practical assistance has been rendered to the French farmers in the shape of implements, such as binders, threshing machines, ploughs, harrows, &c., live stock comprising South Down sheep, pigs, and goats, and a number of consignments of seed wheat for autumn sowing has also been shipped. Gifts, of poultry for shipment in the near future are now being collected. The R.A.S.E.—as the initiator of the fund—made to it a contribution of kl,OOO. lip to date, the sum of £26,000 has been received or promised, in addition to gifts in kind. It will, however, be readily understood that the task before the com- mittee is one of considerable magnitude, and they therefore appeal most earnestly to members of the British agricultural community to be as generous as they are, able to be in their dona- tions to the fund. Cheques or promises should be addressed to the Hon. Treasurer. Agricultural Relief of Allic,; Fund, 16, Bedford-square, Lon- don, W.C.

Nottingham Show.

Though the entries of live stock at Notting- ham were below the numbers of recent years, there was certainly no lack of quality in the exhibits. The show was held in Lord Middle- ton's Park at Wollaton from Tuesday. June 29th, to Saturday, July 3rd. In spite of the un- favourable weather on the first two (lays of the show, the aggregate attendance for the five days was 103,88.3 and the financial result was a debit balance of £2,945, which, when everything is taken into consideration, cannot be regarded as unsatisfactory.

Manchester Show of 1916.

The Manchester Show will be held on the site near Alexandra Park and Withington Stations from Tuesday, June 27th, to Saturday, July Ist. 1916. An influential committee has been ap- pointed of representatives of the county and city to raise the necessary funds for carrying out the local requirements, and arrangements have been satisfactorily concluded with the Royal Lanca- shire Agricultural• Society, who have decided not to hold their show in the year 1916.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
HIGH PRICES FOR WOOL.

Wool prices generally are now on the highest level ever recorded, says the "Times." Certain kinds of Australian scoured merino wools used for the manufacture of fine cloths are now being sold at the London auctions for about 3s. 2d. a pound. or a shilling above the prices regarded as satisfactory in normal times. Varieties of Australian greasy merino wools are being sold for Is. Bd. a pound. as compared with"ls. 2d. before the war, while wool from the Falkland Islands has been sold for is. 7d., as compared with is. 2d. before the war. Crossbreds, Which are used largely for heavy military cloths, have also risen sharply. The rise in prices of all kinds of wool has been caused by the very heavy buying by British spinners largely to meet the ordinary commercial requirements which for some time past have had to yield to military needs, and by buyitig on French, Russian and American account.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

CLIMBERS ON TREES.

Many people have an idea that climbers are injurious to trees. In the case of young trees this may hold good, but on those that are past their best climbers are admissible, many of the old trunks lastifig much longer when covered with a strong growing and vigorous climbing plant. We have distinct recollections of having seen during the past summer vigorous climbing roses threading their way through the branches of some large hollies, the beauty of the rose flowers being considerably enhanced when seen against the dark green leaves of the holly. What again is more beautiful than some of the hand- some clematises when allowed to grow at will through the branches of a tree? Of the climbers that may be used for this purpose many will occur, but to my mind nothing equals climbing roses and clematises. We lose much of the natural grace and beauty of these when they are fastened to houses and walls, on which owing to the enforced restriction they are unable to show their value when in bloom. A rose or a clematis that makes a 12ft. or a 15ft. growth in one year needs plenty of room, and no better, place can be found than a tree which it can ramble over, and bedeck -With blossoms. One thing is all im portant in this plan of treating climbers, and that is at the start to provide a good larder and after a few years (to make up for the probable loss of vigour) to feed well by adding to the sur- face a good mulch of. rotten manure, or if obtain- able some good soakings of diluted 'liquid manure.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
GAS LIME.

In any garden where insects have been troublesome a dressing of gas lime will be an advantage. This, as a rule, can be purchased very cheaply, and if the ground were occasion- ally dressed with it we should hear fewer cora- plaints as to insect pests. Scatter it over the ground and let it lie for a week or two, then fork it in. Let this be done at least three months before any sowing or planting is done so that the lime may have time to become well in- corporated with the soil.—" Wick," in "Firm and Home."

STRANGE "LOTS" AT )UMBLE SALES.

As the result of a number of gift sales held recently, the Agricultural Relief of Allies Fund now amounts to over £35,000. Among some remarkable " lots " offered last week was' a heifer calf, worth about £2 or £3, which was sold sixty-six times at a Maidstone gift sale on Thursday, and realised £202 for the fund. In all, over £2.000 was raised. At other sales a poor boy's kitten, brought 14s. 6d., a cricket ball used in a match between Kent and Middle- sex last year was sold for 10s. 6c1., and a Berk- shire pig was the means of enriching the fund by over £lOO. The fund seeks to give material help to farmers in allied countries whose farms have been devastated in the war.

SACRIFICE AND SERVICE.

Never in Greater Demand Than At Present.

At the Friends' Meeting House, Warrington, on Sunday, Mr. D. P. Boyd, of Manchester, de- livered an address on "Sacrifice and Service," in the course of which he traced the Old Testa- ment ideas of sacrifice and compared them with those of the present day. The Old Testament ideas were three-fold—a sacrifice, to secure, and retain the Divine favour; to remove His displeasure; to express gratitude for benefits re ceived.

Sacrifice, to be of ,any practical value: Must be man's spontaneous expression of his need of God, and all such sacrifice inevitably resulted in whole-hearted service to humanity. All sacrifice meant the entrance into a larger life, as well as a clearer conception of God. The highest form of worship of God was shown in service to humanity, as evidenced by the life and character of Jesus Christ, " who went about doing good." Never, perhaps, in the history of our nation had there :been a greater need of sacrifice and service than at the -present. It was not only a question of .giving, but of serving, quoting the following lines from the Holy Grail:— Not what we give, but what we share, For the gift without the giver is bare,

Who giveth himself, with his alms feed three, Himself, his hungering neighbour and me.

The Comfort Of The Gospel At All Times And In All SphereS.

A. well-attended meeting of the WS-cliffe was held on Sunday afternoon. The Secretary (Mr. B. William Street delivered a very interest- ing address upon "The Gospel." Ile said that in these days of darkness and sorrow the Gospel just met the needs of the people, while even on the battlefield the soldiers loved to hear the Gospel. Families would shortly be meeting to- gether for the Christmas rejoicing; but in many homes, where the clouds of bereavement bung, they would find it hard to rejoice. Such were the homes which would need the Gospel. The nation .itself needed it, for it meant good news to them. It was indeed good news to him he sPeaker) fourteen years ago, when first he knew ,the Lord. The Gospel, which they loved So much, Was given to us by the death of Christ upon the cross. Deniers of Christ attempted to rob the Gospel of its power and comfort. Only God could explain the _fulness of the Gospel. Mr. Beswiek also took part in ,the meeting. GERMAN SPIES.

Sweeping Assertions By Mr. William Le Queux.

Mr. , Win. Le QUeux, speakiag recently at Llandudno on the German spy peril in this country, said

It was no exaggeration to say that to-day there were spies of Germany in every walk of British national life. That there were spies in every department of administration was not denied in the House of Colllllloll§. When the Kaiser decided to make war he sent his agents. over to this country. First came those who moved in high society or had money. Thy gained social distinction, became naturalised. Privy Councillors, birthday baronets and M.P.'s. Then came those with 4 little money who ex- .ploited trade, Wormed their way into the com- mercial life of the country, gained social dis- tinction, and took an interest in local politics. Finally there • came Tom, Dick and Harry, the butchers, the barbers, the bakers, and last but no means least the German clerk speaking three or four languages willing to work for a small wage and .who was always clean' and in- dustrious. Since the outbreak of war a great deal had been found out, and one thing was that none of these Germans came to England without- a monthly subsidy. Another was that they, were urged to marry English girls, and when they did so the subsidy was increased. This was -part of the plan for Germanising Eng- land. The Gernian expenditure on' their spy system five years ago was at the rate of t 350,000 a year, then it rose to 3:WAD, and this year the sum voted the secret service was £1,000,000. What be&ime of that money ? It (lid not go into the pockets of the baker and the candlestick maker, but into the Pockets of people higher up in social life. If that was not so, how was it that certain secrets in this year's Budget were known in Frankfort 36 hours before they were disclosed in the House of • Commons. That proved .there were spies in high quarters. The existence of spies in: England was also proved by the fact that details of the damage done dur- ing Zeppelin raids could be .read in Continental newspapers. .Those details (which were per- fectly true were denied to the British public) were sent over to the Continent by spies by signalling and otherwise.

He advocated the internment of all Germans. naturalised or unnaturalised.

"Why do, the nations?" by Mr. E. 11. Pearce. The choruses were sung with much expression by the choir. A special collection was taken on behalf of the Society funds. Percy meson, who gave an address on 'Are we ready for victery?" said the ques- tion was not Do we want victory? but Are we fit for victory? We say this warfare is 'for spiritual principles. Are we, as a nation, spiritually fit? Until there is a great national cleansing our first prayer ought to be " Cleanse thou me." The question also involved the further consideration—Are we ready to- use victory rightly? ..The use of victory would be not for; the acquisition of territory 'but.,-the crushing of militarism; not for the crushini, of peoples, but the cultivation of- brotherhood. l we succeed in crashing Germany and leave militarism in our midst, then Germany, ap- parently defeated, will have won. . Then are We .as a Church ready? Has the shock of battle roused us to realities? Are we ceasing to live for self and trying. to carry out the ideal of the Christ whose birthday we .are soon to celebrate, to "seek and save the lost?" If we are then as a Church we are ready, and therqt will be a revival 'of personal •holiness in this country which will make her it and ready for victory.

Bacon and Bread Fri.ters.

• , 'Bacon , and bread fritters make a good supper course, especially if served with lightly-poached eggs. Take slices from an end of cold boiled bacon, and put them between slices of thin bread. Cut these sandwiches into neat rounds, dip into frying,' batter, and cook in boiling fat until a golden brown. Vile 'high on a hot dish, scatter chopped lean ham over, and serve very hot.

What She Had Seen.

An English tourist visited Arran, .and being a keen disciple of Izaak Walton, was arranging to have a day's good sport. Being told that the cleg or horse-fly would • suit his purpose admirably for bait, he, addressed 'himself to Kirsty, the Highland servant girl. "I Say, my girl, can you get me some horse-flies?" Kirsty looked stupid, and he repeated his question. Finding that she did not yet comprehend him, he exclaimed, "Why, girl, did you never see horse-fly?"—" Nan, sir," said the simple girl, "but aw wanse saw a coo jump ower a preshipice."

THE VISION OF ANIMALS.

Crocodiles cannot distinguish a man at dis- tances 10 'times exceeding their length. Fish are very short-sighted. The vision of most sr- pents is poor, the boa constrictor being able to see no farther than one-third its own length. Some snakes see no farther than one-eighth of their own length. Frogs are better endowed, for they can distinguish objects at 20 times their own length.

The hearing of noarly till reptiles is mix

I worse than their vision. Most of them are quite deaf. The phrase, "deaf as an adder," represetits the careful observation of our fore- fathers.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
HINTS ON HEALTH.

Useful Information In Popular

Language.

The Use of Olive Oil in the Diet.

, Olive oil may be uscd in cooking in the place of butter, lard, or suet, and for most if not all purposes it .is superior. The only objection is the expense, but, as a rule,' a first class quality of olive oil is less expensive than a first-class grade of butter. The Most popular use of olive oil is in the preparation of salads and of course salad or lucca oil is simply olive oil of'a varying quality. A little olive oil oVer lettuce, cress or tomatoes, with the addition of a little pure lemon jtrice or the merest trace of salt or sugar makes a dressing that is at the same. time both appetising, nourishing and wholesome. . There are very few people who cannot. take olive ,oil with a salad. The oil is equally useful in making a potato, beetroot, or some other vege- table salad, but it is not wise to use the oil too freely in preparing such salads for the table because all people are not equally fond of it. Most varieties of mayonnaise dressing are pre- pared with olive oil, and the same is true of a large Proportion of other salad dressings. In Spain olive oil is used in the place of butter on the bread and is really only a,;matter if custom and taste. Persons who are not accustomed to take olive oil in that sitty dislike it at first, but, after a little practice, come to enjoy it quite as much as they would. ordinary .bread and butter, at least, so we have been told by those who have tried it. Olive oil is really superior to butter in 'being practically sterile and free from microbe organisms when it is .prepared and bottled. But it is well to remember that olive oil like other fats takes up. odours readily, and like butter is liable to spoil, become rancid, and unfit for food. Olive oil is one of the most concentrated of all fuel foods and no food product is a richer source of energy. There is absolutely- no waste, for a good grade of olive oil contains practically no trace of moisture. Weight for weight, compared with sugar, olive oil is:more than twice as valuable as a-souice of heat and energy.—" Good Health."

WHAT A HORSE ASKS.

Don't ask me to " pack " with blinds on; I'm afraid to, says a horse. Don't lot some blockhead drive me that has less sense than I have.

Don't run me down a steep bill, for if any- thing should give way I might break your neck. Don't whip me when I get frightened or I will expect it next time and may make you trouble. Don't trot me up hill, for I have .you. buggy. and myself to carry. Try running up bill with a load yourself. Don't drive me with " over check on; the sun hurts my eyes and I can't see where to step. It's inhuman and cruel.

'reach me to stop when you say "Whoa,." and this you can do without jerking my -head off or tearing my mouth. It may check me it the lines should drop or break and save a runaway and smashup.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

BY MARJORIE.

Their Maiesties' Greetings to the Troops. Last year the King and Queen presented Christmas cards to the troops on active ser- vice, and it Was the kindly intention of Their Majesties to follow that precedent this Yule- tide. But acting on the advice of 'the military authorities, they have, with much regret, relinquished the idea.. Tbe. transport of mil- lions of cards was more . than the authorities could undertake just now, whilst their distri- bution would have proved. an almost impossible task. Even the work of delivering Christmas and New Year greetings. in this country will be attended by many difficulties, for thousands of women and girls with necessarily little experience, although much good-will, • have lately taken the places of .Men of the regular staff who have joined the _Porces. The foreign mails have increased enormously, and So many letters and parcels are being sent to the various fronts, and to prisoners of war, that it has been found necessary to erect special buildings in London to deal with them.

German-made Toys for Christmas. • • Purchasers of toys as Christmas and New Year gifts would .do well to examine these thoroughly before deciding to buy, for it has been proved that numbers of German-made toys have reached this country- through neutrals, more especially through'.. America. Many are marked made in Germany, • but as the lettering is small, and in German char- acters, it is generally oVerlcioked. •, by pur- chasers. Seeing that a considerable amonni, of British capital has been invested in an attempt to revive, the industry of toymaking in this• country, it is a patriotic duty to encourage British trade, more especially when this em- ployment is one of the few open to so many disabled soldiers. It also enables a number of artists who have been hard hit by the war to make a living, and provides work for women also affected by the war. .

Australian " Billies" In The Dardanelles. Australian soldiers in the • Dardanelles have been cheered by the prospect of Christmas Parcels from home packed in "billies." These indispensable adjuncts to the camper's outfit are used everywhere in Australia for boiling tea over the camp fires. The Australians are inveterate tea drinkers generally, and partake of the cup that cheers five or six times daily.

Unfortunately for the men, " billies " were not included in their kits, but the -need for them was quickly realised on landing. They were supplied from Australia, ,together With all kinds of comforts, in the shape of woollen and other garments. The "billies" were care- fully packed, Christmas and other delicacies being added to the -useful garments, and. the parcels were timed to arrive at their destina- tion during Christmas week. Although Christmas in Australia-, occurs in the middle of the summer, roast beef or turkey, mince pies and plum pudding appear on every table, as in the Old Country, A picnic in some beauty spot is often. arranged for Christmas Day by Australians, who revel in outdoor sports and amusements.

Pioneers In Food Reform. The National Food Reform Association has preached reform in diet for a number of years. The need for economical housekeeping has lately brought their work into prominence, and the association has certainly acted wisely in adding Miss Petty, who has earned for herself • the name of "time pudding lady," to the staff. This lady has recently given a series of lectures in the provinces, where, as in Lon- don, she has advised the making of bread at home. There are, as everyone knows, many kinds of bread; that advocated by Miss Petty is made from wheatmeal and white flour, in the proportion of two-thirds- of the former to one-third of the latter; or half of each may be used. Wheatmeal is to be preferred to -white flour, because it loses none of the valuable constituents of the berry in the preparation. Bread-making at home is usually regarded as impossible, because the process entails so much -trouble and labour, but Miss Petty makes her. staff of life with the aid of soda instead of yeast, thus minimising the work. The result is most satisfactory, time bread is _appetising and inexpensive, and well worth a trial in these days of increasing household expenses.

The Importance of, Line. Frenchwomen have deservedly won for them- selves a wtorld-wide reputation for good dres- sing. Englishwomen equal them in many matters requiring taste, and in their choice of suitable materials and dresses for any time, weather, or occasion. The Frenchwoman, however, scores, because she studies the, lines of her figure and adopts the styles that bring out its good points, and hides its defects. The woman of any nationality- who possesses .the valuable knowledge may earn for herself the Frenchwoman's , reputation, and- yet spend comparatively little money on her wardrobe. The Englishwoman approaching middle age, whose hips are well-developed, more often than

not, increases ,her but too apparent rotundity by puffings and pannier effects—when these are worn—because such styles are fashionable, or white fur is frequently chosen by the short, stout girl or woman, and the whole effect of an otherwise well-thought-out toilette is spoiled. Even a Muff—or, rather, its shape— is capable of distorting the lines of the figure of its wearer; its choice is therefore a matter worthy of consideration. New Hosiery and Footwear.

The short skirt necessarily demands smart hosiery and footwear. Several new devices are shown in stripes, .circles and -checks for the former. Black shoes and hose are in request for afternoon Wear.. Boots get higher every week, but all must fit well round the ankle. Black glace kid is worn again, its only draw- back _being that it stretches, and thus the footwear loses its shape. It is, well, therefore, to choose a tight rather than an easy-fitting pair of boots or shoes of this kid. Louis heels are anoilier revival that .meets with general approvallifor evening wear. A most Hensible and seasonable idea, is the long gaiter of tine black stockinette, shaped like a spat, and covering the front part of the foot, ending like a stocking at the knee. It it quite a smart affair for outdoor wear, providing wannth where it is much needed. The short skirts afford but little protection to feet and limbs; this gaiter, therefore, meets a real need of the hour. More especially will it be wel- comed by Women engaged in war work, and who are often compelled to sit in cold" and draughty rooms for hours together.

Bootlaces and Buckles. Bootlaces 'in place of buttons arc much in evidence as fastenings for the front of dresses, blouses, and boleros. Sometimes both are used, the lacing being merely un extra decora- tion. A very lovely blouse in pale pink was smocked with silver thread and laced in front .with a silver cord. Cloth is largely used .for .the tops ,of high boots, the heels and toes .only being of patent leather. The cloth wears remarkably well, but such footwear is expen- sive, and as a consequence of the war, which affects the incomes oven ,of the rich, more re- pairing is done now, than a couple of years ago. Buckles are worn again on evening shoes and are generally of silver. They add consid- erably .to the price, even when only of white Metal. , But they are a good purchase, because they add so much to the appearance of a shoe. The old-fashioned bUckle is always to be pre- ferred to the modern, anti the lucky possessors of these inconsiderable trifles have now a chance of bringing them out of their hiding- places and wearing them.

HOME NOTES.

Profitable Recipes And Useful Suggestions.

Celery Soup. • Use the outer, tough stalks that are not suit- able to be eaten raw, cut them up in small pieces, and simmer in water for three or four hours, or till tender. Towards the last, let the water boil almost away, then put in milk, a little thickening. and salt, pepper and butter. After the .milk is added, great care is needed to keep the soup from burning: It will not do to forget if for a moment. If the milk can be heated scolding hot in a double boiler before it is added to the celery, so much the better. Mutton and Tomato Rice.

Cut some good thick slices of cooked mutton, trim all to the same size, season with pepper and salt, and rub well over with salad oil: then broil over a clear fire. For the tomato rice, spt loz. of butter in a frying-pan and cook in it slowly, a tablespoonful of chopped onion till it smells strongly Then add a teacupful of washed and dried rice, and stir constantly till slightly browned. Take nearly a pint of mashed and strained cooked tomatoes and mix with nearly.the.same quantity of stock. Add this to the rice, &c., in a clean step-pan, season 'highly with pepper and salt, and cook slowly till the rice is perfectly tender and the broth quite absorbed. Just before serving stir in a table- spoonful of butter, and.season to taste. Arrange the rice in a flat mould, witk the boiled mutton in ft circle on it, and round the mould Pour a little thick, rich gravy.

To Boil Fish. The water should be actually boiling. It is then at once drawn to the side of the stove and only allowed to simmer .till the fish is done. Only sufficient water should be allowed to keeP the fish covered, and this water should have added to it a little salt. Remember, from the moment when you bring the water just to the boil it must not boil again. hut only he kept at a gentle simmer, or the outer part of the fish will be broken and over-cooked, while the inner part will be underdone. Especially is this the case with thick fish. Tile general average for fish is oix minutes to the lb., and six minutes over. How to -Cook Plaice.

Plaice should be kept at least 24 hours before cooking. Clean them, cut off their heads, sprinkle them lightly with salt, and hang them up by the tails,, in the open air if possible, for 12 to 24 hours, then dust them with flour, pepper and salt, and broil them over a clear fire, and they are very good. If ..for filleting, skin, trim them into neat fillets, and let them stand. for 2 or 3 hours in a mixture of oil and vinegar (lemon-juice is, nicer); shread onion, parsley, and two or three peppercorns. then either egg and breadcrumb them, and fry them, or tie .them up into shape, and put them in the oven on a buttered tin, with two or three. spoon- fuls of fish stock, a squeeze of lemon-juice, and, if liked, a very little wine; lay a buttered.paper over them, and let them cook about 19 or 12 minutes, then drain them, and serve with any sauce fancied.

Cold Bread and Butter Pudding. • This is a simple but &flan.* sweet when properly made. Cut some slices of thin bread and fresh butter. Rub the bottom of a pie-dish with butter, and decorate it with a star or fancy pattern made of preserved cherries, sultanas and candied peel, or currants and raisins. What-

ever fruit is used should be first dipped in a little melted butter; this. will make it stick well in position. Cut the slices of bread and butter to fit ,the pie-dish, and over each layer sprinkle a little grated nutmeg, castor sugar, sultanas and chopped peel. Over all strain a good custard, allowing three eggs to tho pint of 'milk. Let. there be plenty of liquid, so that the bread may be thoroughly soaked. Leave all for an hour or two, then bake in a slow - oven. Turn out and serve cold with-a good custard poured round the pudding.

Swedish Pudding. Beat four whole eggs until they are foamy Take lb. of butter and the same quantity of powdered sugar and beat it to a cream, in a separate bowl. Pour the -beaten eggs carefully on the butter, mix well, add 111,. of twice sifted flour, flavour with grated vann:a. Steam in th( individual shapes, and erte a wine sauce or a fruit sauce with them.

The Old Year and the New Year.

As you grow up,. you will find that it is a custom to make promises to oneself to be better during the coming year than one has been in the past, and to expuct better times in future than those through. which 0116 has alre:ady passed. 4 shall be glad to see how my 'young

nephews and nieces can think and write upon such a subject.; It is not a difficult subject when tackled in earnest, and you are getting quite expert letter writers now. So let us have a good collection of essays with which to start the New Year. You see I 'dm looking ahead myself for an improvement even in the Children's Corner, which most people think excellent already. But New Years are made principally to allow us to improve upon the old, and I hope you will all do your best in every way to see that idea carried into effect.

"A happy Christmas and a glad New Year" is the greeting of

Your UNCLE JACK

A FISH'S TAIL.

A fish's tail is its wings. 'Owing to the machinery of muscle set along its spine and to its cleaving form, a •trout or salmon can dart through The water at a tremendous pace, though its rapid flights, unlike the bird's, are not long. It is soon tired. The water is not so friendly 40 flight as the air. The stroke of tlic fish's tail is one of great power, and by means of it and tho writhing, snake-like flection of the body, high speed is reached. The strength behind this 'speed is shown in the way in which a :fish or sea-mammal out of the water will raise itLi tail and strike the ground or boat.

Christmas Little Folks.

THE LOST PLUM. PUDDING

it was a vcry jolly Christmas dinner alto- gether. Paul the Polar Bear sat at the, head of the table and did the carving, his family and their friends the Esquimaux children, who lived next door, sat round, and Pat and Patter, the little Arctic foxes, waited on them. The snow house was very comfy, and Mrs. Paul Polar had decorated it most beauti- fully, and the only, sad thing that happened was the plum pudding getting frozen to the dish. Paul tried so' hard to get, it off that he broke the dish, and the pudding jumped right off the table. Everyone scrambled after it. They looked under the table, all over the floor, behind the pictures, and inside the Chinese lanterns, but no pudding could they find. , Little Peter Paul Polar began to cry, and all the Esquimaux children from next door said good-bye and went home, because they wouldn't stay to eat a Christmas dinner with no Christmas pudding: Paul Polar grew more and more savage, and Pat and Patter went pitter-pattering all over the house to get out of his way, and to try to find the missing pudding. Now they had all forgotten about Blabb the pet seal, who had been lying under the table to pick up any scraps. When the pudding fell he bad caught it in his mouth, and hurried away in the' ‘onfu- sion to eat it.

THE CHILDREN'S STOCKINGS.

"Wake up! Wakeup!" said Joy( e to her little brother Roger. s "What's the matter ? " asked Roger, rub- bing his eyes sleepily. "It's Christmas Don't you remember? " whispered Joyce. "And oh! I do really be- lieve Santa Claus has come. Ssh ! Don't wake Nurse. Shall we tell Boobies and Baby Bunting too?" Roger was out of bed in a moment, punch- ing and pummelling his baby brothers to wake them up. Baby Bunting began to cry —he wasn't used to being waked up like that, and especially in the middle of the night.

"Santa Claus has come! Wake up, silly !" said Roger in a very loud whisper. Baby Bunting stopped crying, and Roger helped him out of his cot.

"Oh, dear! I know Nurse will wake up," said Joyce. "And then what should we do?' But just then Roger struck a match and lit the candle. "Why," he said, looking at the big empty bed, "just fancy! Nurse hasn't come to bed yet!" Then all the children rushed off to the fireplace, where Nurse had hung up all their stockings in a row; and, oh! those stockings were so bulging full! There was the prettiest doll for Joyce, and a Teddy Bear for Roger. a Golliwog for Boobies, and a rabbit with pink eyes for Baby Bunting,, all staring at them out of the tops of the stockings. "Oh, I wish we could get them down!" sighed Joyce "Wouldn't it be lovely ! " Suddenly Boobies gave a little frightened scream. "Dere's Santa Claus lookin' in at do window!" he said. All the children looked round. It was only the big moon shining in; and Joyce and Roger began to laugh. "But I seed him make a face at me, I did," said Boobies, crying. "A nasty, horrid face."

Then the door opened, and in came Nurse. "Oh, you naughty, naughty children ! " she cried. "I thought you were all fast asleep. Why, it's only the beginning of the night. It's not Christmas Day for a long time yet." But somalow she couldn't be cross so near to Christmas, so she tucked them all up in their beds.

"I'm sure it was Santa Claus I seed look- ing at us through the window," said Boobies sleepily, "and he was making a face at us because we were naughty children, wasn't he: Nurse?"

"Very likely." said Nurse ; "but let him find you all asleep when he comes back this way."

And the moon still smiled in at the children.

I haven't told you why I put The clock on yet,- you say? . Why, don't you see it was to help The time to pass away And bring to-morrow here? Because To-morrow's Christmas Day!

"Alt, but it is not always Santa Claus who comes!" Madame Dubee said, lifting her finger. "Other people have reindeer belides, and pretend to bring you presento." "What other people?" Georgette asked timidly.

" I have no children,' I said, and quickly shut the door and drew the lijg bolt, be- cause out beyond him across tile snow I saw the shining of many eyes, and I knew they were the wolves, who never leave the Wolf- man for a second."

Just as she finished speaking there came a knock at the door. Even Madame Duboe 6hivered a little, and when the knock came again, louder than before, she wished she had not spoken to the children about the wolves.

Bnt when (she went to the door and opened it, there was no one but a poor old man bent double with the cold.

FORTY YEARS ON THE LINE.

Railwaymen's Recognition Of

Comrade's Services.

I,ItEsTATATION OF A CHEQUE.

At a meeting of the local branch of the National Union of Railway- men, held at .the Co-operative Hall on Sunday afternoon, Mr. W. Adams, tho chairman of the local branch, was presentzqi with a cheque as a recognition of his long ser- vices in the Union. In the absence of Mr. J. A. Seddon, who had been called to Scotland for the 'week-end; the Mayer of Warrington (Alder man Peter Peacock) occupied the chair. The Mayor said that lie was always willing to help the Society with which many of his Into colleagues had been connected. They were called together that afternoon to make a presentation to Mr. Wm. Adams, the chairman of the local branch of the Society. Mr. Adams had been connected with the Society for 25 years, and the Society, had sent him a cheque for =.5. The district branch had sent him a gold chain and a gold medallion. . • The Mayor then handed the gifts to Mr. Adams, amidst loud 'applause. A short address was given by Mr. Rrassington, the district secretary, who said that he was very pleased to be associated with the presenta- tion to Mr. Adams'. Many people believed that when the war was over the country would pass through a period of trade depression. Other people believed that the country would ex- perienecs a period of prosperity; but no one coull gauge exactly what the position would be after the war. This war had been so peculiar, so novel and strange and so gigantic in its pro- portions that it would be fatal for anyone to act the part of a prophet. Without taking any party position, there were problems that would have to be solved in the future. One would be blinding himself if he thought that there would be no industrial struggles after the war. The great industrial-problems had not been settled and the war would not settle them, tliough per- haps the war would teach many things. Ire wanted people to look forward without pre- judice. Mr. Adams also gave a short address. He spoke of his early associations with the Society. He said that his railway career had come,to an end, and he was glad that the time had come when he could retire; for when a man had had 40 years in a:signal box he. bad had sufficient. (Hear, hear, and applause.) He thanked the Society for its gift.

Man Of Many Legs. A young officer at the Front wrote home to his father : "Pelt- rather,—Kindly send me fifty pounds at once. Lost another leg in a stiff engagement, and am in hospital. without means."

The answer was as follows: "My Dear Son,— As this is the fourth leg you have lost (accord- ing to your letters), you ought to he used to it by this time. Try and hobble along on any others you may have left!'

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

A Foreword. They watched their flocks, those shepherds in the East; We Warringtonians take it not Ohara: We sleep on our's; then rising (like the yeast), Think of our friends and usual Christmas card.

Those anxious shepherds held their nightly meetings • Because they feared attack by savage "bist"; I don't, who now send seasonable greetings, Yours truly, An Old Journalist.

Supplementary. Ought one to apologise for a thing like that? I'll narrate instead a little domestic incident Of the bygones. When my daughter was a tiny tot, she laboriously produced a simple rhyme of four lines, and in handing it to me said with serious countenance, "Please don't laugh; my very own." Perhaps that may serve my present turn.. Anyhow, longsuffering readers must allow that I have not troubled them with anything of this kiwi for quite a long while. And at all events the greetings are genuine and heartfelt, for I owe much to my readers' forbearance.

A Vegetarian en the Turkey. That is quite a nice reference to our favourite Christmas bird which the Rev. A. M. Mitchell makes in his current Parish Magazine. "Toothsome," "tasty," and so on, eh? I rather wonder that while he was about it, the good Vicar did not tell his people how the turkey came to take the place of the boar's head. Is it not, evidence that we are progressing in the direction he desiderates? The truth of the matter—l remember a contemporary noting it' years ago—is that the turkey first usurped the boar's head in popular favour owing to the dislike of James I. to pork, as a result of which the old-time dish was banished from Royal circles when that monarch came to the English Throne. The same writer was at some pains to show, moreover, that the turkey has some con- nection with the boar, through its scientific name " Meleagris." The famous naturalist Linne first gave it this name, deriving it from the mythical story of Meleager. Meleager's association with the Christmas bird of the moderns, however, is not very creditable. Having achieved fame by slaying the wild boar which was devastating his father's kingdom of Calydon, he disputed with his uncles over the ownership of that animal's skin, and settled the argument by killing them also. Thereupon his mother -compassed his death, and the goddess Artemis rounded the family business off by changing Meleager's sorrowing sisters into guinea-fowl. My authority added that Linne mistakenly assumed the turkey to be a guinea- fowl, and derived the name " Meleagris " from the guinea-fowl sisters of the hero of the Calydon boar incident.

Toothsome and Tasty. Perhaps, after all. Mr. Mitchell was not aware that the turkey is as a prime ,Christmas dish descended (so to put it) from the boar's head. However, he is good enough to concede that the flesh is " toothsome" and " tasty," "savoury and juicy." That may well be so, considering the extreme care exercised in tfie bird's upbringing. Eiperts tell us that a young turkey is one of the Most delicate things on earth. A sudden chill—even ever so slight a one—spells death to him. An. average breeder thinks himself lucky if he raises 60 per cent. of those hatched out. I know not how it may have been since the Germans worked such havoc on the European Continent, but I read some years ago that on farms that make a speciality of breeding turkeys for the English Christmas markets, thanks to unremitting attention, as many as 90 to 95 per cent. of chicks are reared. They are—or at any rate were when conditions were normal—fed at first with sweet, fresh meal. golden .delicious custard made of egg and milk in equal proportions, and gently physicked twice a week with dandelion milk. Later on they were stuffed with ground pea-flour mixed with olive oil, wheat boiled in milk, and cold, boiled chestnuts, ground up and made into a paste by the addition-of milk or cream. Mr. Mitchell will no doubt remark with satisfaction that the turkey's food is wholly vegetarian; for the moment, however, I am merely concerned in accounting for the savoury, juicy, toothsome, and tasty chavacter of its flesh. I may just add, then, that during the last few weeks ,of the bird's mundane existence, he is given daily doses of a weak solution of vinegar and water—to make his flesh tender—and is hunted about the "exercise yard" by boys paid for the purpose, for twenty minutes or so each day, with a simi- lar end in view. I suppose that, after all this. the reader will enjoy his turkey none the less.

" The Messiah." The time-worn strains of " Gran'fer " Handel's übiquitous oratorio, which. had rever- berated throughout the week, attracted untiring devotees to the Parish Church for the perform- ance by the leading musical organisation in these parts last evening. A friend of mine expresses amazement that "The Messiah" continues time after time to emerge triumphantly from the onslaughts of modern interpreters, but the point is not for 'local application. The fact that the performing members of the Warrington Musical Society are trained studiously- to avoid sensational readings of the work is to be counted unto them for righteousness. Bachelorhood.

In a " Here and There" paragraph last week, the question of choice between a rich bachelor and a rfth widower was raised to the disparage- ment of the former. Eighteen years ago I made the acquaintance of a confirmed bachelor who, being of a decidedly literary turn, compiled and published a book on the subject of my. head- line. I am beginning to took for a revised and up-to-date edition, for the author has (pre• sumably) enjoyed eighteen more years of Single blessedness since he was first inspired to write on this interminable. theme. A Lively Example.

Holding University degrees, my old friend is, of course, an eminently respectable person— sober, staid, quiet-spoken, a typical old bachelor of the middle class. On Saturday night I accidentally found myself in the company of a specimen .of another order. He confided to .me that he lived alone, and did all for himself. Then -he had to make his own are, I inferred. As to that, he explained, he really only re- quired one on Sundays, as when he was about his ,business during the week he took his meals at restaurants (I had met him in one and we chatted thus over tea). But what 'about house- cleaning, I persisted. Ah, well, he admitted, he was obliged now and again to attempt a little sweeping and (lusting and tidying up. "Still, You see," he hastened to add, visibly brightening, "I'm not nearly so particular as some. Take bed-making, for instance. I sup- pose there are people who make their beds daily, whereas I think nothing of tumbling into the hole I Made yesterday or even some days befor that. Sleep? You bet! But I must have my pipe, whatever else. It's last thing at night and first next morning. Eh? Why, yes, if you must have it, I do smoke in bed sometimes. What? Ought to have a woman about the place? Nay, nay; I've had troubles enough of my own without that. If I'd been married—no, it won't bear thinking about; good Lor— i+, good-night, if you're off." As I hurried away to catch my train for home, I thought how that lonely, if cheerful, old bachelor was doomed to spend Christmas, and I envied him not. lam 'Old, 'too—reckoning by years, that is —but, like " The Examiner" little folks' .Editor, I " take a. great interest in children and all that they do." May they all have as good a time as possible this -Cliristmastide.

It is no use praying over Greenland's icy mountains as long as you look like a permanent frost.

THE SCOTCH VERNACULAR.

Local Descriptive Words That Have Historical

===== 0002601_19151225_art0206.txt | included=true | included =====
Value.

SHORT STUDY IN DIALECTS.

BY C. E.

In a former paper I made it clear that the Scotch were descended from the same parent stock as the English, and that the Scots from whom the country derived its name were members of a comparatively small Celtic tribe that originally came from Ireland and occupied a portion of Western Scotland. To some extent they mingled with the Lowland Scotch. From the proportion of words in the Scotch dialect of Celtic origin we conclude that the Celts, which included the Scots, did not form any large -proportion of the Scotch people, except, of course, in the fringe in the west and north. The Lowland Scotch were of Teutonic origin, mainly Saxons, who came direct front the Continent, and were after- wards augmented by Saxons, who had pre- viously settled in England. It is the language of these that is known as Scotch.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0207.txt | included=true | included =====
SCOTCH AND ENGLISH LANGUAGE FROM SAME PARENT STOCK.

The Scotch dialect has therefore for its basis the Anglo-Saxon language as spoken and written during the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries, when the mixed language of Eng- land, which had hitherto been in a state of flux, was. beginning to assume a settled form. This was largely the result of the introduc- tion of printing. Scotch poets contemporary with our English Chaucer wrote in very much the same language. A . Scotchman, and for that matter one conversant with the Lanca- shire and other English dialects, can under- stand Chaucer better than the ordinary Eng- lish reader. This implies that in the dialects we have the original English better preserved than in our modern speech. We see then that dialects, instead of being, as some think, mere vulgarisms, springing up locally, are in reality formed from the early English, and have altered less than our ordinary speech.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0208.txt | included=true | included =====
IDENTITY OF EARLY ENGLISH AND SCOTCH WORDS.

The following early English and Scotch words are identical or very similar, and these words, whichl think are all of Saxon origin, are not now used in our modern speech. attach the moderg English to explain the early words: Wrocht, wrought; spak, spoke; twa, two; lave, the remainder; shone or shoon, shoes; eyen or een, eyes; mon, man; shawes, woods; mak, makes; Berk or sark, shirt; delve, dig; baith, both; biggin, house; bigg'ed, built; sicher, sure; fecht, fight; folk, people; •neive (nek, Shakespeare), fist; gowans, daisies, also called " daye's eyes," from which- we get daisy. These are a few words picked out in reading a few early Eng- lish poems. There are, of course, very many more. •

===== 0002601_19151225_art0209.txt | included=true | included =====
FRENCH WORDS IN SCOTTISH DIALECT.

Many French words were introduced into the Scotch vernacular through the close asso- ciation between the two countries when Scot- land .was a separate kingdom. Several place names in Scotland are derived from the French. The Scotch tongue also owes some- thing to the Scandinavian, of which the Danes were a branch. There are also a few Gaelic words that have been incorporated. We may consider the Teutonic Saxon as the main source and stream, and the French, the old Norse, with its various branches, and the Celtic, as smaller streams that have joined the main one in making the language. When all these sources have allocated to them their contributions we find they -cover nearly the whole vernacular, and few Scotch words have come to birth in Scotland itself. No doubt new. words had to be introduced to describe new things, natural or manufactured, but the new words have not been distinctly Scotch, but have been those common to our language as a whole. For example, we would not expect the Scotch to invent a differentr name for phonograph, automobile, or aeroplane. I am more convinced, the more I look into the subject, that dialects .are not the outcome of the locality in which they are found, but grew from roots that go far back into the past; hence they have an historical value not gener- ally accorded to them. ThefolloWing words indicate the tributary streams to which I have 'referred

French. Scotch. English. Assiette Asbet , A large plate Bouie Bools Marbles Bossu Bowsie Bandy-legged, crook- backed Cadet Caddie (golf) Originally younger son, then messenger Cliquet Cleek (golf) A hook Contre Conter Oppose Craquer Crack To talk, to talk boast-

ingly Commere Cummer A neighbour Dressoir Dresser Kitchen sideboard Gare de reau Gardyloo Look out Gauche Gawky Awkward Haulier • Hash A sloven, to cut care- lessly Hachis Haggis Minced meat, especially applied to mince meat cooked in a sheep's stomach - Leal Leal Loyal Mauvis Mavis Thrush Merle Merle Blackbird Poche Pooch Pocket Troc Troke Exchange Dour Dour Sulky Coup Cowp Overturn Gigot Gigot The thigh, hind leg of mutton

To the Danish Scotland owes several place names and a few ordinary words, such as gar, to make; greet, to shed tears; also to the Celtic, Inver, confluence of waters; Inverness, Crag, Loch, Dun, etc.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0210.txt | included=true | included =====
EXPRESSIVE SCOTCH WORDS.

Some Scotch words are very expressive, and this can best be realised in poetic setting, and we will try'and find some examples. The word Wimple is expressive, and takes

===== 0002601_19151225_art0211.txt | included=true | included =====
THE VILLAGE WHITELEY.
