While waters wimple to the sea While day breaks in the lift sae he, Till clay cauld death shall bin ray e'e Ye shall be my .dearie.

This verse introduces another interesting word, the Lift, meaning the heavens, and it expresses the same thought as the word heaven, i.e., heav-en up, lifted up. "And God made the firmament, and divided the waters which were under the firmament from the waters which were above the firma- ment; and it was so. And God. called the

firmament heaven." . . . Is it not true that

God on this throne is • Eldest of poets. Unto His measure Moveth the whole?

The word Did is a good one and better than the English equivalent vibrate. We find it used to describe the funny-bone, and this in Scotch is the dirlybane. In the English dia- lects we have the word Dither, which is quite as descriptive as dirl. Note the force of dirl in Burns' " Tarn O'Shanter " :

At winnock-burn in the east, There sat old Nick, in shape o'beast; A towzie tyke, black, grim and large, To gi'e them music was his charge; lie iecrewed the pipes and gart them skirl, Till roof and rafters a' did dirl!

"At Winnock-bunker " means that the Devil sat playing the bagpipes on the window- seat to the east in the ruined church.

Roof and rafters suggest another interest- ing word connected with a building. Riggin is the ridge of a house, a riggin tree is the ridge piece, possibly allied to the rigging of a ship. The word Bonnie has almost become Anglicised; it conveys more than pretty and rather less than beautiful. •

The word Gloamin' for twilight has always been a favourite Scotch word; it seems to whisper its meaning. I used to think this word peculiar to the Scotch, but have dis- covered, what appears to be its original in an early English poem, and used for twilight in the form Of Gloome coming from Anglo-Saxon Glom. Gloom and twilight are naturally allied. In the Lancashire we have three poetic picture terms for the same.' Edge o', ncet, Eawl-leet .(owl light), and Neet-fo (night fall). The coming of darkness has often been ex- pressed as the falling of night, but lacking a single word it has to be expressed in English at length: The shades of night were falling fast, As through an Alpine village passed. The tern' " tak-tent," to take heed, • Tak tent olrath and heed this well, The man that sins maks his ain hell. Tak-tent is no doubt related to the .early English Tentaiu, meaning taking care Of. • Hound Billy's curly head all fairies play,

Tentain his little bed till break of dui. A tenter in Scotland is one who looks after or takes care of a number of looms.

I cannot understand why the early English To-morn got altered into to-morrow. If we speak of to-day, why not of to-morn ? • Hinder-yeir in early English means the behind year, and the Scotch Yestreen stands for yesternight or yesterday night.

Yestreen there were four Monies. This nicht there'll be but three.

Mornin'-side, meaning the East, is" common in early Etiglish, Scotch and Lancashire.

An' a pratty bit of garden greawand, O'th mornin'7,side o'th fowd.—Waugh.

The Scotch and Lancashire word Lippen suggests a couthie thought, which we lack in the English equivalent trust. I find I have unconsciously used the Scotch word " couthie," which is a good one and. means tender—and something more—a comforting tenderness. This suggests a similar word, Cantie, which means cheery.

0! could some cantie word o' mine, But mak. yer careworn faces shine, Or cause the hairts in grief that pine, To throb wi' pleasure; Then wad my cup to auld lang syno Fill to .its measure.

Yird-earth (early English) and Kirk-yard (Scotch) mean a burying-place. Church cemetery, conveying the idea of a sleeping- place in its Latin origin, is appropriate, but it is somewhat soulless unless you have in mind the root thought. The poetic expres- sion, " God's Acre," is beautiful, but scarcely accommodates itself as a definite name. The Scotch' Laverock and the Lancashire Layrock for the lark are good; both spring from the early English Leverock. „

The laverock• shuns the palace gay, And o'er the cottage sings; For nature smiles as sweet I ween, To Shepherds as to Kings.—Burns.

When th' layroeks finished his work aboon An' laid his music by, Ile feathers deawn to his mate, an' stops Till dayleet stirs i'th sky.—Waugh. ,

The word Oxter means armpit or arms; we have both in early English and Scotch. " Tak the bairn up in yer oxter "—take the child up in your arms. I cannot trace the origin of this uncouth word beyond the early Eng- lish. Can any of my readers help? The word Stour has no exact equivalent in English. It means dust, but dust in motion. Dust at rest is not stour. I think the word comes from the Scandinavian.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 25, 1915. SHEPHERD LIFE IN THE EAST.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0215.txt | included=true | included =====
NEW LIGHT ON OLD TEXTS.

The concluding lecture of a series of eight was•given by Mr. A. Mounfield in Friars' Green Schoolroom on "Shepherd Life in the East." The imagery of the sheep and the shepherd, said Mr. Mounfield, were woven into many memorable passages in the Bible, and meta- phors and stories touching shepherd life marked almost every book within it. This came about because the Bible belonged largely to the simple life of the past. In the earliest days depicted in the Scriptures men were farmers and shep- herds. Agriculture supplied food and the sheep's wool provided raiment. Life was simpler and wants were fewer. Wealth was counted V flocks and herds. The sheep of Bible lands differ from the sheep of our own ,(lay. They were not reared for slaughter, but for the growing of wool. Hence they were kept as long as possible. They became the companions of the shepherd and received pet names, as do the horses of our own day.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0216.txt | included=true | included =====
HERDMEN AND HIRELINGS.

In view of the many references to herdmen and hirelings in the Bible, it was worth while to take careful note of the usages of the past. The owner of the sheep did not usually tend them. He was, perhaps, a land holder, who looked after the crops. For the tending of the sheep he had a herdman. This individual took supreme control of the flocks, lie shared the gains which came from careful treatment of them and shared the losses also. Ile was a partner as far as the flocks were concerned. To help him with the routine work he engaged men who were called hirelings. These men worked for pay, but had no interest in the success of the sheep farming. A reference to the story of Laban and Jacob will make plain the old usage. Labatt was the farmer and Jacob the berdman. When they had hot words at parting, Jacob told Laban that the sheep "torn of beasts I brought not unto thee. I bare the loss of it; of my band didst thou require it, whether stolen by day or by night." By strict right Laban ought to have snared the losses as well as the gains. Jacob asserts that he himself had had to bear them all, suggesting that Laban had not dealt fairly with him. it is interesting to know that the usage of Syria is still the same in this _respect.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SHEPHERDS' LONG JOURNEYS.

It was necessary to commit the flocks to some trustworthy man, because of the nature of the country. The sheep had to be taken long dis- tances during the summer in order that food and water might be found. On the plains the grass soon withered and the water streams dried up. For seven months there. is no rain in Palestine. The sheep and goats are therefore led to the higher' levels, where there is water and grass. This means long ' journeyings and constant watchfulness over the flocks. The herdman must find food of some kind. Where there are trees he must gather sycamine fruit, as did Amos the herdman. and at the end of the summer lie may find himself far away up the slopes of Lebanon, pulling the branches from the trees and getting water from the last trick- lings of the meltint, snow of the heights. To secure water was usual to dig wells or pits by the side of water streams. When the streams were full in the winter the pits were filled and the shepherd, by removing the stone covers, found water when he visited the place in the summer. These pits were spread thickly over the country. Into such a well Joseph was thrown, and Jesus referred to the ox or ass falling into the pit—a common occurrence in Palestine.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PROBLEM OF SHELTER.

The other great problem of the shepherd was to find security and shelter. If there were caves he placed the sheep there and slept in the entrance himself. Sheep folds were built on the hill sides, and in a place where he had made a fold and water pits he might remain for some weeks until water and food gave out and he must climb higher into the mountains. The fold, was a circular wall of stones, protected • on the top by thorns. It had a narrow entrance, and the shepherd himself spent the night crouched in the doorway to protect the flock against wild beasts. He was "-the door of the sheep," and it is to this that Jesus referred in the saying "I am the door." The fold had a wall.across.the centre, and the sheep were kept Ott one side and the goats on the other. They did not allow them to remain together, because the goats were quarrelsome. As they entered the fold the shepherd divided the sheep from the goats."

===== 0002601_19151225_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ROD AND STAFF.

Bible stories and texts make us familiar with the herdman's outfit. The staff was a weapon, kind of club, to be used against the wild beasts. With such a club David said he had killed both a lion and a bear, and it was this weapon that Jesus forbad to His disciples with the words "neither staves." The rod was the crook With which he dealt with the sheep and hoisted them if they had fallen into the stream or pit. His sling was often used to get the attention of the sheep when they were straying. A stone would be dropped near to the erring sheep as a warn- ing. Though not a very accurate weapon, the shepherd became quite proficient in its use, and could handle it with •effect.

As the sheep grew to maturity they became intelligent and responsive. They knew their 'mates and understood the shepherd's language. Thus, when he 'called " Irrgho they knew it meant food, and "lb " meant water. The passage in Isaiah, "Ho, every one that thirsteth, come to the waters," is the shepherd's cry to the sheep. One of the older *routs was trained to be the leader of the rest. Et a-as cal:Nl the " of the flock." This exphins the nu:4Boov in Jeremiah 50, viii.," Be as the he-goats before the the flocks." These trained leaders were of much help to the shepherd. If a sheep were lost he took two of the trained sheep or goats with him. At his signal they would 'utter a cry, to which the lost sheep would bleat in response.

These leaders were also useful: where streams had to be crossed. The sheep and goats had to swim the streams, and the swift water often made the crossing difficult. The shepherd stood in mid-stream and called them by name, ready to save them if the current should prove too strong, while the leaders swam fi..st and stc.d in the opposite side bleating hopefully for /their (n- -couragement. The picture of the thepherd in the stream is reflected in a beautiful passage in Isaiah 43: "I have called thee by thy name; thou art mine. When thou passest through the waters I will be with thee; and through the rivers they shall not overflow thee." There is also the passage: "He drew me out of. many waters."

===== 0002601_19151225_art0220.txt | included=true | included =====
THE 23rd PSALM.

The familiar 23rd Psalm became more full of help and consolation when read with a full knowledge of the East. The valley of shadow was the ravine in the mountains where the ser- pent might lurk in the grass and the vultures or eagle overhead. They often had their food prepared in the presence of enemies, and the overhead enemies were often the worst..

The anointing oil was the medicinal oily mixture that was put on their scratches an:l wounds, the cup was the wooden- bowl dipped into the well for a refreshing drink. The still waters were the. pools made by the sides of the streams for the advantage of the lambs who found it 'difficult to drink from the turbulent stream.

" Passing under the rod " was the counting of the sheep as they entered the fold. This was done each evening. The passage "I will cause my people to pass under the rod," was often quoted as though it were a threat that affliction or punishment wosuld be sent. The meaning, however, was quite different.. To pass under the rod was to be numbered in with the sheep, and a study of the context in Ezekiel 20, xxxvii., would show this was the true meaning. The figure of ppeech 'lWolves f,n sheep's: clothing" referred to the hirelings who proved false., The men wore sheep skins, and in the night could creep among the flock without being detected. Jesus referred to them as thieves who came I" to steal, to kill and destroy." - In the closing part of the lecture Mr. Moun- field explained the allusion in the tenth chapter of John. There were, he said, not one parable but three, the parables of the sheepfold, the door and the shepherd, and all prer,ented

===== 0002601_19151225_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
WATCHING THE FLOCKS BY NIGHT.

When a flock is housed in a cave the shepherd usually makes a small shelter at the entrance, a covered place made of branches, or in the language of Peter "a tabernacle." In such sheltered places they watched their flocks by night. The pictares which represented flocks lying in the open country by night were woe- fully wide of the truth. No shepherd Would allow a single member of the flock to be out of the Shelter of a cave or fold. Often the herdman carried a small tent, which served as a night cover. This consisted of a piece of woven material- supported by a single post. By removing the post ,it was, taken down in a moment. The sudden collapse or removal of such a tent supplied to Hezekiah xxxviii.. 12) a figure of the uncertainty of life. The ending of life, he said, might come as sud- denly as the snapping of *a weaver's thread, and he might "be removed as a shepherd's tent." The fact that shepherds were watching sheep by night proves that the nativity of Jesus could not have been in December. Winter is the time of rain, and the flock would be safe at -home and not'far afield on December 25th. The birth of Jesus was more probably in September. At the close the Chairman Coun. George expressed gratification at the deep interest which had been taken in the lectures, and their appreciation of them. A further 'series by Mr. Mounfield will he given in the New Year.

• THE SPIRIT OF CHRISTMAS PRESENT.

By S.C.J.

The Flock struck one, and Scrooge waited for his second visitor. No spirit appeared, and h.:, shuffled out of bed to the door of his sitting- room. A voice bade him enter, and he obeyed.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
* * * * *

It was his own ,room. There was no doubt about that. But it had undergone a surprising transformation. The walls and ceiling were so hung with skulls and bones that it looked a perfect charnel-house. The empty sockets that once possessed the organs. of sight seemed to start right through him; the jaw-bones rattled; and a shivering draught rushed up the chim- ney with a wailing sound that caused the stony- hearted Scrooge to shiver as ii in sympathy. Heaped up on the floor, to form a kind of throne were corpses of men; mangled bodies of women and children, stiff and cold, clasped in a last embrace; horses Writhing in the agonies of death, guns, rifles, bayonets, swords, revolvers, broken aircraft, splintered. waggons, dismantled ships, bombs, shells, cartridges, hand grenades; and surrounding all a welter of human blood that flowed like a river beneath the door and disappeared into the street below. In easy state upon this couch there sat a huge giant fearful to see; Iho bore a flaming torch and held it high up, to shed it light on Scrooge. " I am the Ghost of Christmas Present," said the Spirit. " Look upon me!" Scrooge tremblingly did so. It was clothed in one 'black, blood-stained robe, or mantle, bordered with human hair. The garment hung so loosely on the'figure that its capacious breast was bare, as if disdaining to be warded or con- cealed by any artifice. • Its feet, observed beneath the ample folds of the garment, were cloven and covered with thick clots of blood; and on its head was a casque of steel, set here and there with shining spikes. . Its dark brown• curls were matted, thick and long; its scarred and grimy face fierce and repellent; its eye shining with hate and lust; its band gory; its voice thunderous, and its manner boisterous and drunken. Girded round its middle was a belt With blood- stained sword, and stocked with cartridges and deadly weapons. "You have never seen the like of me before!" roared the Spirit. ." Never," Scrooge made answer to it. The Ghost of Christmas 'Present rose. "Spirit," said Scrooge submissively, "conduct me where you will. To-night if you have aught to teach me, let me profit by it."

"Touch my robe." Scrooge did as he was told, and held it fast,

The throne vanished instantly. So did the room, the skulls, the bones and the hour of night, and they stood in the city streets on Christmas morning. Everything was quiet. The passers-by went or their way with serious step and saddened countenance. The shops were shut. The very church bells seemed to ring funebrially. The Spirit conducted Scrooge to a huge build- ing—Once a school. Inside, where but recently the happy faces of youthful students had shed their radiance, were rows of beds, each contain- ing a prostrate form. Nurses moved noiselessly about the room. The very air seemed sad and silent.

The Spirit led Scrooge to a bed at one end of the room. Here lay a figure swathed in bandages, tossing feverishly and moaning. _ _ _

"Blind," said the Spirit, and without his legs." Scrooge shuddered as if in pain himself. „

.• " Seven little mites at home;" continued the Spirit, " and this man will die."

"Die!" gasped Scrooge, almost fainting from the strain. " Oh, no, dread Spirit! say he will be spared." "Are there no Workhouses?" returned the Ghost.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
* * * * * *

The Spirit then conducted him to a little dwelling in a humble street.

A young mother sat nursing a tiny baby. Two other children, a boy and girl, stood shivering by her knee, their faces .bathed in tears. The woman's eyes were glazed and past all weeping. A letter lay on the table. The greater portion of the contents remained unread. The Spirit pointed with his finger and Scrooge read on " . . . your husband was killed in action this morning . . . ."

Scrooge would have spoken, but his voice was choked with tears.

The Phantom spread its dark robe before him for a moment, like a wing; and withdrawing it, revealed a wooden cross surmounting a mound of earth. A name was .cut in rough letters; and a few branches of holly were twined in a circle to form the funeral wreath.

" There are thousands of these in Europe, said the Spirit. "They die to decrease the surplus population." Scrooge hung his head to hear his own words ciliated by the Spirit, and. was overcome with penitence and grief.

*** * * *

" And is this Christmas?" faltered .Scrooge.. "Can we hope for nothing else save this?" " This is Christmas made by man " answered time Spirit. " Nothing better can be hoped for till the day when greed, and lust and hate are crushed for ever from men's hearts; until the souls of men are purified and the Angel's message rings in every land,

. , " Glory to God in the highest and on earth peace, good will toward men."

S. C. J

STUNT memauls.

The Original Santa Claus And What We Know

About Him.

A CHAPTER FOR YOUNG PEOPLE OF ALL AGES.

BY ARTHUR MOUNFIELD.

Standing like a sentinel on the bank of the Mersey at Liverpool is a church which all my readers must .have seen. It has a beautifully designed tower and is dedicated to St. Nicholas. When I tell you that St. Nicholas is the patron saint of travellers in general, and sailors in particular, you will see how appropriate is the dedication. In order to get everybody interested In Nicholas, I had better say at once that he was the original Santa Claus, the first to hit upon the glorious idea of filling people's stockings while they were asleep. And every time you see three golden balls hung over a pawn- broker's shop it will be interesting to remem- ber that these originally represented three well-filled pockets, from which he filled the hungry stockings. Having said so much, I propose to tell some- thing about Nicholas, giving first of all the warning that there is much of the story that I cannot manage to believe, however hard I try.

crime. Finding that concealment was use- less, the wretched man made confession, and begged the Saint to absolve him. This Nicholas did, and not only so, but went to the pickling tub and made the sign of. the cross. After a prayer, the limbs of the boys were miraculously united and they stood up in the brine and blessed their benefactor.

After this story had been told; believed and improved for a century or two it came to ho understood that Nicholas was specially inter- ested in small boys, and he was regarded as their special protector.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
A WONDERFUL BABY.

Nicholas belongs to the fourth century; that is to say, he was born .during the period when people said it was three hundred and something years after Christ. His home was in Lycia in Asia Minor, and we are told that he was such a saintly child (please note that this is the first thing I don't believe) that on Fridays and other fast .days of the Church he refused to suck his mother's breast. .He entered the monastery of Sion, and became in due course an Abbot, afterwards being raised to the dignity of an Archbishopric. This is only what might have been expected from such a baby, and it is not surprising to learn that his fame and power outlived him. As with our own St. Cuthbert, his relics are said to have worked many wonders; and quite se'ven centuries after his death Someone formed an expedition to Myra to steal his bones. These were secured after some scuffling and carried to Italy, and the Church at Bari, where they were deposited, became henceforward a place of pilgrimage. It is doubtful where any saint has enjoyed greater repute and popularity. The Russian nation took him as its patron saint, and he duly fills the stockings of the myriads of Rus- sian children. He is the Santa Claus of the Norse countries, and our own Father Christ- mas. He is moreover the special guardian of all maidens, of children, of sailors, of mer- chants, and (dare we put it in print?) of rob- bers. This last distinction he gained through an adventure in which he compelled sonic road thieves to restore stolen goods to their owners, and won them to better things.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FIRST STOCKING-FILLING ADVENTURE.

The really interesting thing about Saint Nicholas is the filling of the stockings, and we_had better get this story told first.

There was a—nobleman in Patara who had three daughters. Owing to losses he • was unable to provide them with Marriage por- tions, and in consequence no one would marry them. Nicholas Was a wealthy man, a for- tune having been bequeathed to him,. and he delighted to use his money to relieve dis- tress. He suspected the nobleman's trouble, and debated many- plans by 'which he hoped to help him. It was his desire that the help Should be given secretly, and one night as he inspected the'house"the moon shone from be- hind a cloud and showed him an open window. Through this window he .threw a purse of gold, which fell at the feet of the father of the maidens, and enabled him to provide a portion for his eldest daughter. A second night visit found the window still open and another purse of gold was flung in, which provided for the second daughter. But the nobleman was determined to discover the mysterious visitor, and on the following nights set himself to watch. Nicholas came under the cover of darkness to throw in a third purse of gold, but someone grasped his cloak, and the householder recognising him cried out, "0, .Nicholas! Servant of God! Why seek to hide thyself" The saint begged him to promise thal- he would tell no one of the incident, and in this way the story ends. -

HOW THE STOCKING HABIT SPREAD.

Those who honoured Saint Nicholas began to think that it was a good thing to follow his example. in the convents especially the habit was followed on the eve of St. Nicholas' day of banging silk stockings on the door of the abbess with a paper commending them- selves to great St. Nicholas. In the morning the abbess summoned them to witness the display of sweetmeats with which the Saint had honoured them.

ln the homes of _Russia and the East it became the habit to put little gifts secretly into the shoes, pockets and stockings of the children, with the pleasant information next morning that good St. Nicholas had placed them there. As the years passed and the children grew in wisdom they bun.- up stock- ings upon the bed to save the kindly visitor any needless trouble.

-gmall wonder that Nicholas kept his popu- larity. Other nations borrowed him, and though they changed his day and changed his. name-

But this is anticipating.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
ANOTHER STORY.

There is yet another story which explains why he was regarded as the patron of chil- dren. I cannot possibly believe it, but I sup- pose it has to be told. A gentleman of Asia Minor sent his two sons to be educated at Athens, and asked them, as they passed through Myra, to tall on the good Archbishop Nicholas and obtain his blessing. The . boys arrived in the town late one night, and went to an inn for lodg- ing. The landlord, in order to rob them, 'mur- dered them both as they slept, and after cut- ting their bodies in pieces put them in a pickling tub along with some pork, intending to sell them as pork and so rid himself of all traces obis guilt. But the Archbishop was warned in a vision of the horrible deed, and in the moiming went to the innkeeper and charged him with the

===== 0002601_19151225_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
PROTECTOR OF SAILORS.

The homage he receives from seamen comes from sonic stories which tell how that on mak- ing a voyage to the Holy Land he caused a tempest to cease by his prayers, .and how on another occasion he appeared to some dis- tressed mariners and saved them. .The old. office of Neptune, who was the protector of sailors, was handed over by medifeval Chris- fiats to Nicholas. Churches in seaports. were dedicated to him and sailors on reaching home took votive offerings to his shrifies. In Eng- land alone there are more churches dedicated to him than there are days in the year. • -

===== 0002601_19151225_art0230.txt | included=true | included =====
CURIOUS SCHOOLBOY DOINGS.

if Nicholas was the patron .of boys in general, he was. the friend of schoolboys in particular, and there was always a school holiday with big rejoicings on December 6th, which was his day. In the Middle Ages the boys who sang or served at the altar always met on St. Nicholas' Eve and appointed from among themselves a boy-bishop. This hap- pened in every school and cathedral, and the bishop reigned until December 28th, which was Innocents' Day. The boys conducted mock services in the church, and, the sham bishop walked through the streets to beg for money, food and sweetmeats.

Parents took the opportunity to give parties to their own and other people's chil- dren, and the bishops and his clerks usually held .prominent places at the board. It was the children's festival, and there were not a few sympathetic folks in those days who be- queathed money to provide a yearly dinner on St. Nicholas' Day for a given number of poor children, the children being asked in return to pray for the soul of the founder of the feast.

These festivals with Aheir boy-bishops were quite a feature in the. Middle Ages. and a long chapter could be written about them. They continued at Eton College in some form or other until the year 1845, -though, of course, after the Reformation they were shorn qf their irreverence 'and were not allowed to take place in the churches. In Salisbury Cathedral the visitor is shown the tomb of a boy who died during his term of office. His frail figure clad in episcopal robes is sculptured in stone upon the monument. So seriously did they take the chil4ren's fes- tival five or six centuries ago! • SAINT NICHOLAS IS SANTA.

As we have said, St. Nichd7as Day was December 6th. In Russia and Holland this is the day when the children wake up to find the stockings well filled. But we have pushed the event further back, and fill them for Christmas morning.. It is, however, St. Nicholas who brings the- goods, for Sianta. Claus is only an American corruption of the. name Sankt Klaus (for Sankt Ntkolas). The name went to America as St. Nicholas; it came back to us as Santa Claus. This curious little twist we owe to the old Dutch settlers of New York.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0231.txt | included=true | included =====
THE THREE GOLDEN BALLS.

As to the three balls that glitter over our pawnshops, these :are the three well-laden purses wnich the, generous saint flung through the window, in art Nicholas is shown with three golden pockets. The Medici family, who did well in business, took three golden pouches as their coat of arms. The Lombard merchants who came to England in the long ago brought the same sign with them, and as these enterprising gentlemen were the first to play the part of the obliging " uncle," the three golden balls became .the sign of a money- lender's shop. It is a pity that the three purses of the kindly Nicholas should have gained this flavour. But there it is, and one doein't know what more to say about it, except that it makes a very bad finish for a Christmas' article.

IN THE COURTS.

gave their names as Harry Davies and Henry Drummond. From the time of their arrival at the Central Station fipin Manchester their movements aroused the suspicions of Detective Green, and he eventually, with the assistance of Inspector Breese, of Stockton Heath, took them into custody. The Chief Constable, in stating the facts, said that on Monday week Detective Green saw the prisoners leave the station separately and then join together in Horsemarket-street. He followed their movements along Horse- market-street, Bridge-street, Wilderspool- causeway, until they were in the Stockton Heath area. At the corner of Fairfield-road and Ellesmere-road they stood taking obser- vation of a number of houses. On their return , to Warrington the men were arrested by Detective Green and Inspector Breese. When taxed by the officers as to their business, one of them replied: "We have come on a voyage of discovery." - The police officers took them at their. word and conveyed them to the Police Station, where they were. detained in separate rooms. During ,the absence of the officer for :only a minute or so, • one of the prisoners tried to get- up. to the window of. the room. When -rharged with .the offence of loitering they de- lined to give their names or addresses. "Market. People" -

Mr. A. Browne, who defended, said the accused were market people, and went into different towns disposing of goods. If other people walked to Stockton Heath their move- ments would not excite suspicion, but because there had been convictions against the two prisoners it was an offence. Under the circu)n- stances he had advised the men to plead guilty as they could not get out of it. He had made a suggestion to the Chief Constable that• if a certain course was adopted the men were pre- pared to give an undertaking not to come to Warrington again.

- With Remarkable Records.

The Chief Constable said if they were pre- pared to give such an undertaking he was prepared to say something in their favour. Both men had 'very bad criminal records. Davies, who had been convicted under seven different names, started his career in 1901, and up to 1913 he had been sixteen times convicted for larceny, loitering and other offences. The last occasion was at Salford in June, 1913. He thought the prisoner had been in the Army. It was evident that since 1913 he had behaved himself, Or, in all probability, he would have got into the hands of the police. The prisoner Drummond had also a number of "aliases," and on twehty-four occasionf! had got into trouble with the police kir larceny and other. offences.

Mr. Browne: I don't think they will ever come to Warrington again. The Chairman (Mr. Henry Roberts)) I have grave doubts about liberating men of the kind with such records. Warrington is only a small . part of the United Kingdom. I should like some undertaking from them that they will behave themselves wherever they go. Prisoner Davies: We never intend to go any- where else. I have been in the 'Army and got my discharge! The Chairman: We have some misgivings about it, but, acting on the Chief Constable's ' suggestion for leniency, we have decided to send you to gaol for three months each. I hope you will try to turn over a new leaf. Prisoners: Thank you! Thank you!

Praise For Detective.

The Chief Constable pointed out that a re- mark of Mr. Browne as to the officer being forewarned with information was totally in- correct.‘ Having regard to all the circum- stances, he thought Detective. Green was en- titled to some commendation by the Court. He had acted with a good deal .of smartness and had prevented the men from housebreaking somewhere. •

The Chairman: We are agreed that the officer acted with great smartness. Personally, I think if it had not been for your sympathetic nature the prisoners would have received a good deal more.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0238.txt | included=true | included =====
WORKMEN PAY

For Leming Their Job Without Giving

_ Notice.. Five workmen, who were summoned by their employer, Mr. I. Tennant, fire-brick maker, Folly-lane, for leaving their work without notice, were each fined 10s. and costs at the Borough Police Court on Monday. The case was adjourned from the previous week' in order that the complainant in:glit bring evidence as to the custom of the trade in regard to serving notice. • The defendants were Henry Wainwright, of 221, Winwick-road; Robert Mason, of 42, Eliza- beth-street; Alfred Jones, of 15, Aiken-street; William J. Keddie, 25, Allcard-street; and Henry Buckley, 219, •Winwick-road; ,and they, were represented at the adjourned hearing by Mr. T. S. Steel. It was pointed out by Mr. Tennant that a short time ago two of the defendants left his employ and they then gave him iat least three days' notice. In the cross-examination by Mr. Steel it was admitted by Mr. Tennant that Wainwright left in 1914 without giving notice, but on that occasion he went to join the Army and was later discharged. Replying to Mr. S. Wright (one of the mag- istrates), witness said if he intended to dis- charge a man immediately he would certainly give him two days' pay. He had never dis- charged a man without notice. Mr. Steel: There was no arrangement as to their engagement? Mr. Pennant: I never made any agreement. One of the Magistrates: Is there a works in Warrington where they, do anything of the kind? Mr. Steel: I am sure there are several. The Mayor: I am sure there are not. An old employe of Mr. Tennant's named Guthrie said it was the acknowledged custom of the trade to give two days' notice. The week's work generally finished on Thursday and they were. paid on Saturday. Mr. Steel submitted that before the prose- cution could succeed the magistrates must be satisfied that there was a- contract of service. A notice or list of rules ought to be provided in the workshop. Dr. Joseph: Is there no importance attached to an unwritten law? Mr. Steel: I am here to advocate my clients' legal standpoint. Continuing, Mr. Steel said the defendants would state that they had left without giving notice. Such •evidence would negative any contention as to the custom of the trade. He contended that the prosecutor had voluntarily agreed to let the men leave his employ. If Mr. Tennant was entitled to two .days' notice he could have refused to have paid them their full money and told them 'they had forfeited any claim by not giving notice. Two of ..the defendants—Jones and 'Wain- wright—gave evidence in support of Mr. Steel's statement, but in cross-elamination by Mr. Tennant they admitted that they had never been discharged without notice, although they had left •without so doing.

MUNITION WORKERS' UNSUC- CESSFUL APPEALS.

Four applications for discharge certificates by workmen engaged on munitions were heard at the Munitions Court on Monday afternoon, before Mr. T. J. Ridgway (chairman), assisted by Messrs. G. L. Walker (employers) and Charles Dukes (trade unions) as assessors. Three of the applications were dismissed and the fourth was adjourned for a fortnight for the applicant to appear personally. Preferred Tram Conducting.

A youth of sixteen, who was earning a guinea a week and war bonus as a turner at , St. Helens applied for a certificate on the ground that his widowed mother had left St. Helens to reside at Leigh. He was quite willing to work on munitions at Leigh: He had promise of a job as a tram conductor. The employers pressed for his return, and sub- mitted they were of the opinion 'that he ought to keep himself in lodgings at St. Helens. In dismissing the claim, the Chairman said applicant ought to find decent lodgings at St. Helens and put up with some little incon- venience in war-time.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0242.txt | included=true | included =====
Liked Manchester Better Than Warrington.

A married. Man employed as a riveter in a Warrington factory, whose home was in Man- chester, complained that he suffered from rheu- matism in his leg and was unable to follow his employment. 'He also had a sick .child at home who required some attention. The em- ployers who claimed his retention pointed out that they were quite willing to allow appli- cant to visit his home every week-end and pay his railway fare, and that he should work under. cover during wet weather. In. reply to Mr. Dukes as to why he did not reside in Warrington, applicant said all his and his wife's relatives belonged to Manchester and he liked to live there. The application was refused.

Employed as an apprentice to the occupation of a "turner," a youth, who was represented hiq father, applied for a certificate of dis- charge, The father stated that he had removed from 1 Thatto Heath to Pendlebury tp follow hisl occupation as a collier. His soWs wages were only 9s. per week and he did not see why he should be called upon to pay his son's lodgings in St. Helens. His son had obtained employ- ment as a "turner" in Pendlebury. The application was adjourned for a fort- night for the applicant to appear personally.

111 E VALUE OF INTERMEDIATE

LEAGUES.

Outlook For The New Year.

The first half of the season is almost over, and things have turned out almost as well as was anticipated. The outlook for the secend per- tion, however, says a writer in the " Manchester Guardian," is not promising. Attendances at the Northern Union matches have been dwind- ling for some little time, and with the calling up of the groups of recruits early in the New Year a further fall is probable. But apart from the question of spectators, the matter of attested players being called up will place clubs in difficulties, and, hard as it has been for some of them lately to raise a „team, it will be much more difficult for them as the season advances. The neglect to encourage intermediate football will perhaps be keenly felt in the near future, as many of the Northern Union clubs have few reserves to call upon, and will probably have to close down altogether before the end of the

season.

Swinton Defeat Huddersfield

NTERMEDIATE LEAGUE.

Only Two League Matches Played.

For the first time for a month, the weather conditions on Saturday were fit for Rugby foot- ball, but in spite of this fact, only two out of ,the five League matches were played. Silver- street were due to Meet St. James' at Wilders- pool, but as only about three of their men turned up, the game had to be abandoned. At Victoria Park, St. Mary's, who were due to play -ISt. .Thomas', failed to put in an appearance, and this was also the case with the Whitecross team, Who were engaged to play the Beamont Old Boys. Consequently neither of the games at Victoria Park was played, but a friendly was arranged between Beamont and St. Thomas'. On the Wilderspool ground a good match be- tween Heathside and Parochial was witnessed.

RESULTS UP TO AND INCLUDING DECEMBER 18th. '

Points P. W. L. D. F. A. P. lieathside Parochial Rylands's Bolton St. James' St. Thomas' Silver-street Beamont lO 4 - 6 0 32 71 8 St. Mary's Sacred Heart Whitecross -

LAST' SATURDAY'S RESULTS. Parochial, 5 points; Heathside, nil. Sacred Heart, 6; Bylands's, 5.

PAROCHIAL TOO GOOD FOR HEATHSIDE. The Parochial v. Heathside match attracted about sixty spectators, and proved to be, one of the most interesting matches that has been played at Wilderspool this season. Until about a month ago Parochial headed the League, but when they were defeated by St. Thomas' they had to give pride of illace to Heathside: So naturally there was a bit of " feeling " between the two teams. Though Heathside were de- feated on Saturday, they still remain at the head of the League, but whilst they are only a point in front of Parochial, they have played an extra match. Saturday's game opened dramatically. With- in the first three minutes Heathside were awarded two free-kicks, and made two futile attempth to open the score. For some time Heathside kept up the attack, but gradually Parochial gained the .upper hand. Through a misunderstanding on the parb of two Heathside players as to who should take the ball, Parochial was given a fine opportunity to score. They took it and a try followed. This was con- verted and the Parochial lads were given a five point lead, which they maintained until the close of the game. The chief reason why Parochial won the match was not so much that they were the superior team, but because they took their opportunities, whilst Heathside let them slide. Time after time, when they had practically a clear field before them, they would be pulled up by the referee for knocking on, or for forward passing. It is to be regretted that during the match several unsportsmanlike incidents occurred. Rough play started as soon as the game opened and it was only controlled by the strong action of Mr. J. Bibby, who was refereeing, sending a couple of men off the field before the game was very old. Later, however, he had to' send a couple more off. These incidents marred what otherwise would have been the best match of the season. • The Church-street lads were at full strength against the scratch team of Heathside, but both teams put up a good game. Heathside were short of their regular full-back, as young Griffiths was very seriously injured the previous week and unable to play. This youth was greatly missed. as it is said he is one of the best full-backs in the League, and would be sure of , his place on any Warrington junior team. For the Parochial side Stretford, Gibbs and Brown played a rattling good game, while Lingard of Heathside came to the forefront with his fine turn of speed and tricks. He got through any amount of good work, which un- fortunately was all thrown away through great lack of determination in the forward line. For the losers Lingard. Booth, Rutter and the half- backs played excellent games. The result Was :—Parochial, 5 points; Heath- side, nil.

SACRED HEARTS' SMART WIN.

Such did not prove to be the ease, hOtvever, for after a ding-dong struggle, in which Sacred Heart had slightly the better of the struggle, the latter 'team ran out winners by six points to five. First one and then the other team was on-the attack,. but the defence of the i-espective teams proved very good.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT

LEAGUE.

PADDINGTON v. HOLLINS GREEN.

- - - - At this return fixture .at Paddington there was a fair attendance of spectators, most of whom came with the visiting team. The fol- lowing players turned out, under the control Of Referee J. Wright : Paddington.: Horrocks; Holmes and Holden; Taylor, Wrench and Sherratt; Greenwood, Hardman, Bothwell, Darbyshire and Barlow- Hollins Green: Blundell; Clarke and 'Mill- ington; Buckley, Etchells and Clewo.rth; Tay- lor,, Green, Muskett, Mellor and Nelson. The home contain won the toss and defended the bottom goal. Muskett set the ball rolling ' and the visitors at once got into the home quarters, where Holden relieved splendidly. The visit Ors returned to the attack, and Taylor shot past the wrong side, of the upright. The visiting team continued to have the best, of the exchanges, and after a fruitless corner, play was transferred to the visitors' end by Hardman and Greenwood, and good tackling by Millington *cleared the danger away. Fol- lowing even exchanges in midfield the visitors' forwards got in front_ Of the home goal and Muskett opened the score after fifteen minutes' play. After several % offside decisions against the visitors the home forwards became more aggressive, Hardman and Greenwood being most effective.. Hardman beat Millington for the ball* and Made the scores equal with a good shot. After a couple of fruitless corner- kicks to the home team, Blundell made a good save from Rothwell, and play was quickly transferred to the home end, where Muskett gained the lead by a good individual effort. Half-time: Hollins' Green, 2 goals; Paddington,

1 goal. ... On •resuming, the visitors were the first to attack, and after.Etchells had shot wide Mus- kett tested Horrocks with a great drive, which was successfully dealt with. After a 'good return by Millington, Green *placed MuSkett in 'possession and he scored the third goal. .After this the defence always proved tee good for the attack. and at the call of time the result was: Hollins Green, 3 goals;` Paddington, 1 goal.

FRIENDLY MATCHES.

STOKE RESERVES v. WARRINGTON ASSOCIATION. On the Victoria Ground this return fixture' was played Under ideal conditions. Stoke Reserves: Dennis; Bentley. and Brooke; .Burton, James and Follows; Biddulph, Greaves, Smith; Fitchford and Gunner For- rester. , • Warrington: Brindle; Glover and .Leah; Millington, Haywood and Ditchfield; Midge, T. Whitfield, Aspinall, Foster and Millward. Referee, Mr. F. Whitter, of Leek. The home team won the toss and the open- ing exchanges were in their favour, Forrester placing behind. The visitors then had a turn, and Aspinall, getting in a dangerous position, was. neatly. robbed by James. Play was trans- ferred to the visitors' end, and Brindle had to stive from Forrester, and a little later Fitch- ford was unfortunate with a splendid shot which ht the crossbar and rebounded into play. At. this juncture the visitors' goal was hotly bombarded, and Brindle made a brilliant save with a shot from Pitchford, but at the end of ten minutes' play Smith opened the score with a. hard drive, Brindle having no chance to save. It was only on rare occasions :that the visitors got to close Quarters with the home defence, and in one of these raids, led by Illidge, Dennis misfielded his centre and Aspinall was too far behind to take advantage of the opening and Bentley cleared. From a well-placed flag-kick by Biddulph, Smith headed in, when Brindle effected a ,good save just inside the Post. Whitfield forced a fruit- less corner, and then Aspinall was badly fouled by Bentley. From the free-kick Haywood skimmed the bar with a fast drive. From the goal-kick the, home team were scion in front of the visitors' goal again, and Greaves was just too late to accept a. grand centre by For- rester, and then Brindle again saved well at close range from Smith. The game Continued to be contested in the viAtors' half, and Burton was fouled by Leah. The free-kick was well,, placed ,by Greaves, and although Brindle handled the ball, he failed to complete the clearance, when Smith dashed in and netted the ball. half-time: Stoke Reserves, 2 goals; Warrington, nil. The home team were early on the aggressive in the second half, and Forrester, by a splendid individual effort, Scored the third goal. From the centre-kick the visitors were able to attack by a good run on the left by Millward, and from his centre Dennis had to exert himself to clear. Smith, gaining possession of the leather, transferred to. Greaves, who forced Brindle to concede a. corner-kick. Pitchford adding the-fourth goal. After a spell of even play in mid-field, and then a visit to the home goal. Smith broke away, and, getting past the, backs, Brindle coming out to meet him, he had not much difficulty in netting the fifth goal. Other goals•were.scored by Greaves and Forrester before the .call of time, when the result was :—Stoke Reserves, 7 goals; Warring- ton, nil.

ASSOCIATION.

Last Saturday the Paddington and Hollins I Green teams met in their return fixture, and played under favourable conditions overhead, but the ground was in too rough a condition to allow much science in the passing game. Both teams were at fault in holding on to, the ball too much. Had they adopted the long, swing- ing methods, a much keener game would have been seen. On the general run of the game the visitors deserved their success. Their forwards, when near goal, were alwayt the more danger- ous, and although Muskett was closely watched he scored all the goals for the visitors. The ' home team never appeared to settle down to their game, Hardman and Greenwood being their most dangerous forwards. Rothwell, although 'working hard, did not appear able to break away from the attentions of • Mellor. Sherratt was a diligent and quiet worker in the

half-back line. The visitors' supporters were greatly delighted with the result of the match, for the Hollins Green team now have the satis- faction of taking four points from the Padding- tonians, and they appear to have a good chance of retaining their position at the head of the League Table. UP to date they have not suffered defeat in this season's games.

Garnett and Marsh were notable absentees from the Paddington team. Millington, late of Queen's Park hangers, has proved a great catch for the, Hollins Green Club. Throughout the game he could often be 'heard' giving advice to his colleagues, which, if followed, while ,the club can avail themselves of his assistance, should prove of great value to the younger mem- bers of the team.

The Warrington Association team paid A visit to the Potteries to play their return fix- ture with the Stoke Reserves with whom, it will be remembered, a goalless game was contested a few weeks ago on the Hood-lane ground. But on this occasion Stoke was represented by a much stronger eleven, only four players of th,e former team retaining their places, namely, Dennis, Brooke, Bentley and Greaves. On the other hand the Warrington Club were not able to call on the services of Siddall and G. Whit- field. There was no doubt that the record of the visitors had travelled before them. Under the name of Great Sankey P.C., they had gone 1 through three seasons without suffering defeat, and as the reserve team of Stoke did not suc- ceed in lowering the Warrington colours in the first engagement, they did not intend to allow the second attempt to pass without success. So successful were the Stoke team that they managed to get the ball into the net on seven occasions, and preventing their visitors from scoring at all. Brindle was the outstanding player on the Warrington team, and had it not been for his splendid custodianship the score would have been much greater. In the second portion of the game he came in for _special at-

iention from the home forwards, and sustained minor injuries, but was fortunately able to con- tinue the game until the call of time.

After a long run of success such as the War- rington. teaM, has enjoyed, , they • will scarcely bo able to realise that they were defeated by seven clear goals in the first match away from,home this season.

In the Warrington and District Junior League there were again some disappointments with the fixtures. Owing to the withdrawal of the Bewsey ..klbions the Stockton Heath Scouts were unable to, make other arrangements, and had an idle day.

The Bewsey Athletic were again disappointed n their fixtures, on this occasion the offenders wing the Latchford Cadets who, owing to some rifling differences among the players, could not .aise a team, and their committee had to cancel he match.

The Fairfield United Reserves journeyed to Padgate to play the C.L.8., and they returned with an easy victory, scoring on twelve occasions, whilst the Lads had only responded once.

On the Arpley ground the leaders of the League got a fright in their engagement with the Mersey Juniors. It was only after a. hard and strenuous game that the " Saints " gained the verdict by the odd goal in three.

The .Juniors are to be congratulated on the splendid fight they made with St. Elphin's. There is no disgrace in being defeated by the odd goal, hut the result of .this'game should give them more encouragement in 'their remaining fixtures. •

Waq it the absence of Boyle that upset the calculations of the St. Elphin's team?

Mr. Simpson has resigned the secretaryship of the Padgate C.L.8.. and Mr. A. Profitt. The Vernals, Padgate, has been appointed to fill the racancy.

The Warrington' Central had -a Pleasant out- inv.: and a good game last week, when opposing Widnes St. Mary's. The result was a eonifort- able victory for the Central by six goals against two. The Central committee have been very fortunate .in securing the services of Smith, st. Burnler•Reserve player, who has come to work in the town, and his display of last week greatly strengthened their attack, and he had a good [. -share in the spoils of their match. Mr. G. .13amber was persimded to take the field again, and officiate as Kitight of the Whistle. Both

SANKEY ATHLETIC v. ELMWOIOD CONGREGATIONALISTS.

PlaTed on Elmwood ground. Woodhouse started for the home teak:, but they were soon repulsed by the visitors' half-backs. The Sankey forwards took the game in hand and kept up a constant pressure until a dead-ball brought relief. From a Aish along the home right they opened their account, the inside- left taking the perfect centre. The visitors' goalkeeper failed to hold the ball shot in, owing to the turf being very slippery, and a goal resulted. This roused the Athletic, whose forwards made great efforts to equalise, and , were rewarded when E. Taylor scored from ten yards' range. Half-time: Elmwood, 1; Sankey,

. . The game becitme very fast and many in- juries happened on both sides. Sankey con- tinued to hold the upper hand and were awarded a penalty after ten minutes' play. W. Taylor was entrusted with the kick, but he placed outside. However, he soon made amends, for after placini, to Kenyon on the extreme left,., he received' the ' ball again and scored a splendid goal. This Proved to be the only goal in the second half. Result: Sankey Athletic, 2 goals; Elmwood Congregationalists, 1 goal. AMBULANCE DRILL.

New Class To Be Formed At AVarringtou.

We have this week received a letter in the following terms : " You would be doing a great gatiee to num- bers of men who have signed the attestation forms for service in the R.A.M.C. if you would say where they can drill. They have no instruc- tions where to go for training instruction, as the ambulance class in Warrington was dropped when Dr. Law went to France."

Our correspondent is scarcely correct in bis statement. There have been four classes in Warrington ,since Dr. Law left for active ser- vice, Dr. Law and Dr. Manson having conducted them, and many men have thereby been added to the ranks of the R.A.M.C. But lately the Parade Room at the Police Station, where. the Brigade met and drilled, has been used by the workers under Lord Derby's recruiting scheme. Another class will be started in January, and if the Police Station is not available, the Cairo-street Schoolroom will be used, as it has already been used.

All information will be given most gladly by Superintendent Baldry, 125, Church-street, War-

c EXAMINER, SATUR DA V. DECEMBER 25) 1915.

WOUNDED WARRIORS.

FRODSHAM AUXILIARY MILITARY HOSPITAL.

More Beds Provided.

A new ward, to accommodate fifty patients, and providing rooms for the addttional burstaf• staff, erected at the above hospital, was opened by Colonel Gemmel, of the Western Division, last Saturday afternoon. The hospital now con- ;tains 120 beds.

The Colonel, addressing a large attendance of the friends of the hospital,, thanked the com- mittee, an behalf of the authorities, for their endeavour to aid in the work of caring for the wounded soldiers. When the urgent appeal came from the War Office for additional beds in that .area, he replied by the same evening's post that he ecuildarnange for an additional 1,106 beds. If all the counties had responded as liberally as Lancashire and Cheshire had done, there would have been a sufficiency for all the demands. The additional roams there were most satisfactory, and he was sure that the men who were sent to the hospital would have the best attention by the matron and,her willing staff of workers. Mr. J. J. Crosfield, proposing a vote of thhnks to Colonel Gemmel, said the committee were greatly indebted to him. At the opening of the hospital, he '(the speaker), asked for help for its maintenance, and as there had been such an ample response, the committee thought it was their duty to enlarge the hospital, and they had done what they thought was needful. They would wish him to extend to Colonel Gemmel a cordial invitation to visit the hospital in the summer, so that he could see for himself what bright sunshine and the needful care could do for wounded " Tommies." applause.)_

_ The vote was carried unanimously, and Colonel Gemmel reiterated his thanks for thP work achieved by the committee, and thanked Mr. Bower for his interest in the various details of the institution.

The committee expressed themselves quite satisfied with the newly-completed additions. The main structure was built by Messrs. J. G. Davies and Co.; the decorations had been carried out by Mr. John Barrow, and the store- rooms, &c., adjoining was the work of Messrs. R. Gleave and Son. The hospital, with its additions, is now able to accommodate 120 patients.

Miss Marie Houghton At Whiteeross.

On Saturday Miss Marie Houghton gave her sixth concert for the entertainment of the wounded soldiers at the Whitecross Military Ho:Vital, and a capital programme was pro- vided. Amongst the items contributed by Miss Houghton, in her usual finished style, was the song "Keep the home fires burning. which is becoming very popular, amongst the boys at home and abroad. Miss Doris Reid gave several pianoforte solos, which were greatly appreciated. Miss Cooler (contralto) was in good voice and heartily applauded. Mr. Pearse sang with great effect, and the humorous items provided by Mr. Charles Lambert were both interesting and en- tertaining. He was particularly successful in his coster selections.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0283.txt | included=true | included =====
DEATH OF REV. OWEN WATKINS.

Former Warrington Wesleyan Superintendent Passes Away.

The Rev. Owen Watkins, who was Wesleyan superintendent minister in Warrington in - the 'nineties, died on Wednesday after a few, weeks' illness. He was superintendent. of Chelsea Wesleyan Circuit, and father of the Senior Wesleyan Chaplain at the Front. Mr. Watkins was of Welsh descent, and was born at Worsley, in Manchester, in 1842. ,He was accepted for the ministry in,1863, and after some years in the home work a threat of con- sumption compelled residence in South Africa. In 1876 he was appointed by the Missionary Committee to the charge of Maritzburg in Natal. After three years' work there, during part of which he acted as Wesleyan chaplain to the British forces in the Zulu War, he was corn- missioned by the Missionary Committee in 1880 to form a new district and extend Wesleyan missions in the Transvaal. Unfortunately, the outbreak of thd Boer War delayed this work, and 'again Mr., Watkins served as chaplain to the Forces. When peace came he began his pioneer task, and as chairman and general superintendent of the Transvaal and Swaziland District from 1879=93 he organised and developed this large and flourishing branch of South African Missions. .In 1884-5 his unique know- ledge of native life caused his appointment as adviser on Baralong affairs to General Sir Charles Warren in the Bechuanaland Ex- pedition. In 1889, when the Chartered Company was formed, and the Colony of Rhodesia founded, Mr. Watkins was appointed by the IMissionary Committee to- establish a mission there and make arrangements with the com- pany. After carrying out this task Mr. Watkins made an arduous journey on foot to his home at Pretoria. A severe illness followed, which com- pelled his permanent return to the English work in 1893. At Warrington, Llandudno, Car- diff, Reading, Aldershot, Westminster and Chelsea he rendered able service. A short, sturdy man, with something of the Boer in his aspect, his stentorian voice was a familiar sound in the Conferences as he kept a watchful eye on the platform. He was a genial and lovable man in personal intercourse, modest about his life-work, and cherishing to the end a consum- ing love of Boer tobacco. He was in his 73rd year.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0284.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ORDERS OF OUR FRIENDS.

By W. Stewart Royston, in "Great Thoughts."

There are three Orders to which we belong and from which we choose our friends.. In the lowest of the Order we prefer .to have no choice because it consists of those people who are selfish in thought, word and deed, and who are spiteful. jealous, and who never have time nor desire to encourage or help anyone who try to do good. We do not desire to associate with them be- cause if we did their mental food would. be our poison! . The second Order consists of those we dearly love .and it is from this we choose our true friends, for we find them so thoughtful for us, so' kind, and ever ready and willing to help us with their cheery words and their silver if need be, though more often it is the first-named was most needed than the latter

It is from this Order we gather trust simply because we have trusted them, even though there are those who break ohr trust when it has been witnessed on a. scrap of paper! in this Order of friendship we find some of the most precious gems, much more valuable and helpful to us than those in the windows of the jeweller!. To be with them in mind and body is to be in their safe keeping! The third Order is. the. ideal, and we can only hope to reach by. imitating the beauty of good souls. Many of those in this Order are beyond anything we can say or do for them, but we can say theirs is the living influence within us, it is their past in our future hope, and they are far away from harm and need none of our help now, but we need' theirs!

If we give the matter one moment's thought. are there not some we know quite fit to he num- bered with those in our third Order of noble friends who are with us to-day? When they are in trouble we appeal to the King of our earthly kings, and when we are suffering their appeal goes, too, up for us!

_ Would not our life be less worth living if it were not for our friends?

WOMEN LIBERALS' SEWING CLASS.

Lady Crosfleld Appeals For Funds. Lady CroSfield, president of. the Women's Liberal Association, writes us to a' that:— "The sewing class of the Whrrington Women's Liberal Association is again in urgent need of -funds to enable it to continue the good work it - carried out .last year, when so many thousands of garments were sent out to our soldiers at the Front. How much this good Work adds to the comfort and helps to safeguard the health .of many hundreds of Warrington men now fighting for our country and Empire will be obvious- to every one, and I hope there- fore that -this appeal will meet with such generous and widspread .response as will enable its to equal and perhaps even exceed the results of our efforts of last winter. I. therefore feel sure you will give your kind And favourable con- sideration and .your support to sp deserving a cause."

"BETTY" DUE AT THE ROYAL COURT.

The New Musical

The managers of the Royal Court Theatre have secured. a strong attraction at their theatre for Boxing Night, and the following five night's, for "Betty "—whiCh is being pro- duced in Warrington for the first time—is a musical coanedy that has gained an excellent reputation both in London and the provinces. The story of "Betty" is as follom4:—Betty is a kitchenmaid in the employ of Gerard, Earl of Beverley, heir to the Dukedom of Crow- borough- Gerard's father, the Duke of Crow- borough, politely but firmly informs his son that unless he takes unto himself a wife the greater part of his allowance will be cut off and a very material alteration made in the

===== 0002601_19151225_art0287.txt | included=true | included =====
Play Appears At Warrington Next Week.

Duke's will. Gerard determines to obey at least one ition of his father's command, and sending for the kitchenrnaid, who had already given evidence of her charm and vocal talent, forthwith proposes to make her his wife, and Betty, who thought Gerard a perfect Prince Charming, modestly signifies her willingness to become a future Duchess. The ceremony takes place, and then Gerard has his revenge on both father and bride by refusing filial duty to the one and declining to make his home with the other. Betty is taken in hand by good-natured Lady Playne, who has something of a handful in her own rightful lord, and the young Countess blossoms forth into a very bewitching Society lady. So much so that,

AN ELABORATE SCENE IN THE PLAY.

Gerard (who had been unwillingly attracted by her sweet personality on the occasion of their wedding) comes to the conclusion that he was rejecting a good sporting chance of excep- tional domestic happiness, and as Betty, with womanly instinct, realised that only a very little stood between her and her heart's desire, it may be taken for granted that the taxi-cab ride on a beautiful warm night was fraught with potential bliss.

The cast for Monday next includes: Miss Dorothy Hersee, Miss Chris. Vincent, Miss Olivia Glynn, Miss Yvonne Betts. Miss Dorothy Vitgers, Mr. Ernest R. Hollway, Mr. Fred Randall, Mr. Haydn Scott, Mr. Fred Payne and Mr. Myles Clifton.

STORIES FROM THE TRENCHES.

"PUSH-AND-GO."

An Exciting " Game " In The Trenches.

Mr. Charles Nurse, 165, Bewsey-road, War- rington, who is well known at Bewsey-raiad Wesleyan Church, has this week received the following letter, dated December Bth, from his son, Rifleman Nurse, of the King's Royal Rifles: "It is about a week .since I wrote you, but the lapse is no fault of mine. Writing was made impossible owing to the conditions we were in. We came out of the trenches last night. They brought us to a fine barn, and I slept like a top. I received your parcel and also one from Bewsey=road Chapel. It contained cigarettes, chocolates, and a writing pad, all of which are very good and useful. We (his brother and himself) are both quite well, and in for a good time, for we are going to have at least a week's rest. The cake and mince pies were lovely, and we thoroughly enjoyed them. Wally and I have been despatch carriers while in the trenches this time, and it isn't half a game of push and go.' The officers would not have anyone else do this work only the two Nurses. It was exciting at times, as we had to run messages during the day. Nevertheless, we came off all right. Now I must close,' for we have to be washed and cleaned ready for dinner."

===== 0002601_19151225_art0289.txt | included=true | included =====
A WORD FROM THE BRINY.

Warrington Sailor Who Is Still "hobbling About On Top."

We have received the following characteristic British Jack Tar's letter this week : "My Dear Editor,— "Just a line to say I am still hobbling about on top. If you remember last time I wrote you I was on H.M.S. different clime to this. lam sorry to tell you, though, I am at present out of action—slight love tap on left elbow. Nothing to speak of; shall probably be On deck again, as they say, about October, the end of the Month. "Well, Sir, I suppose the people at home are wondering what is being done out here, seeing what appears to be to them but little progress is being made. Just a day or two out here would satisfy their curiosity I can tell you, for a More God-forsaken country than this would be hard to imagine. You see, Sir, the country itself Is full of natural defences, and we are climbing all the way, whilst the other fellow is looking down on you. If you want him you must climb to get him. Taken altogether, ho is a cleaner fighter than the despicable German, although he in- variably presents a pitiable spectacle when he is brought in prisoner. "We are waiting for the weather to cool a bit, for just at present the flies are terrible; in fact, you have to keep your food on the move,. or else they will have as big a share of it, as you yourself. And the sand! Oh! the sand! It's lovely. You are eating sand, drinking sand, and sleeping in sand! "Never, mind, sir, the victory will be worth all the more when it is all over; and I am just thinking I shall not forget to celebrate it when I get to dear old Warrington, should it be my luck.

"Hoping the dear old town is still keeping up its reputation, I remain, yours truly,

"A WARRINGTONIAN AFLOAT AND ASHORE."

===== 0002601_19151225_art0290.txt | included=true | included =====
NN MANAGER'S DEATH.

Weil-hnown Penketh Resident Passes

Away.

By the tragically sudden death of Mr. Edwin G. Medley, Sunnyside, Station-road, Pen- keth will lose one of those men who can least be spared from any community. Though apparently in good health on Sunday morning, towards noon he complained of feeling faint, and this feeling increased as time went on. Dr. Murray was called in, and later on Dr. Bowden was also summoned to his bedside, but despite all professional attention he quietly passed away at about 8.15 p.m., death being due to paralysis of the brain.

The deceased gentleman, who leaves a widow and three children to mourn their loss, was born in Liverpool 42 years ago. Educated at Rossall and Blair Lodge, he afterwards followed a business career with his father, Mr. Gerdld Medley. His residence in Penketh dates from the year 1901. From that time onwards he has placed himself at the public service in every good cause. A trqe sportsman and of exceptional character, he was ever ready to give a helping hand whereby individuality could possibly be de- veloped. At one time he held the rank of Captain in the local Volunteer Battalion. A keen tennis player and golfer, his services were requisitioned as captain of the Mersey Golf Club. As a staunch Churchman he was heart and soul in the Sankey Sunday School, filling the position of superintendent. Messrs. J. Cresfield's claimed his services as works manager, where his nevei failing tact and sound business qualities were thoroughly appreciated.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0291.txt | included=true | included =====
LOCAL AMUSEMENTS.

THE HIPPODROME.

A " Scotch " Week And A Splendid Programme.

The vaudeville menu this week at the Friars' Green house is ol an entertaining and bright nature, and one that is thoroughly enjoyable. One might say that it is a Scotch week, for there are many items on the programme to remind one of the land o' cakes and thistles.

Premier place is awarded to Duncan's Royal Scotch Collies, a " turn " which is delightful to both young and old. Trained to perfection, these educated canines compare very favourably with any animal performance at present on the music halls. Another item on the programme that met with a hearty reception was Jock McPherson, the Caledonian jester. in a charac- ter study entitled "The Shepherd." His im- personation of a Highland shepherd was both bright. and refreshing, and was capitally acted. The Musical McGregors, in their wonderful manipulation of xylophone and concertinas were also seen at their best, especially in their render- ing of "The Miserere," from " Trovatore." Worthy of note was Eugene Garadini, described as England's premier actor-musician, who de- lighted the audience with his protean dexterity, his impersonation of Listz being especially fine. One of the successes of the evening's entertain- ment was Harry Brown, coloured comedian, in his latest success, "Pack up all 'you've got," for his turn was both novel and refined. His efforts met with such success that he was re- called on numerous occasions. •

Ada Wright, in funny patter songs, and Jack Murray, comedian, together liith the Palace perfection pictures, conclude an excellent pro- gramme.

Holiday Programme.

The programme at the, Hippcdrome next week will be made up of a number of "turns," each of which is worthy of being -termed a "star turn." The Four Sisters Ruby i are direct from the London Palladium, and will present one of the Most charming and clever turns on the stage. Fine singing may be ex- pected from the .Four Shades, a ladies' vocal quartet, for they have gained a reputation in the vaudeville world. Arthur Vern& is a character vocal comedian of no mean ability; and George floss styles himself as a "topsy- turvy pianist and one-man music-hall." Two other excellent musical "turUs" are those Provided by Doraswami, the "man who makes a violin sound like an orchestra," and The Four Its—four refined young entertainers with their latest B:wigs and dances. But the great "tit-bit" of the programme should be the Lancashire comedy entitled "Listing far a Soldier," which is to be produced by J. J. Wild's Company.

FINE FILMS AT THE STAR

KINEMA.

"Excellent!" Such is a correct summing up of the programme presented at the popular Star Kinema House, Church-areet, this week. "The Second in Command," a fine five-part military drama, was the " star " picture. It was awarded the first prize at the Panama Exhibi- tion this year, in open competition against the leading film producers. Part sit of the "Adven- tures of Elaine," featuring "The Vampire," was another fine picture, and proved to be every bit as exciting as its predecessors. Scenes from the seat of war were depicted in the " Pathe Gazette," while a comedy entitled " Gertie's Joy Ride," fairly "brought the house down."

For the latter half of the week the premiere position will be held by that well-known picture "Antony and Cleopatra." Thel scenery and

costumes are super, the total cost of the film being f:40,000. Holiday Attractions.

A series of fine films will be shown at the Star 'Cinema next week. For the first three days "May Blossom" will be the " star " picture. and for the latter part of the week " Niobe " will occupy that position. Other good films will be shown. It is well that there are three shows daily at the Star. The times are 3, 7 and 9.

===== 0002601_19151225_art0293.txt | included=true | included =====
BOXING DAY AT THE OLYMPIA.

Big attractions have been hooked at the Olympia, Liverpool, .for Boxing Day, the chief of which is the Band of Ms Majesty's Cold stream Guards. One of the attractions is a troupe of artistes named the "Bogannys," who have Come specially from America • to . renew acquaintance with Olympia audiences; and are what Americans term a turn with "Pep." Cliff Berme, who is exhibiting his "Jack-Ass Family," claims .the credit of being the 'in- ventor of one of the best laugh-getting circus entertainments over presented. Rossi's Ele- phants deserve in their degree the description onc,e applied to an eminent body of ecclesiastic teachers—they are the "intellectual aristocracy" of the elephant world. These items, however„ are but a particle of the good things that will be at the Olympia on Boxing Day, and every- body who visits it will be assured of a good time. The commencing time is 2 p.m.
